The Alperin–McKay conjecture for the prime 2
Lucas Ruhstorfer

Abstract
. In this paper we consider the inductive Alperin–McKay condition for quasi-isolated 2 2 -blocks of exceptional groups of Lie type. Thereby, we complete the proof of the Alperin–McKay conjecture for the prime 2 2 .



2010 Mathematics Subject Classification
20C33

Introduction

Alperin–McKay conjecture

Let
\ell
be a prime and
b
an
\ell
-block of a finite group
G
with defect group
D
. We denote by
\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(G,b)
the set of height zero characters of
b
, i.e. the set of characters
\chi\in\operatorname{Irr}(G,b)
associated to the block
b
with
\chi(1)_{\ell}=|G:D|_{\ell}
. Then the Alperin–McKay conjecture
[
Alp76
]
from 1976 predicts the following:

Conjecture

(Alperin–McKay)
.

Let
b
be an
\ell
-block of
G
with defect group
D
and
B
its Brauer correspondent in
\mathrm{N}_{G}(D)
. Then

|\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(G,b)|=|\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(\mathrm{N}_{G}(D),B)|.

Our main objective in this paper is to prove this conjecture for the prime
2
.

Theorem A
.

The Alperin–McKay conjecture holds for the prime
\ell=2
.

Späth
[
Spä13
, Theorem C]
showed that the Alperin–McKay conjecture holds for the prime
\ell
if the so-called inductive Alperin–McKay (iAM) condition holds for all finite simple groups with respect to the prime
\ell
. Our proof of Theorem
A
therefore shows more precisely that the iAM condition holds for all finite simple groups with respect to the prime
2
.

Brauer’s height zero conjecture

This particular way of proving Alperin’s conjecture has important consequences for Brauer’s height zero (BHZ) conjecture
[
Bra56
]
from 1955 which states that if
b
is an
\ell
-block of
G
with defect group
D
, then
\operatorname{Irr}(G,b)=\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(G,b)
if and only if
b
is abelian. The ”if”-direction of this conjecture was fully established by Kessar–Malle
[
KM13
]
while on the other hand, Navarro–Späth
[
NS14
]
showed that the ”only-if” direction of BHZ would follow from a proof of the Alperin–McKay conjecture via the inductive conditions. As a consequence of our proof of Theorem
A
, we therefore also obtain Brauer’s height zero conjecture for the prime
2
.

Corollary B

(BHZ for the prime
2
)
.

Let
b
be a
2
-block of a finite group
G
with defect
group
D
. Then
\operatorname{Irr}(b)=\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(b)
if and only if
D
is abelian.

Our strategy towards the proof of Theorem
A
is now as follows.
By
[
BR21
]
the iAM-condition holds for all finite simple groups at the prime
\ell=2
except possibly for groups of exceptional Lie type defined over a field of odd characteristic. Moreover, by the main result of
[
Ruh22
]
it suffices to check the inductive condition only for the quasi-isolated blocks of these groups. Throughout the introduction,
\mathbf{G}
therefore denotes a simple simply connected linear algebraic group of
exceptional Lie type in characteristic
p\neq 2
with Frobenius endomorphism
F:\mathbf{G}\to\mathbf{G}
defining an
\mathbb{F}_{q}
-structure on
\mathbf{G}
and
G:=\mathbf{G}^{F}
its associated finite group of Lie type.

To finish the proof of Theorem
A
we therefore show the following:

Theorem C
.

Let
G
be an exceptional group of Lie type as above and
b
a quasi-isolated
2
-block of
G
. Then the block
b
satisfies the iAM-condition.

Jordan decomposition for quasi-isolated blocks

In a first step towards the proof of Theorem
C
we describe the structure of the quasi-isolated blocks of groups of exceptional type. For this recall that a quasi-isolated
2
-block is a block associated to a quasi-isolated semisimple element
s\in(\mathbf{G}^{\ast})^{F}
of odd order in the group
(\mathbf{G}^{\ast},F)
in duality with
(\mathbf{G},F)
. In
[
KM13
]
Malle–Kessar give a parametrization of these blocks in terms of quasi-central
e
-cuspidal pairs, where
e
denotes the order of
q
modulo
4
. More precisely, the set of
2
-blocks associated to the semisimple element
s
is in bijection with the set of
G
-conjugacy classes of quasi-central
e
-cuspidal pairs
(\mathbf{L},\lambda)
with
\lambda\in\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{L},s)
.

Motivated by the results of Enguehard
[
Eng08
]
and by using Malle–Kessar’s parametrization we construct a Jordan decomposition for quasi-isolated blocks of exceptional groups. Following
[
Eng08
]
we construct for this an
F
-stable subgroup
\mathbf{G}(s)\leq\mathbf{G}
in ”duality” with
\operatorname{C}_{\mathbf{G}^{\ast}}(s)
. From a philosophical point of view the representation theory of a block associated to
s
should be closely linked to the representation theory of a corresponding unipotent block of the group
G(s):=\mathbf{G}(s)^{F}
. Using Kessar–Malle’s parametrization and comparing it with the parametrization of the involved unipotent blocks we can now prove the following:

Proposition D
.

In the above setting, assume that
\mathrm{C}_{\mathbf{G}^{\ast}}(s)^{F}=\mathrm{C}^{\circ}_{\mathbf{G}^{\ast}}(s)^{F}
.

(a)

There exists a bijection between the set of
2
-blocks of
G
associated to
s
and the set of unipotent
2
-blocks of
G(s)
.

(b)

There exists a bijection
\mathcal{E}_{\ell}(G,s)\to\mathcal{E}_{\ell}(G(s),s)
which induces a bijection
\mathcal{J}:\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(G,b)\to\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(G(s),c)
, where the blocks
b
and
c
correspond under the bijection of (a).

(c)

Assume that
\mathrm{Out}(G)_{b}
is cyclic. Then there exists a bijection
\mathcal{J}
as in (b) which is
\mathrm{Aut}(G)_{G(s),c}
-equivariant.

Determination of defect groups

Our next aim is to relate the local structure of the blocks of
G
associated to
s
to the structure of the corresponding unipotent block of
G(s)
. In
[
KM13
]
the authors give a description of the defect group in terms of this
e
-cuspidal pair. Except for a few cases in type
E_{8}
all quasi-isolated
2
-blocks have maximal possible defect, i.e. their defect group has order
|\operatorname{C}_{(\mathbf{G}^{\ast})^{F}}(s)|_{2}
. For this reason, we call blocks associated to the semisimple element
s
with defect
|\operatorname{C}_{(\mathbf{G}^{\ast})^{F}}(s)|_{2}
maximal blocks. Building on Kessar–Malle’s description of defect groups we obtain the following result which shows that the local categories of Jordan correspondent blocks are related.

Proposition E
.

Let
b
be a maximal quasi-isolated
2
-block of
G
associated to the semisimple element
s
.

(a)

The defect group
P
is conjugate to a Sylow
2
-subgroup of
G(s)
.

(b)

Suppose that the centralizer of
s
is not of type
A_{2}(\pm q^{3})
or
{}^{3}D_{4}(q).3
. Then we have
\operatorname{N}_{G}(P,b_{P})=\operatorname{N}_{G(s)}(P)
.

As an interesting byproduct of the previous proposition we obtain the following theorem about Sylow
2
-subgroups, which appears to be new:

Theorem F
.

Let
\mathbf{H}
be a simple algebraic group of simply connected type
A_{n}
,
D_{2n+1}
or
E_{6}
with
n>1
and
F:\mathbf{H}\to\mathbf{H}
a Frobenius endomorphism defining an
\mathbb{F}_{q}
-structure for some odd prime power
q
. Then the Sylow
2
-subgroups of
\mathbf{H}^{F}
are Cabanes (see Definition
4.1
).

We also have a result for other groups
\mathbf{H}
with different root system but under an additional assumption on
q
, see Theorem
4.10
.

Verification of the inductive condition

After having described the structure of these blocks, we turn towards proving the inductive AM-condition. As this condition is vastly simpler for blocks
b
with cyclic stabilizer
\mathrm{Out}(G)_{b}
we will focus on those first. It turns out that most quasi-isolated blocks actually have such a cyclic stabilizer in the outer automorphism group. As a consequence of Proposition
D
and Proposition
E
we can therefore show that these blocks satisfy the iAM-condition.

After having dealt with blocks
b
with cyclic
\mathrm{Out}(G)_{b}
, we shift our attention to the remaining maximal blocks.
Any height zero character of such a block corresponds under the Jordan decomposition constructed above to a
2^{\prime}
-character of
G(s)
. Building on Malle–Späth’s
[
MS16
]
parametrization of
2^{\prime}
-characters via Harish-Chandra theory we are able to prove the following.

Theorem G
.

Any height zero character in
\mathcal{E}_{\ell}(G,s)\cap\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(G,b)
of a maximal block
b
lies in the Harish-Chandra series of a semisimple character in
\mathcal{E}_{\ell}(\mathbf{L}_{0},s)
.

Here,
\mathbf{L}_{0}
is a Levi subgroup dual to the minimal
1
-split Levi subgroup
\mathbf{L}_{0}^{\ast}
of
\mathbf{G}^{\ast}
which contains
s
, see Lemma
1.8
. The previous theorem yields in particular a parametrization of the height zero characters of maximal blocks in terms of Harish-Chandra series. Let
(\mathbf{L},\lambda)
be the
e
-cuspidal pair associated to the block
b
and
B
the unique block of
\operatorname{N}_{G}(\mathbf{L})
with
B^{G}=b
. We are able to parametrize the local characters
\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(\operatorname{N}_{G}(\mathbf{L}),B)
and combining this with the parametrization obtained from Theorem
G
we provide an iAM-bijection
\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(\operatorname{N}_{G}(\mathbf{L}),B)\to\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(G,b)
which shows that the remaining maximal blocks satisfy the inductive condition.

We are therefore left to consider non-maximal unipotent blocks. In this case, Enguehard
[
Eng00
]
has provided an explicit description of characters in these blocks. Through an explicit case-by-case analysis of these blocks we are then able to verify the iAM-condition for these blocks, thereby completing the proof of Theorem
C
and as a consequence the proof of Theorem
A
.

Structure of the paper

The organization of this paper is as follows.
In Section
1
we recall the Jordan decomposition for groups with connected center. In Section
2
we then introduce Kessar–Malle’s parametrization of
2
-blocks. Using their parametrization we are then able to provide a Jordan decomposition for quasi-isolated
2
-blocks of groups of exceptional type. In Section
3
we use results of Malle–Späth and determine the Harish-Chandra series of height zero characters of maximal blocks.

The next sections are then devoted to computing the local structure of quasi-isolated blocks. In Section
4
we show that many Sylow
2
-subgroups of group of Lie type have the so-called Cabanes property. In Sections
5
to
7
this is used to determine the structure of the defect groups of the quasi-isolated blocks. In Section
8
we analyze the action of automorphisms on quasi-isolated blocks and deduce that the Jordan decomposition in Section
2
can be chosen to be automorphism equivariant.

After having developed these structural results about quasi-isolated blocks we start discussing the inductive AM-condition. In Section
9
we recall a criterion for checking this condition and some simplifications which arise in our situation. This simplified criterion will allow us later in Section
13
to check the inductive condition for blocks with cyclic stabilizer in the outer automorphism group. In Sections
10
and
11
we then deal with the maximal blocks with non-cyclic stabilizer. This will in particular involve explicit calculations with the semisimple elements
s
of
E_{6}(q)
whose centralizer is of type
A_{2}(q)^{3}.3
. Finally we will deal in Section
13
with the remaining non-maximal (unipotent) blocks and then prove our main theorem in Section
14
.

1.
Jordan decomposition for groups with connected center

1.1.
Representation theory

For the representation theory of finite groups we mainly follow the notation in
[
KM13
, Section 2.1]
. Let us recall the most important notation from there. For this let
G
be a finite group,
\ell
a prime and
(K,\mathcal{O},k)
an
\ell
-modular system large enough for
G
. We denote by
\operatorname{Irr}(G)
the set of
K
-valued irreducible characters of
G
. By an
\ell
-block of
G
we will mean a primitive idempotent of
\operatorname{Z}(kG)
or
\operatorname{Z}(\mathcal{O}G)
. This defines a partition

\operatorname{Irr}(G)=\displaystyle\bigsqcup_{b}\operatorname{Irr}(G,b)

of the irreducible characters into their
\ell
-blocks. For
\chi\in\operatorname{Irr}(G,b)
let
\mathrm{bl}(\chi):=b_{G}(\chi):=b
.

A Brauer pair of
G
with respect to
\ell
is a pair
(Q,c)
with
Q
an
\ell
-subgroup of
G
and
c
an
\ell
-block of
\operatorname{C}_{G}(Q)
. The set of Brauer pairs has a structure of a
G
-poset by the order relation ”
\leq
”. A
Brauer pair
(Q,c)
is a
b
-Brauer pair if and only if
\mathrm{br}_{Q}(b)c=c
,
where

\mathrm{br}_{Q}:(kG)^{Q}\to k{\operatorname{C}_{G}(Q)}

denotes the Brauer homomorphism.

1.2.
Groups of Lie type

Let
\mathbf{G}
be a connected reductive linear algebraic group with Frobenius endomorphism
F:\mathbf{G}\to\mathbf{G}
defined over
\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
for some prime
p\neq\ell
. Given such a group
\mathbf{G}
it is often convenient to consider it as a closed normal subgroup of a group whose center is connected. Therefore, we fix a regular embedding
\iota:\mathbf{G}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{\tilde{G}}
of
\mathbf{G}
as in
[
CE04
, Section 15.1]
and we identify
\mathbf{G}
with its image in
\mathbf{\tilde{G}}
. For any closed subgroup
\mathbf{M}
of
\mathbf{G}
we define
\tilde{\mathbf{M}}:=\mathbf{M}\mathrm{Z}(\tilde{\mathbf{G}})
. Moreover, if
\mathbf{H}
is any closed
F
-stable subgroup of
\mathbf{\tilde{G}}
then we denote by
H:=\mathbf{H}^{F}
its subset of
F
-stable points.

In addition, we fix a group
(\mathbf{G}^{\ast},F^{\ast})
in duality with
(\mathbf{G},F)
as in
[
DM91
, Definition 13.10]
and we denote the Frobenius endomorphism
F^{\ast}
with the same letter
F
if no confusion can arise. We also extend our notation for fixed points to the dual group, that is we write
H:=\mathbf{H}^{F^{\ast}}
for any
F^{\ast}
-stable subgroup
\mathbf{H}
of
\mathbf{G}^{\ast}
. The duality betwen
(\mathbf{G},F)
and
(\mathbf{G}^{\ast},F)
extends to a duality between the
G
-conjugacy classes of
F
-stable Levi subgroups of
\mathbf{G}
and the
G^{\ast}
-conjugacy classes of
F
-stable Levi subgroups of
\mathbf{G}^{\ast}
. Hence, if
\mathbf{L}
is an
F
-stable Levi subgroup of
\mathbf{G}
, then
\mathbf{L}^{\ast}
denotes an
F
-stable Levi subgroup of
\mathbf{G}^{\ast}
in duality with it.

1.3.
Character theory

We now recall the character theory of the finite groups
G=\mathbf{G}^{F}
. By a fundamental result of Lusztig, the set of irreducible characters
\operatorname{Irr}(G)
is a disjoint union of rational Lusztig series
\mathcal{E}(G,s)
, where
s
runs over semisimple elements of
G^{\ast}
up to conjugation. If
\mathbf{L}
is an
F
-stable subgroup of
\mathbf{G}
of a (non-necessarily
F
-stable) parabolic subgroup
\mathbf{P}
of
\mathbf{G}
then we have a map

R_{\mathbf{L}\subset\mathbf{P}}^{\mathbf{G}}:\mathbb{Z}\operatorname{Irr}(\mathbf{L}^{F})\to\mathbb{Z}\operatorname{Irr}(\mathbf{G}^{F})

called Lusztig induction. This map possibly depends on the choice of the parabolic subgroup used in its definition but in our case it will not, see the remarks following
[
KM13
, Theorem 2.8]
. We will therefore write
R_{L}^{G}
instead of
R_{\mathbf{L}\subset\mathbf{P}}^{\mathbf{G}}
in the following. An important consequence of the definition of Lusztig series (see
[
KM13
, Theorem 2.8(b)]
) is that for any semsimple element
s\in L^{\ast}
the map
R_{L}^{G}
restricts to a map

R_{L}^{G}:\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{E}(L,s)\to\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{E}(G,s).

1.4.
Jordan decomposition

We recall the following uniqueness statement about Jordan decomposition for groups with connected center by Digne–Michel. The explicit form of this theorem is taken from
[
GM20
, Theorem 4.7.1]
.

Theorem 1.1
.

There exists a unique collection of bijections

J_{G,s}:\mathcal{E}(G,s)\to\mathcal{E}(\operatorname{C}_{G^{\ast}}(s),1)

where
\mathbf{G}
runs over connected reductive groups with connected center and with Frobenius
map
F
, and
s\in(\mathbf{G}^{\ast})^{F}
is semisimple, satisfying the following, where we write
\mathbf{H}:=\operatorname{C}_{\mathbf{G}^{\ast}}(s)
:

(1)

For any
F^{\ast}
-stable maximal torus
\mathbf{T}^{\ast}\leq\mathbf{H}
,

\langle R^{\mathbf{G}}_{\mathbf{T}^{\ast}}(s),\rho\rangle=\varepsilon_{G}\varepsilon_{H}\langle R^{\mathbf{H}}_{\mathbf{T}^{\ast}}(1_{\mathbf{T}^{\ast}}),J_{G,s}(\rho)\rangle\text{ for all }\rho\in\mathcal{E}(G,s).

(2)

If
s=1
and
\rho\in\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{G}^{F},1)
is unipotent then

(a)

the Frobenius eigenvalues
\omega_{\rho}
and
\omega_{J_{G,1}}(\rho)
are equal, and

(b)

if
\rho
lies in the principal series then
\rho
and
J_{G,1}(\rho)
correspond to the same
character of the Iwahori-Hecke algebra.

(3)

If
z\in\operatorname{Z}(G^{\ast})
then
J_{G,{sz}}(\rho\otimes\hat{z})=J_{G,{s}}(\rho)
for
\rho\in\mathcal{E}(G,s)

(4)

For any
F
-stable Levi subgroup
\mathbf{L}^{\ast}
of
\mathbf{G}^{\ast}
such that
\mathbf{H}\leq\mathbf{L}^{\ast}
, with dual
\mathbf{L}\leq\mathbf{G}
,
the following diagram commutes:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{E}(G,s)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{E}({H},1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{E}(L,s)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{E}(H,1)}
\mathrm{id}
J_{G,s}
J_{L,s}
R_{{\mathbf{L}}}^{{\mathbf{G}}}

(5)

If
\mathbf{G}
is of type
E_{8}
and
\mathbf{H}
is of type
E_{7}A_{1}
(resp.
E_{6}A_{2}
) and
\mathbf{L}\leq\mathbf{G}
is a Levi
subgroup of type
E_{7}
(resp.
E_{6}
) with dual
\mathbf{L}^{\ast}\leq H
then the following diagram
commutes:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{E}(G,s)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{E}({H},1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{E}(L,s)_{c}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{E}(L^{\ast},1)_{c}}
R_{\mathbf{L}^{\ast}}^{\mathbf{H}}
J_{G,s}
J_{L,s}
R_{{\mathbf{L}}}^{{\mathbf{G}}}

where the index
c
denotes the subspace spanned by the cuspidal part of the
corresponding Lusztig series.

(6)

For any
F
-stable central torus
\mathbf{T}_{1}\leq\operatorname{Z}(\mathbf{G})
with corresponding natural epimorphism

\phi:\mathbf{G}\to\mathbf{G}_{1}:=\mathbf{G}/\mathbf{T}_{1}
and for
s_{1}\in\mathbf{G}^{\ast}_{1}

with
s=\phi^{\ast}(s_{1})
the following
diagram commutes:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{E}(G,s)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{E}({H},1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{E}(G_{1},s_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{E}(H_{1},1)}
J_{G,s}
J_{G_{1},s_{1}}

where the vertical maps are just the inflation map along

\mathbf{G}^{F}\to\mathbf{G}_{1}^{F}

and the restriction along the embedding
\mathbf{H}^{F}_{1}\to\mathbf{H}^{F}
respectively.

(7)

If
\mathbf{G}
is a direct product

\mathbf{G}=\prod_{i}\mathbf{G}_{i}
of
F
-stable subgroups
\mathbf{G}_{i}
then
J_{G,{\prod_{i}s_{i}}}=\prod_{i}J_{G_{i},s_{i}}
.

In the following, we denote by
\mathcal{Z}_{F}
the subset of
F
-covariants of the quotient group
\mathcal{Z}:=\operatorname{Z}(\mathbf{G})/\operatorname{Z}(\mathbf{G})^{\circ}
. Let
\mathcal{L}:\mathbf{G}\to\mathbf{G},g\mapsto g^{-1}F(g),
denote the Lang map. Then
\mathcal{Z}_{F}
acts on
\operatorname{Irr}(\mathbf{G}^{F})
via the isomorphism
\mathcal{L}^{-1}(\operatorname{Z}(\mathbf{G}))/\operatorname{Z}(\mathbf{G})\mathbf{G}^{F}\cong\mathcal{Z}_{F}
induced by the Lang map.

Moreover, for a semisimple element
s\in(\mathbf{G}^{\ast})^{F}
we denote by

A_{\mathbf{G}^{\ast}}(s)
(or
A(s)
if the ambient group is clear from the context) the component group of its centralizer
\operatorname{C}_{\mathbf{G}^{\ast}}(s)
.

Let
\sigma:\mathbf{G}\to\mathbf{G}
be a bijective morphism commuting with the action of
F
. Then there exists a dual morphism
\sigma^{\ast}:\mathbf{G}^{\ast}\to\mathbf{G}^{\ast}
which commutes with
F^{\ast}
as in
[
CS13
, Definition 2.1]
. This dual morphism is unique up to automorphisms induced by the action of elements of
\mathcal{L}_{F^{\ast}}^{-1}(\operatorname{Z}(\mathbf{G}^{\ast}))
.

Theorem 1.2
.

Let
\mathbf{G}
be a connected reductive group with connected center and
\sigma:\mathbf{G}\to\mathbf{G}
a bijective morphism commuting with
F
. Then for
s\in(\mathbf{G}^{\ast})^{F}
the bijection
J_{s}:\mathcal{E}(G,s)\to\mathcal{E}(\operatorname{C}_{\mathbf{G}^{\ast}}(s),1)
from Theorem
1.1
satisfies
J_{s}(\lambda)^{\sigma}=J_{\sigma^{\ast}(s)}(\sigma^{\ast}(\lambda))
for all
\lambda\in\mathcal{E}(G,s)
.

Proof.

This is
[
CS13
, Theorem 3.1]
.
∎

Example 1.3
.

Keep the notation and assumptions of Theorem
1.2
. Assume that
\sigma
stabilizes all rational components of
\mathbf{H}:=\operatorname{C}_{\mathbf{G}^{\ast}}(s)
. The action of automorphisms on
\mathcal{E}(H,1)
can be read off from
[
Mal08
, Theorem 2.5]
. In particular, if
\mathbf{H}
has no rational component with the same type as the groups appearing in
[
Mal08
, Theorem 2.5]
, then
\sigma^{\ast}
acts trivially on
\mathcal{E}(H,1)
and hence
\sigma
acts trivally on
\mathcal{E}(G,s)
by Theorem
1.2
.

The following theorem is probably well-known but since we will need parts of its proof later we decided to include its proof as well.

Theorem 1.4
.

Let
\mathbf{G}
be a connected reductive group with Frobenius endomorphism
F
and assume that
\mathcal{Z}_{F}
is cyclic. Then for every semisimple element
s\in(\mathbf{G}^{\ast})^{F}
the following holds.

(a)

There exists a bijection
\overline{J}_{s}:\mathcal{E}(G,s)/\mathcal{Z}_{F}\to\mathcal{E}(\operatorname{C}^{\circ}_{\mathbf{G}^{\ast}}(s),1)/A_{G^{\ast}}(s)

on orbits which are of the same size for corresponding characters.

(b)

Let
\sigma:\mathbf{G}\to\mathbf{G}
be a bijective morphism commuting with
F
. Then for every
\lambda\in\mathcal{E}(G,s)/\mathcal{Z}_{F}
we have
{}^{\sigma^{\ast}}\overline{J}_{s}(\overline{\lambda})=\overline{J}_{\sigma^{\ast}(s)}(\overline{\lambda}^{\sigma})
.

(c)

There exists a bijection
J_{s}:\mathcal{E}(G,s)\to\mathcal{E}(\operatorname{C}_{G^{\ast}}(s),1)
lifting the bijection
\overline{J}_{s}
which for
\chi\in\mathcal{E}(G,s)
satisfies

\chi(1)=|G^{\ast}:\operatorname{C}_{G^{\ast}}(s)|_{p^{\prime}}J_{s}(\chi)(1).

Proof.

When the center
\operatorname{Z}(\mathbf{G})
is connected, it follows that
\operatorname{C}_{\mathbf{G}^{\ast}}(s)
is connected as well and the existence of such a bijection follows from
[
GM20
, Corollary 2.6.6]
.

In the general case one first considers a regular embedding
\mathbf{G}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{\tilde{G}}
and by Theorem
1.1
one obtains a bijection
\mathcal{E}(\tilde{G},\tilde{s})\to\mathcal{E}(\operatorname{C}_{\mathbf{\tilde{G}}^{\ast}}(\tilde{s}),1)
. The dual map
\iota^{\ast}:\mathbf{\tilde{G}}^{\ast}\to\mathbf{G}^{\ast}
induces a surjective map
\operatorname{C}_{\tilde{\mathbf{G}}}(\tilde{s})\to\operatorname{C}^{\circ}_{\mathbf{G}}(s)
with kernel
\operatorname{Z}(\tilde{\mathbf{G}}^{\ast})
, see
[
Bon05
, Equation (2.2)]
and therefore a bijection
\mathcal{E}(\operatorname{C}_{\tilde{G}}(\tilde{s}),1)\to\mathcal{E}(\operatorname{C}_{G}(s),1)
between unipotent characters. By Clifford theory this leads to a bijection

\mathcal{E}(G,s)/\mathcal{Z}_{F}\to\mathcal{E}(\operatorname{C}^{\circ}_{\mathbf{G}^{\ast}}(s),1)/A_{G^{\ast}}(s)

on orbits which are of the same size for corresponding characters which shows part (a). Part (b) is now a consequence of Theorem
1.2
.

Lifting the bijection between orbits from part (b) (see also
[
CE04
, Problem 15.3]
) yields a bijection

J_{s}:\mathcal{E}(G,s)\to\mathcal{E}(\operatorname{C}_{G^{\ast}}(s),1)
.
By Clifford theory, the degrees of these characters can be solely read off from the orbit lengths.
Indeed, let
\tilde{\chi}\in\mathcal{E}(\tilde{G},\tilde{s})
and
\chi\in\mathcal{E}(G,s)
below
\tilde{\chi}
with Jordan correspondents
\tilde{\psi}
and
\psi
respectively, so that
\tilde{\chi}(1)=|\tilde{G}:\operatorname{C}_{\tilde{G}}(\tilde{s})|_{p^{\prime}}\tilde{\psi}(1)
. Let
\mathcal{O}
be the
A(s)
-orbit of the character
\tilde{\psi}\in\mathcal{E}(\operatorname{C}_{\tilde{G}^{\ast}}(s),1)
. Then
\chi(1)=\tilde{\chi}(1)/|\mathcal{O}|
and
\psi(1)=\tilde{\psi}(1)|A(s)|/|\mathcal{O}|
. Moreover, we have
|\tilde{G}/G|=|\operatorname{Z}(\tilde{G})|=|\operatorname{C}_{\tilde{G}}(\tilde{s})|/|\operatorname{C}_{G}^{\circ}(s)|
. Putting these equations together yields
\chi(1)=|G:\operatorname{C}_{G^{\ast}}(s)|_{p^{\prime}}\psi(1)
as desired.
∎

Remark 1.5
.

Keep the notation of Theorem
1.4
.
Assume that
\iota:\mathbf{G}\hookrightarrow\hat{\mathbf{G}}
is a closed immersion with
\iota([\mathbf{G},\mathbf{G}])=[\hat{\mathbf{G}},\hat{\mathbf{G}}]
and identify
\mathbf{G}
with its image
\iota(\mathbf{G})
. The map
\iota
induces by duality a map
\iota^{\ast}:\hat{\mathbf{G}}^{\ast}\to\hat{\mathbf{G}}
and we assume that
\operatorname{C}_{\hat{\mathbf{G}}^{\ast}}(\hat{s})^{F}=\operatorname{C}^{\circ}_{\hat{\mathbf{G}}^{\ast}}(\hat{s})^{F}
for some
\hat{s}\in(\hat{\mathbf{G}}^{\ast})^{F}
with
\iota^{\ast}(\hat{s})=s
. A special case of this is when
\hat{\mathbf{G}}
has connected center, so that the centralizer of any semisimple element in
\hat{\mathbf{G}}^{\ast}
is connected, see
[
CE04
, Section 15]
. Nevertheless, the constructions in
[
CE04
, Section 15]
also work in our slightly more general situation and we obtain that the
(\operatorname{Z}(\mathbf{G})/\operatorname{Z}(\mathbf{G})^{\circ})_{F}
-orbit of a character in
\mathcal{E}(G,s)
is the same as its
\hat{\mathbf{G}}^{F}
-orbit.

Let
\mathbf{H}
be a (not necessarily connected) reductive group and
\mathbf{T}
a maximal torus of
\mathbf{H}^{\circ}
. Then we denote
W_{\mathbf{H}}(\mathbf{T}):=W(\mathbf{H},\mathbf{T}):=\operatorname{N}_{\mathbf{H}}(\mathbf{T})/\mathbf{T}
and we let
p_{\mathbf{T}}:\operatorname{N}_{\mathbf{H}}(\mathbf{T})\to W_{\mathbf{H}}(\mathbf{T})
be the natural surjection. In addition, if
\mathbf{M}
is a closed subgroup of
\mathbf{H}
containing a maximal
torus
\mathbf{T}
of
\mathbf{H}
, then
W(\mathbf{H}\mid\mathbf{M},\mathbf{T})
is as in
[
CE99
, Notation 1.8]
. Namely,
W(\mathbf{H}\mid\mathbf{M},\mathbf{T})
is the subgroup of
W(\mathbf{H},\mathbf{T})
generated
by involutions associated with the roots of
\mathbf{H}
relative to
\mathbf{T}
that are
orthogonal to all the roots of
\mathbf{M}
.

Let
\mathbf{G}
be a connected reductive group. For a semisimple element
s\in\mathbf{G}^{\ast}
whose
\mathbf{G}^{\ast}
-conjugacy class is
F
-stable we denote by
\tilde{\mathcal{E}}(\mathbf{G},s)
its geometric Lusztig series, see
[
DM91
, Definition 13.16]
. Moreover, if the
F
-stable maximal tours
\mathbf{T}
of
\mathbf{G}
is in duality with the maximal
F
-stable torus
\mathbf{T}^{\ast}
of
\mathbf{G}^{\ast}
, then we denote by
{}^{\ast}:W(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{T})\to W(\mathbf{G}^{\ast},\mathbf{T}^{\ast})
the anti-isomorphism induced by duality, see the remarks after
[
DM91
, Definition 13.10]
. The following proposition will be used frequently all the time.

Proposition 1.6
.

Let
\mathbf{T}\subset\mathbf{L}\subset\mathbf{G}
and
\mathbf{T}^{\ast}\subset\mathbf{L}^{\ast}\subset\mathbf{G}^{\ast}
be in duality
with
\mathbf{L}
an
F
-stable Levi subgroup of
\mathbf{G}
and
\mathbf{T}
an
F
-stable maximal torus of
\mathbf{L}
. Let
s\in\mathbf{T}^{\ast}
such that its
\mathbf{L}^{\ast}
-conjugacy class is
F
-stable.

(a)

There is a natural isomorphism

\operatorname{N}_{\mathbf{G}^{F}}(\mathbf{L})/\mathbf{L}^{F}\to(\operatorname{N}_{W(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{T})}(W(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{T}))/W(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{T}))^{F},\quad x\mathbf{L}^{F}\mapsto n(x)W(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{T}),

for
x\in\operatorname{N}_{\mathbf{G}^{F}}(\mathbf{L})
with
{}^{x}\mathbf{T}={}^{l}\mathbf{T}
for
l\in\mathbf{L}
and
n(x):=l^{-1}x
.

(b)

For the geometric Lusztig series, we have
{}^{x}\tilde{\mathcal{E}}(\mathbf{L},s)=\tilde{\mathcal{E}}(\mathbf{L},{}^{n(x)^{\ast}}s)
.

(c)

The map from part (a) induces an isomorphism from
\operatorname{N}_{\mathbf{G}^{F}}(\mathbf{L},\tilde{\mathcal{E}}(\mathbf{L},s))/\mathbf{L}^{F}
onto a subgroup of
(\operatorname{N}_{W(\operatorname{C}_{\mathbf{G}^{\ast}}(s),\mathbf{T}^{\ast})}(W(\operatorname{C}^{\circ}_{\mathbf{L}^{\ast}}(s),\mathbf{T}^{\ast}))/W(\operatorname{C}_{\mathbf{L}^{\ast}}(s),\mathbf{T}^{\ast}))^{F}
.

(d)

If
x\in\operatorname{N}_{\mathbf{G}^{F}}(\mathbf{L})\cap p_{\mathbf{T}^{-1}}(W(C_{\mathbf{G}^{\ast}}^{\circ}(s)\mid\operatorname{C}^{\circ}_{\mathbf{L}^{\ast}}(s),\mathbf{T}^{\ast}))
and
s
have coprime orders, then
x
fixes every element of
\tilde{\mathcal{E}}(\mathbf{L}^{F},s)
.

Proof.

See
[
CE99
, Proposition 1.9]
.
∎

Remark 1.7
.

Let
\lambda\in\tilde{\mathcal{E}}(\mathbf{L}^{F},s)
where
s\in(\mathbf{L}^{\ast})^{F^{\ast}}
is of
\ell^{\prime}
-order. Then by Proposition
1.6
(c) the group
W_{\mathbf{G}^{F}}(\mathbf{L},\lambda):=\operatorname{N}_{\mathbf{G}^{F}}(\mathbf{L},\lambda)/\mathbf{L}^{F}
is isomorphic to a subgroup of

(\operatorname{N}_{W(\operatorname{C}_{\mathbf{G}^{\ast}}(s),\mathbf{T}^{\ast})}(W(\operatorname{C}^{\circ}_{\mathbf{L}^{\ast}}(s),\mathbf{T}^{\ast}))/W(\operatorname{C}_{\mathbf{L}^{\ast}}(s),\mathbf{T}^{\ast}))^{F}.

Moreover, according to part (d) a Sylow
\ell
-subgroup of
W_{\mathbf{G}^{F}}(\mathbf{L},\lambda)
is isomorphic to a Sylow
\ell
-subgroup of
(\operatorname{N}_{W(\operatorname{C}_{\mathbf{G}^{\ast}}(s),\mathbf{T}^{\ast})}(W(\operatorname{C}^{\circ}_{\mathbf{L}^{\ast}}(s),\mathbf{T}^{\ast}))/W(\operatorname{C}_{\mathbf{L}^{\ast}}(s),\mathbf{T}^{\ast}))^{F}
.

For instance if
\operatorname{C}_{\mathbf{L}^{\ast}}^{\circ}(s)
is a maximal torus of
\operatorname{C}^{\circ}_{\mathbf{G}^{\ast}}(s)
then a Sylow
\ell
-subgroup of
W_{\mathbf{G}^{F}}(\mathbf{L},\lambda)
is isomorphic to a Sylow
\ell
-subgroup of
W(\operatorname{C}_{\mathbf{G}^{\ast}}(s),\operatorname{C}^{\circ}_{\mathbf{L}^{\ast}}(s))^{F^{\ast}}
.

1.5.
d
-tori and minimal Levi subgroups

An
F
-stable torus
\mathbf{T}
of
\mathbf{G}
is called a
d
-torus if there exists
a\geq 0
such that
|\mathbf{T}^{F^{k}}|=\Phi_{d}(q^{k})^{a}

for all
k\geq 1
with
(k,d)=1
, where
\Phi_{d}
denotes the
d
th cyclotomic polynomial. We denote by
\mathbf{T}_{\Phi_{d}}
the maximal
d
-subtorus of
\mathbf{T}
. Centralizers
\mathbf{L}
of
d
-tori
of
\mathbf{G}
are called
d
-split Levi subgroups. By construction, these satisfy
\mathbf{L}=\operatorname{C}_{\mathbf{G}}(\operatorname{Z}^{\circ}(\mathbf{L})_{\Phi_{d}})
, see
[
GM20
, Proposition 3.5.5]
.

Lemma 1.8
.

Let
s\in(\mathbf{G}^{\ast})^{F}
be a semisimple element and
d
some positive integer. Then for a Levi subgroup
\mathbf{L}^{\ast}
of
\mathbf{G}^{\ast}
the following are equivalent:

(i)

\mathbf{L}^{\ast}=\mathrm{C}_{\mathbf{G}^{\ast}}(\mathbf{T}_{0}^{\ast})
, where
\mathbf{T}_{0}^{\ast}
is a Sylow
d
-torus of
\operatorname{C}^{\circ}_{\mathbf{G}^{\ast}}(s)
.

(ii)

\mathbf{L}^{\ast}
is a minimal
d
-split Levi subgroup of
\mathbf{G}^{\ast}
which contains
s
.

In this case, the Levi subgroup
\mathbf{L}^{\ast}
is unique up to
\operatorname{C}_{\mathbf{G}^{\ast}}(s)^{F}
-conjugation and we call it the
d
-split Levi subgroup associated to
s
.

Proof.

Indeed, if
\mathbf{L}^{\ast}
is a Levi subgroup satisfying (ii), then
\operatorname{Z}^{\circ}(\mathbf{L}^{\ast})_{\Phi_{d}}\subset\operatorname{C}^{\circ}_{\mathbf{G}^{\ast}}(s)
and therefore by
[
MT11
, Theorem 25.11]
we have
\operatorname{Z}^{\circ}(\mathbf{L}^{\ast})_{\Phi_{d}}\leq\mathbf{T}_{0}^{\ast}
, up to
(\operatorname{C}^{\circ}_{\mathbf{G}^{\ast}}(s))^{F^{\ast}}
-conjugation. Since
\mathbf{L}^{\ast}=\operatorname{C}_{\mathbf{G}^{\ast}}(\operatorname{Z}^{\circ}(\mathbf{L}^{\ast})_{\Phi_{d}})
we find
\mathbf{L}^{\ast}=\operatorname{C}_{\mathbf{G}^{\ast}}(\mathbf{T}_{0}^{\ast})
by the minimality of
\mathbf{L}^{\ast}
and also
\operatorname{Z}^{\circ}(\mathbf{L}^{\ast})_{\Phi_{d}}=\mathbf{T}_{0}^{\ast}
. The converse direction is proved in the same fashion.

The uniqueness statement follows from the fact that any two Sylow
d
-tori of
\operatorname{C}_{\mathbf{G}^{\ast}}^{\circ}(s)
are
\operatorname{C}^{\circ}_{\mathbf{G}^{\ast}}(s)^{F}
-conjugate, see
[
MT11
, Theorem 25.11]
.
∎

Remark 1.9
.

For
d\in\{1,2\}
let
\mathbf{L}^{\ast}
be the minimal
d
-split Levi subgroup containing
s
. A consequence of the characterization of the previous lemma and
[
KM15
, Lemma 3.17]
is that
\operatorname{C}^{\circ}_{\mathbf{L}^{\ast}}(s)
is always a maximal torus of
\operatorname{C}^{\circ}_{\mathbf{G}^{\ast}}(s)
.

1.6.
Blocks of groups of Lie type

We let
\ell\neq p
be a fixed prime and assume that
s\in G^{\ast}
is a semisimple element of
\ell^{\prime}
-order. Denote by
\operatorname{C}_{G^{\ast}}(s)_{\ell}
the subset of
\ell
-power order elements of the centralizer of
s
. By a fundamental result of Broué–Michel
[
CE04
, Theorem 9.12]
, the set

\mathcal{E}_{\ell}(G,s)=\displaystyle\bigcup_{t\in\operatorname{C}_{G^{\ast}}(s)_{\ell}}\mathcal{E}(G,t)

is a union of
\ell
-blocks. We denote by
e_{s}^{\mathbf{G}^{F}}
the central idempotent in
\operatorname{Z}(\mathcal{O}\mathbf{G}^{F})
or
\operatorname{Z}(k\mathbf{G}^{F})
associated to this union of blocks.
The following lemma can be seen as a variant of Proposition
1.6
(c).

Lemma 1.10
.

Let
s\in(\mathbf{G}^{\ast})^{F}
be a semisimple element of
\ell^{\prime}
-order. For some integer
d\geq 1
we let
\mathbf{L}^{\ast}
be a minimal
d
-split Levi subgroup of
\mathbf{G}^{\ast}
containing
s
. If
\mathbf{T}^{\ast}
is a maximally split torus of
\operatorname{C}^{\circ}_{\mathbf{L}^{\ast}}(s)
, then

\operatorname{N}_{\mathbf{G}^{F}}(\mathbf{L},e_{s}^{\mathbf{L}^{F}})/\mathbf{L}^{F}\cong W(\operatorname{C}_{\mathbf{G}^{\ast}}(s),\mathbf{T}^{\ast})^{F}/W(\operatorname{C}_{\mathbf{L}^{\ast}}(s),\mathbf{T}^{\ast})^{F}.

Proof.

Duality between the Levi subgroups
\mathbf{L}
and
\mathbf{L}^{\ast}
yields an anti-isomorphism
\operatorname{N}_{\mathbf{G}^{F}}(\mathbf{L})/\mathbf{L}^{F}\cong\operatorname{N}_{(\mathbf{G}^{\ast})^{F}}(\mathbf{L}^{\ast})/(\mathbf{L}^{\ast})^{F}
under which the stabilizer of
e_{s}^{\mathbf{L}^{F}}
corresponds to the stabilizer of the
(\mathbf{L}^{\ast})^{F}
-conjugacy class of
s
. Let
n\mathbf{L}^{F}\in\operatorname{N}_{\mathbf{G}^{F}}(\mathbf{L})/\mathbf{L}^{F}
and
n^{\ast}(\mathbf{L}^{\ast})^{F}
the coset of
\operatorname{N}_{(\mathbf{G}^{\ast})^{F}}(\mathbf{L}^{\ast})/(\mathbf{L}^{\ast})^{F}
corresponding to it. By multiplying
n^{\ast}
by an element of
(\mathbf{L}^{\ast})^{F}
we can assume that
n^{\ast}
stabilizes
s
. In particular,
n^{\ast}
normalizes
\operatorname{C}_{\mathbf{L}^{\ast}}(s)
. Thus, there exists some
l\in\operatorname{C}^{\circ}_{\mathbf{L}^{\ast}}(s)^{F}
such that
n^{\ast}l
stabilizes
\mathbf{T}^{\ast}
. Hence, without loss of generality
n^{\ast}\in W(\operatorname{C}_{\mathbf{G}^{\ast}}(s),\mathbf{T}^{\ast})^{F}
. This induces a well-defined map

\operatorname{N}_{\mathbf{G}^{F}}(\mathbf{L},e_{s}^{\mathbf{L}^{F}})/\mathbf{L}^{F}\to W(\operatorname{C}_{\mathbf{G}^{\ast}}(s),\mathbf{T}^{\ast})^{F}/W(\operatorname{C}_{\mathbf{L}^{\ast}}(s),\mathbf{T}^{\ast})^{F},\quad

by sending the coset of
n
to the coset of
n^{\ast}
.

Assume conversely that
w\in W(\operatorname{C}_{\mathbf{G}^{\ast}}(s),\mathbf{T}^{\ast})^{F}
. By assumption
\mathbf{L}^{\ast}=\operatorname{C}_{\mathbf{G}^{\ast}}(\operatorname{Z}(\mathbf{L}^{\ast})^{\circ}_{\Phi_{d}})
.
We claim that
\operatorname{Z}(\mathbf{L}^{\ast})^{\circ}_{\Phi_{d}}=\mathbf{T}^{\ast}_{\Phi_{d}}
. Indeed,
\operatorname{Z}(\mathbf{L}^{\ast})^{\circ}
is a central subgroup of
\operatorname{C}^{\circ}_{\mathbf{L}^{\ast}}(s)
and hence contained in the maximal torus
\mathbf{T}^{\ast}
. Hence,
\operatorname{Z}(\mathbf{L}^{\ast})^{\circ}_{\Phi_{d}}\leq\mathbf{T}^{\ast}_{\Phi_{d}}
. On the other hand,
\operatorname{C}_{\mathbf{G}^{\ast}}(\mathbf{T}^{\ast}_{\Phi_{d}})\leq\mathbf{L}^{\ast}
is a
d
-split Levi subgroup of
\mathbf{G}^{\ast}
containing
s\in\mathbf{T}^{\ast}
. This implies
\mathbf{L}^{\ast}=\operatorname{C}_{\mathbf{G}^{\ast}}(\mathbf{T}^{\ast}_{\Phi_{d}})
and so
\mathbf{T}^{\ast}_{\Phi_{d}}=\operatorname{Z}(\mathbf{L}^{\ast})^{\circ}_{\Phi_{d}}
. In particular,
w
stabilizes the Levi subgroup
\mathbf{L}^{\ast}
and the semisimple element
s
.
∎

We also mention the following fact for later:

Lemma 1.11
.

Let
\mathbf{G}
be a connected reductive group,
s\in(\mathbf{G}^{\ast})^{F^{\ast}}
a semisimple element of
\ell^{\prime}
-order and suppose that
\mathbf{L}:=\operatorname{C}_{\mathbf{G}}(Q)
is a Levi subgroup for some
\ell
-subgroup
Q
of
\mathbf{G}^{F}
. Then
\mathrm{br}_{Q}(e_{s}^{\mathbf{G}^{F}})=\sum_{t}e_{t}^{\mathbf{L}^{F}}
, where
t
runs over a set of representatives of the
(\mathbf{L}^{\ast})^{F^{\ast}}
-conjugacy classes of semisimple
\ell^{\prime}
-elements of
(\mathbf{L}^{\ast})^{F}
which are
(\mathbf{G}^{\ast})^{F}
-conjugate to
s
.

Proof.

This follows from the explicit description of the Brauer morphism in
[
BDR17
, Theorem 4.14]
.
∎

Corollary 1.12
.

Under the assumptions of Lemma
1.11
assume additionally that
\mathbf{L}^{\ast}
is the minimal
d
-split Levi subgroup containing
s
for some integer
d
. Then
\mathrm{br}_{Q}(e_{s}^{\mathbf{G}^{F}})=\sum_{t}e_{t}^{\mathbf{L}^{F}}
, where
t
runs over a set of representatives of the
(\mathbf{L}^{\ast})^{F}
-conjugacy classes of semisimple
\ell^{\prime}
-elements of
(\mathbf{L}^{\ast})^{F}
which are
\operatorname{N}_{(\mathbf{G}^{\ast})^{F}}(\mathbf{L}^{\ast})
-conjugate to
s
.

Proof.

Assume that
t
is
(\mathbf{G}^{\ast})^{F}
-conjugate to
s
so that
s={}^{g}t
for some
g\in(\mathbf{G}^{\ast})^{F}
. Hence,
\mathbf{L}^{\ast}
and
{}^{g}\mathbf{L}^{\ast}
are both minimal
d
-split Levi subgroups that contain
s
and so
\mathbf{L}^{\ast}={}^{xg}\mathbf{L}^{\ast}
for some
x\in\operatorname{C}^{\circ}_{\mathbf{G}^{\ast}}(s)^{F}
. Hence,
s={}^{xg}t
with
xg\in\operatorname{N}_{(\mathbf{G}^{\ast})^{F}}(\mathbf{L}^{\ast})
.
∎

1.7.
Characters of quasi-central defect

We denote by
\mathcal{E}(G,\ell^{\prime})
the union of all Lusztig series
\mathcal{E}(G,s)
with
s
an element of
\ell^{\prime}
-order.
To introduce the parametrization of
\ell
-blocks by Kessar–Malle, we need to recall the following definition:

Definition 1.13
.

Let
\lambda\in\mathcal{E}(G,\ell^{\prime})
. We say that
\lambda
is of central
\ell
-defect if

|G|_{\ell}=\lambda(1)_{\ell}|\operatorname{Z}(G)|_{\ell}

and that
\lambda
is of quasi-central
\ell
-defect if some (and hence any) character of

[\mathbf{G},\mathbf{G}]^{F}

covered by
\lambda
is of central
\ell
-defect.

The blocks
b_{G}(\lambda)
with
\lambda
of central defect are quite easy to describe. By
[
KM13
, Proposition 2.5]
, the block
b_{G}(\lambda)
is a nilpotent block of central defect and hence by
[
Nav98
, Theorem 9.12]
we have a bijection
\operatorname{Irr}(\operatorname{Z}(G)_{\ell})\to\operatorname{Irr}(G,b_{G}(\lambda))
. Moreover, by
[
KM13
, Proposition 2.5]
,
G/[G,G]\operatorname{Z}(G)
is an
\ell^{\prime}
-group. Since
G/[G,G]\cong\operatorname{Z}^{\circ}(G)
it follows that

\operatorname{Irr}(G,b_{G}(\lambda))=\{\lambda\mu\mid\mu\in\operatorname{Irr}(G/[G,G])_{\ell}\},

whenever
\ell\nmid|\operatorname{Z}(G):\operatorname{Z}^{\circ}(G)|
.

2.
Kessar–Malle’s parametrization of
\ell
-blocks

2.1.
Kessar–Malle’s parametrization of
\ell
-blocks

From now on, unless stated explicitly otherwise,
\ell=2
and
\mathbf{G}
will always denote a simple, simply connected algebraic group of exceptional type with Frobenius endomorphism
F:\mathbf{G}\to\mathbf{G}
defining an
\mathbb{F}_{q}
-structure on
\mathbf{G}
, where
q
is an integral power of an odd prime
p
. Recall that a semisimple element
s\in\mathbf{G}^{\ast}
is called quasi-isolated if its centraliser
\mathrm{C}_{\mathbf{G}^{\ast}}(s)
is not contained in any proper Levi subgroup of
\mathbf{G}^{\ast}
. We assume now that
1\neq s
is such a quasi-isolated element of odd order in
G^{\ast}
and we let
b
be a
2
-block associated to
s
. Let
e
denote the order of
q
modulo
4
. According to
[
KM13
, Theorem 1.2]
there exists an
e
-cuspidal pair
(\mathbf{L},\lambda)
, unique up to
\mathbf{G}^{F}
-conjugation with
\lambda\in\mathcal{E}(L,s)
of quasi-central defect such that all irreducible constituents of
R_{\mathbf{L}}^{\mathbf{G}}(\lambda)
lie in
\mathcal{E}(G,s)\cap\operatorname{Irr}(b)
. We then write
b=b_{\mathbf{G}^{F}}(\mathbf{L},\lambda)
.

We say that
b
is a
maximal block
if its defect group has order
|\operatorname{C}_{G^{\ast}}(s)|_{\ell}
. For each
s
there is at least one maximal block associated to it, see
[
KM13
, Lemma 2.6(b)]
. More precisely we can characterize maximal blocks as follows:

Remark 2.1
.

Assume that
b=b_{\mathbf{G}^{F}}(\mathbf{L},\lambda)
is a maximal block. Then from the tables in
[
KM13
]
it can be deduced that the Levi subgroup
\mathbf{L}^{\ast}
is a minimal
e
-split Levi subgroup associated to the semisimple element
s
with
\lambda\in\mathcal{E}(L,s)
. Moreover, the character
\lambda
corresponds under Jordan decomposition to one of
|A_{L^{\ast}}(s)|
many regular-semisimple characters of
\mathcal{E}(\operatorname{C}_{L^{\ast}}(s),1)
.

2.2.
Jordan decomposition for blocks

We follow
[
Eng08
, 3.5.1(a)]
to construct a group
\mathbf{G}(s)
which is ”dual” to the not necessarily connected group
\operatorname{C}_{\mathbf{G}^{\ast}}(s)
. We observe that there exists a surjective morphism
\pi:\mathbf{G}\to\mathbf{G}_{\mathrm{ad}}
and we may identifty
\mathbf{G}_{\mathrm{ad}}
with
\mathbf{G}^{\ast}
as
\mathbf{G}
is simply connected of exceptional type. We define
\mathbf{G}(s):=\pi^{-1}(\operatorname{C}_{\mathbf{G}^{\ast}}(s))
which is an
F
-stable subgroup of
\mathbf{G}
. However, note that
\mathbf{G}(s)
is not necessarily the centralizer of an
F
-stable semisimple element of
\mathbf{G}
. We observe that the connected component
\mathbf{G}^{\circ}(s)
of
\mathbf{G}(s)
is a connected reductive group in duality with
\operatorname{C}^{\circ}_{\mathbf{G}^{\ast}}(s)
and the map
\pi:\mathbf{G}(s)\to\operatorname{C}_{\mathbf{G}^{\ast}}(s)
induces by
[
CE04
, Theorem 13.14]
an
(F,F^{\ast})
-equivariant isomorphism
\mathbf{G}(s)/\mathbf{G}^{\circ}(s)\cong A(s)
. By duality we obtain the following lemma:

Lemma 2.2
.

Let
s\in G^{\ast}
be a semisimple element of odd order and
G(s)=\mathbf{G}(s)^{F}
the group constructed above. Then we have

\operatorname{N}_{G}(\mathbf{G}(s),e_{s}^{G^{\circ}(s)})=G(s).

Proof.

If
\operatorname{C}^{\circ}_{\mathbf{G}^{\ast}}(s)
is a Levi subgroup of
\mathbf{G}^{\ast}
, then the conclusion of the lemma is contained in
[
BDR17
, Equation 7.1]
.

We let
\mathbf{T}^{\ast}
be a maximally split torus of
\operatorname{C}^{\circ}_{\mathbf{G}^{\ast}}(s)
and
\mathbf{T}\leq\mathbf{G}^{\circ}(s)
a maximal torus in duality with it. Any element
g\in\operatorname{N}_{G}(\mathbf{G}^{\circ}(s),e_{s}^{\mathbf{G}^{F}})
will map the torus
\mathbf{T}
to a
G^{\circ}(s)
-conjugate. We can therefore assume that
g\in\operatorname{N}_{G}(\mathbf{T})
. The image
w
of
g
in
W^{F}=\operatorname{N}_{G}(\mathbf{T})/T
corresponds to an element
w^{\ast}\in(W^{\ast})^{F}
in the dual Weyl group. By Theorem
1.4
(b) we therefore have
{}^{w}\mathcal{E}(G^{\circ}(s),s)=\mathcal{E}(G^{\circ}(s),{}^{w^{\ast}}s)
. However,
s
is
\operatorname{C}_{G^{\ast}}(s)
-conjugate to
{}^{w^{\ast}}s
if and only if
{}^{w^{\ast}}s=s
. By construction this means
g\in G(s)
.
∎

As before, let
\iota:\mathbf{G}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{\tilde{G}}
be a regular embedding and fix a semisimple element
\tilde{s}\in(\mathbf{\tilde{G}}^{\ast})^{F}
of
2^{\prime}
-order with
\iota^{\ast}(\tilde{s})=s
. The group
\mathbf{\tilde{G}}
is self-dual and we shall identify it with its dual
\mathbf{\tilde{G}}^{\ast}
compatible with the map
\pi:\mathbf{G}\to\mathbf{G}^{\ast}
. We let
\mathbf{\tilde{G}}(\tilde{s})
be the subgroup of
\mathbf{\tilde{G}}
corresponding to
\operatorname{C}_{\mathbf{\tilde{G}}^{\ast}}(\tilde{s})
under this identification. We have
\mathbf{\tilde{G}}(\tilde{s})=\operatorname{Z}(\mathbf{\tilde{G}})\mathbf{G}^{\circ}(s)
and so the group
\tilde{G}(\tilde{s})
induces all diagonal automorphisms of
G
. Finally note that
G(s)
normalizes
\tilde{G}(\tilde{s})
.

Lemma 2.3
.

With the notation from above, there exists a
\tilde{G}(\tilde{s})
-equivariant bijection
\mathcal{J}:\mathcal{E}_{2}(G,s)\to\mathcal{E}_{2}(G(s),s)
, which preserves the underlying character of
\operatorname{Z}(G)
.

Proof.

Suppose first that
A(s)^{F}=1
.
By Theorem
1.1
, for
t\in\operatorname{C}_{G^{\ast}}(s)_{2}
one has bijections
J_{G,st}:\mathcal{E}(G,st)\to\mathcal{E}(\operatorname{C}_{G^{\ast}}(st),1)
and
J_{G(s),st}:\mathcal{E}(G(s),st)\to\mathcal{E}(\operatorname{C}_{G^{\ast}}(st),1)
, where we observe that
\operatorname{C}_{\operatorname{C}_{G^{\ast}}(s)}(t)=\operatorname{C}_{G^{\ast}}(st)
. This yields a bijection
\mathcal{J}:=J_{G(s),st}^{-1}\circ J_{G,st}:\mathcal{E}(G,st)\to\mathcal{E}(G(s),st)
.

We now first construct the bijection in the general case. As before, we obtain a bijection
\tilde{\mathcal{J}}:\mathcal{E}_{2}(\tilde{G},\tilde{s})\to\mathcal{E}_{2}(\tilde{G}(\tilde{s}),\tilde{s})
. By duality, we have a canonical bijective map
\operatorname{Z}(\tilde{G}^{\ast})\to\operatorname{Irr}(\tilde{G}/G),z\mapsto\hat{z},
and since
\tilde{G}/G\cong\tilde{G}(\tilde{s})/G^{\circ}(s)
restriction defines a bijection

\operatorname{Irr}(\tilde{G}/G)\to\operatorname{Irr}(\tilde{G}(\tilde{s})/G^{\circ}(s)).

Fix a character
\chi\in\mathcal{E}(\tilde{G},\tilde{s}\tilde{t})
with
\tilde{t}\in\operatorname{C}_{\tilde{G}^{\ast}}(\tilde{s})_{2}
and let

\psi:=\tilde{\mathcal{J}}(\chi)=J_{\tilde{G}(\tilde{s}),\tilde{s}\tilde{t}}^{-1}(J_{\tilde{G},\tilde{s}\tilde{t}}(\chi))\in\mathcal{E}(\tilde{G}(\tilde{s}),\tilde{s}\tilde{t}).

Observe that
\hat{z}\chi=\chi
for
z\in\operatorname{Z}(\tilde{G}^{\ast})
implies that
z\tilde{s}\tilde{t}={}^{\tilde{a}}(\tilde{s}\tilde{t})
for some
\tilde{a}\in\tilde{G}^{\ast}
with image
a^{\ast}=\iota^{\ast}(\tilde{a})\in\operatorname{C}_{G^{\ast}}(st)\subset\operatorname{C}_{G^{\ast}}(s)
.
Let
a\in G(s)/G^{\circ}(s)
be the element corresponding to
a^{\ast}
in
A(s)^{F}
under the isomorphism
G(s)/G^{\circ}(s)\cong A(s)^{F}
. By Theorem
1.1
and Theorem
1.2
, we obtain

J_{\tilde{G}(\tilde{s}),\tilde{s}\tilde{t}}\tilde{z}^{-1}=J_{\tilde{G}(\tilde{s}),\tilde{s}\tilde{t}\tilde{z}}=J_{\tilde{G}(\tilde{s}),{}^{a^{\ast}}(\tilde{s}\tilde{t})}={}^{a}J_{\tilde{G}(\tilde{s}),\tilde{s}\tilde{t}}.

Hence,
\hat{z}\chi=\chi
if and only if
\hat{z}{}^{a}\psi=\psi
. Note that
a
is unique up to multiplication with an element of
\operatorname{C}_{\tilde{G}^{\ast}}(st)
. We therefore obtain a bijection

\operatorname{Irr}(\tilde{G}/G)_{\chi}\to(\operatorname{Irr}(\tilde{G}(\tilde{s})/G^{\circ}(s))\times G(s)/G^{\circ}(s))_{\psi},\hat{z}\mapsto(\hat{z},a).

Observe that
A(s)
has
2^{\prime}
-order by
[
CE04
, Proposition 13.16(i)]
. Moreover, for
z\in\operatorname{Z}(\tilde{G}^{\ast})
the elements
z_{2^{\prime}}\tilde{s}
and
\tilde{s}
are only
\operatorname{C}_{\mathbf{\tilde{G}}^{\ast}}(\tilde{s})
-conjugate when
z_{2^{\prime}}=1
. From this we deduce that
\operatorname{Irr}(\tilde{G}(\tilde{s})/G^{\circ}(s))_{\psi}
is a
2
-group. We therefore have a bijection

(\operatorname{Irr}(\tilde{G}/G)_{\chi})_{2}\to\operatorname{Irr}(\tilde{G}(\tilde{s})/G^{\circ}(s))_{\psi}.

Hence, by Clifford theory the character
\operatorname{Res}^{\tilde{G}(\tilde{s})}_{G^{\circ}(s)}(\psi)
has a
2
-power number of constituents. Denote
\mathcal{A}:=(G(s)/G^{\circ}(s))_{\operatorname{Res}^{\tilde{G}(\tilde{s})}_{G^{\circ}(s)}(\psi)}.

Similar considerations show that we have a bijection

(\operatorname{Irr}(\tilde{G}/G)_{\chi})_{2^{\prime}}\to\mathcal{A}.

In particular, since
\mathcal{A}
is a
2^{\prime}
-group and
\operatorname{Res}^{\tilde{G}(\tilde{s})}_{G^{\circ}(s)}(\psi)
has a
2
-power number of constituents we deduce that there exists an
\mathcal{A}
-stable constituent
\psi_{0}
. Since
[G(s),\tilde{G}(\tilde{s})]\subset G^{\circ}(s)
it follows that every constituent of
\operatorname{Res}^{\tilde{G}(\tilde{s})}_{G^{\circ}(s)}(\psi)
is
\mathcal{A}
-stable.

From this we deduce again by Clifford theory that the number of characters in
\operatorname{Irr}(G(s)\mid\operatorname{Res}^{\tilde{G}(\tilde{s})}_{G^{\circ}(s)}(\psi))
, i.e. the number of irreducible characters of
G(s)
which lie over an irreducible constituent of
\operatorname{Res}^{\tilde{G}(\tilde{s})}_{G^{\circ}(s)}(\psi)
, is equal to the number of characters in
\operatorname{Irr}(G\mid\chi)
. Moreover, by Mackey’s formula

\operatorname{Irr}(G(s)\mid\operatorname{Res}^{\tilde{G}(\tilde{s})}_{G^{\circ}(s)}(\psi))=\operatorname{Irr}(G(s)\mid\operatorname{Ind}_{\tilde{G}(\tilde{s})}^{\tilde{G}(\tilde{s})G(s)}(\psi)).

In particular, these characters form one
\tilde{G}(\tilde{s})
-orbit. We can therefore define a bijection
\mathcal{J}:\mathcal{E}_{2}(G,s)\to\mathcal{E}_{2}(G(s),s)
by sending the set
\operatorname{Irr}(G\mid\chi)
in a
\tilde{G}(\tilde{s})
-equivariant way to
\operatorname{Irr}(G(s),\operatorname{Ind}_{\tilde{G}(\tilde{s})}^{\tilde{G}(\tilde{s})G(s)}(\psi))
. The compatibility of
\mathcal{J}
with central characters follows from
[
Bon06
, Equation 9.12]
.
∎

Note that the bijection
\mathcal{J}
depends on our particular choices of Jordan decompositions involved in its definition. We will later refine the construction to make it suitable for our purposes.

Our aim is now to show that the number of unipotent
2
-blocks of
G(s)
and the number of
2
-blocks of
G
associated to
s
coincide.

Let
N
be a normal subgroup of a finite group
H
. Then we denote by
B_{0}(N)
its principal
\ell
-block and by
\mathrm{Bl}(H,B_{0}(N))
the set of blocks of
H
covering
B_{0}(N)
.

Lemma 2.4
.

Let
N
be a normal subgroup of a finite group
H
such that the factor group
H/N
is cyclic of
\ell^{\prime}
-order. Then
|\mathrm{Bl}(H,B_{0}(N))|=|\operatorname{C}_{H}(P)N:N|,
where
P
is a Sylow
\ell
-subgroup of
N
. Moreover, any block of
\operatorname{C}_{H}(P)N
covering
B_{0}(N)
is Morita equivalent by restriction to
B_{0}(N)
.

Proof.

Denote
b=B_{0}(N)
. We let
H[b]\lhd H
be Dade’s ramification group as in
[
Mur13
]
.
By
[
Mur13
, Theorem 3.5]
, block induction yields a surjective map
\mathrm{Bl}(H[b],b)\to\mathrm{Bl}(H,b)
.
However, this map is injective as well since every block of
\mathrm{Bl}(H[b],b)
is
H
-stable, due to it containing an extension of the trivial character and
H/N
being abelian. However, we have
H[b]\geq\operatorname{C}_{H}(P)N
by
[
Dad77
, Lemma 3]
. On the other hand, by
[
Nav98
, Theorem 9.19, Lemma 9.20]
, block induction gives a bijection

\mathrm{Bl}(\operatorname{C}_{H}(P)N,b)\to\mathrm{Bl}(H[b],b).

Since
H/N
is cyclic of
\ell^{\prime}
-order,
\operatorname{C}_{H}(P)N=H[b]
by
[
Mur13
, Proposition 3.9]
. The last statement follows from the main theorem of
[
Dad77
]
.
∎

Table
1
, which was taken from
[
KM13
, Table 3]
, lists all quasi-isolated
2
-blocks of
E_{6}(q)
.

2

Lemma 2.5
.

There exists a
\tilde{G}(\tilde{s})
-equivariant defect preserving bijection between the set of
2
-blocks of
G
associated to
s
and the set of
2
-blocks of
G(s)
associated to
s
.

Proof.

The number of
2
-blocks of
\mathcal{E}_{2}(G,s)
is given by the tables in
[
KM13
]
. Note that every numbered line corresponds to one
2
-block whenever
A_{\mathbf{L}^{\ast}}(s)^{F}=1
. If
A_{\mathbf{L}^{\ast}}(s)^{F}\neq 1
then
\mathbf{G}=E_{6}
and the
2
-blocks associated to
s
are all maximal blocks and correspond to the
|A_{\mathbf{L}^{\ast}}(s)^{F}|
many regular-semisimple characters of
\mathcal{E}(L,s)
.

We now compare this number to the number of unipotent
\ell
-blocks of
G(s)
. Observe that if
\mathbf{H}
is a connected reductive group then the number of unipotent
2
-blocks of
\mathbf{H}^{F}
is known: First use
[
CE04
, Proposition 17.1]
to reduce to the case of adjoint (simple) groups and then use the parametrization of
2
-blocks in
[
Eng00
]
. Applying this to the case
\mathbf{H}:=\mathbf{G}^{\circ}(s)
we can compute the number of blocks of
G^{\circ}(s)
. Using the tables of
[
KM13
]
and the aforementioned argument the lemma follows whenever
G^{\circ}(s)=G(s)
or equivalenty when
A_{\mathbf{G}^{\ast}}(s)^{F}=1
.

We can therefore assume that
\mathbf{G}
is of type
E_{6}
. Let us first assume that
e=1
and that
G=E_{6}(q)
. We inspect the cases in Table
1
. In case (6) we have
A_{\mathbf{G}^{\ast}}(s)^{F}=1
and the lemma follows. In cases (3)-(5), the connected centralizer
\operatorname{C}^{\circ}_{\mathbf{G}^{\ast}}(s)
is a Levi subgroup of
\mathbf{G}^{\ast}
and so the lemma follows (more naturally) from the Bonnafé–Dat–Rouquier equivalence
[
BDR17
]
.

In case (1) or (2) note that
G^{\circ}(s)
is of classical type and thus
\mathcal{E}_{2}(G^{\circ}(s),1)
is the set of characters lying in the principal
2
-block by
[
CE04
, Theorem 21.14]
. To compute the set of
2
-blocks of
G(s)
lying over the principal block of
G^{\circ}(s)
it is by Lemma
2.4
enough to compute the centralizer of a Sylow
2
-subgroup of
G^{\circ}(s)
inside
G(s)
. In case (1), a non-trivial element in
A(s)
permutes the three rational components of
G^{\circ}(s)
. We therefore obtain that
G(s)
has one block covering the principal block of
G^{\circ}(s)
. In case (2) we obtain that a non-trivial element in
A(s)
acts (modulo inner automorphisms of
G^{\circ}(s)
) as a field automorphism of order
3
on
G^{\circ}(s)\cong A_{2}(q^{3})
. Since
|A_{2}(q^{3})|_{2}=|A_{2}(q)|_{2}
, we obtain that such a field automorphism centralizes a Sylow
2
-subgroup of
G^{\circ}(s)
. We obtain again by Lemma
2.4
that there are exactly three blocks covering the principal block of
G^{\circ}(s)
.

The same arguments work for
e=2
, and the case
G={}^{2}E_{6}(q)
follows from Ennola duality.
∎

3.
Harish-Chandra theory

As before, let
\mathbf{G}
be a simple, simply connected group of exceptional type.
Recall that
s\in\mathbf{G}^{\ast}
is called isolated if
\operatorname{C}^{\circ}_{\mathbf{G}^{\ast}}(s)
is not contained in any proper Levi subgroup of
\mathbf{G}^{\ast}
. Since Levi subgroups are the centralizer of their connected center this is equivalent to
\operatorname{Z}^{\circ}(\operatorname{C}^{\circ}_{\mathbf{G}^{\ast}}(s))=1
, or equivalently to
\operatorname{C}^{\circ}_{\mathbf{G}^{\ast}}(s)
being a semisimple algebraic group. A character
\chi\in\operatorname{Irr}(H)
of a finite group
H
is called
2^{\prime}
-character if
\chi(1)
is odd.

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
s\in G^{\ast}
be a quasi-isolated semisimple element of odd order. Then any
2^{\prime}
-character of
\mathcal{E}_{\ell}(G^{\circ}(s),1)
lies in the principal series of
G^{\circ}(s)
.

Proof.

We first discuss the case when
s
is not isolated. In this case
\mathbf{G}
is of type
E_{6}
and
\mathbf{H}:=\mathbf{G}^{\circ}(s)
is a Levi subgroup of
\mathbf{G}
of type
D_{4}
by Table
1
. Since the center of
G
has order
3
, it follows from
[
CE04
, Proposition 13.12]
that the center of
\mathbf{H}
is connected and thus
\mathbf{H}^{F}=[\mathbf{H},\mathbf{H}]^{F}\times\operatorname{Z}(\mathbf{H})^{F}
. By
[
MT11
, Proposition 12.14]
and
[
MS16
, Proposition 8.7]
any
2^{\prime}
-character of
[\mathbf{H},\mathbf{H}]^{F}
lies in the principal series. Hence, by compatibility of Harish-Chandra series with the embedding of
[\mathbf{H},\mathbf{H}]^{F}
into
\mathbf{H}^{F}
, any
2^{\prime}
-character of
\mathbf{H}^{F}
lies in the principal series.
Assume now that
s
is isolated so that
\mathbf{H}:=\mathbf{G}^{\circ}(s)
is a semisimple algebraic group.
Let
\mathbf{H}\hookrightarrow\tilde{\mathbf{H}}
be a regular embedding and
\tau:\mathbf{H}_{\operatorname{sc}}\to\mathbf{H}
a simply connected covering. By
[
GM20
, Example 1.7.14]
there exists a regular embedding
\mathbf{H}_{\operatorname{sc}}\hookrightarrow\hat{\mathbf{H}}
together with a surjective map
\hat{\mathbf{H}}\to\tilde{\mathbf{H}}
whose kernel is a central torus such that the following diagram commutes:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{\mathbf{H}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\mathbf{H}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{H}_{sc}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{H}}
\iota

Let
\chi\in\operatorname{Irr}(H)
and
\tilde{\chi}\in\operatorname{Irr}(\tilde{H}\mid\chi)
a character of
\tilde{H}
covering it. As the kernel of
\hat{\mathbf{H}}\to\tilde{\mathbf{H}}
is connected the map
\hat{H}\to\tilde{H}
on
F
-fixed points is surjective and we can thus consider
\tilde{\chi}
via inflation as character of
\hat{H}
along this surjection. Let
\chi^{\prime}
be a character of
H_{sc}
below
\tilde{\chi}
. By
[
CE04
, Theorem 15.11]
and Clifford theory we have the equalites

\frac{\tilde{\chi}(1)}{\chi(1)}=|\{\lambda\in\operatorname{Irr}(\tilde{H}/H\operatorname{Z}(\tilde{H}))\mid\lambda\tilde{\chi}=\tilde{\chi}\}|

and

\frac{\tilde{\chi}(1)}{\chi^{\prime}(1)}=|\{\lambda\in\operatorname{Irr}(\hat{H}/H_{\operatorname{sc}}\operatorname{Z}(\hat{H}))\mid\lambda\tilde{\chi}=\tilde{\chi}\}|.

The map
\hat{H}\to\tilde{H}
is surjective with central kernel and maps
H_{\operatorname{sc}}\operatorname{Z}(\hat{H})
to
\operatorname{Z}(\tilde{H})H
. Thus, deflation yields a surjection
\operatorname{Irr}(\hat{H}/H_{\operatorname{sc}}\operatorname{Z}(\hat{H}))\to\operatorname{Irr}(\tilde{H}/Z(\tilde{H})H)
.
Therefore,
\chi^{\prime}(1)\mid\chi(1)
. Thus, if
\chi
is a
2^{\prime}
-character then so is
\chi^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Irr}(H_{\operatorname{sc}})
.

Now,
H_{\operatorname{sc}}\cong H_{1}\times\dots\times H_{r}
where each
H_{i}
is the fixed points under a Frobenius of a simple algebraic group of simply connected type. An inspection of the relevant tables in
[
KM13
]
shows that
H_{i}
is never of rational type
C_{n}(q^{\prime})
for
n\geq 1
and
q^{\prime}
an integral power of
q
. By
[
Mal19
, Theorem 3.3]
it thus follows that every
2^{\prime}
-degree character of
H_{i}
lies in the principal series. From this, we deduce that
\chi^{\prime}
lies in the principal series of
H_{\operatorname{sc}}
. It thus follows by the commutative diagram above and the compatibility of Harish-Chandra induction with regular embeddings and central quotients that
\chi
lies in the principal series as well.
∎

Corollary 3.2
.

Let
s\in G^{\ast}
be a quasi-isolated
2^{\prime}
-element and
\mathbf{L}_{0}
be the minimal
1
-split Levi subgroup associated to
s
, see Lemma
1.8
. Then every height zero character of
\mathcal{E}_{\ell}(G,s)
, which lies in a maximal block, lies in the Harish-Chandra series of a semisimple character in
\mathcal{E}_{\ell}(L_{0},s)
.

Proof.

Let us first assume that the center of
\mathbf{G}
is connected.
Observe by Remark
1.9
or alternatively by checking the tables in
[
KM13
]
that
\operatorname{C}_{\mathbf{L}_{0}^{\ast}}({s})
is a maximally split torus of
\operatorname{C}_{\mathbf{G}^{\ast}}({s})
. Therefore,
\operatorname{C}_{\mathbf{L}_{0}^{\ast}}({st})=\operatorname{C}_{\mathbf{L}_{0}^{\ast}}({s})
is a maximal torus of
\operatorname{C}_{\mathbf{G}^{\ast}}(st)
for all
t\in\operatorname{C}_{G^{\ast}}(s)_{2}
. Thus, by applying
[
GM20
, Theorem 4.8.24]
twice, we can find appropriate Jordan decompositions such that the left square in the following diagram commutes. For
H\in\{G,L_{0}\}
we define
\mathcal{J}_{H}:=J_{H(s),st}^{-1}\circ J_{H,st}
so that also the right square in the diagram commutes.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{E}({C}_{G^{\ast}}(st),1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{E}(G(s),t)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{E}({G},{st})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{E}({C}_{L_{0}^{\ast}}({s}{t}),1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{E}(L_{0}(s),t)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{E}({L_{0}},{st})}
R_{{L_{0}(s)}}^{{G(s)}}
J_{G(s),st}
J_{L_{0},st}
R_{{C}_{L_{0}^{\ast}}({s}{t})}^{{C}_{G^{\ast}}(st)}
R_{{L_{0}}}^{{G}}
\mathcal{J}_{G}
\mathcal{J}_{L_{0}}

Now, under the map
\mathcal{J}_{G}
any character
\chi\in\mathcal{E}(G,st)
with
\chi(1)_{2}=|G^{\ast}:\operatorname{C}_{G^{\ast}}(s)|_{2}
corresponds to a character
\psi:=\mathcal{J}_{G}(\chi)\in\mathcal{E}_{2}(G(s),t)
of
2^{\prime}
-degree. By Lemma
3.1
this character lies in the principal series. It follows that
\chi
lies in the Harish-Chandra series of the (unique) semisimple character in
\mathcal{E}(L_{0},st)
.

Finally assume that
G
is of type
E_{6}
. We consider a regular embedding
\mathbf{G}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{\tilde{G}}
such that
\mathbf{\tilde{G}}=\mathbf{\tilde{G}}^{\ast}
. Recall
\tilde{\mathbf{G}}(\tilde{s})=\operatorname{C}_{\mathbf{\tilde{G}}^{\ast}}(\tilde{s})
and that
\tilde{\mathbf{G}}(\tilde{s})=\operatorname{Z}(\tilde{\mathbf{G}})\mathbf{G}^{\circ}(\tilde{s})
.

Now suppose that
\chi\in\mathcal{E}_{\ell}(G,s)
is a height zero character. Then there exists a character
\tilde{\chi}\in\mathcal{E}_{\ell}(\tilde{G},\tilde{s})
, which covers
\chi
. Since
2|\nmid\tilde{G}/G\operatorname{Z}(\tilde{G})|
this character has necessarily height zero again. As the arguments from above apply in the group
\tilde{\mathbf{G}}
as well, we deduce that
\tilde{\chi}
also lies in the principal series. Since Harish-Chandra series are compatible with regular embeddings it follows that
\chi
also lies in the principal series.
∎

Note that in all cases under consideration,
\operatorname{C}^{\circ}_{\mathbf{L}_{0}^{\ast}}(s)
is a maximal torus and
\operatorname{Z}^{\circ}(\operatorname{C}^{\circ}_{\mathbf{L}_{0}^{\ast}}(s))_{\Phi_{1}}=\operatorname{Z}^{\circ}(\mathbf{L}_{0}^{\ast})_{\Phi_{1}}
, see Remark
1.9
. Hence, every character in
\mathcal{E}_{\ell}(\mathbf{L}_{0},s)
is cuspidal, see
[
CE99
, Propositiion 1.10]
.

We let
\Lambda_{0}
be an
\operatorname{N}_{G}(\mathbf{L}_{0})
-equivariant extension map for
L_{0}\lhd\operatorname{N}_{G}(\mathbf{L}_{0})
(such a map always exists, see the beginning of
[
MS16
, Section 5]
). As in
[
MS16
, Section 5]
the extension map
\Lambda_{0}
uniquely determines for every (cuspidal) character
\theta\in\mathcal{E}_{\ell}(\mathbf{L}_{0},s)
a bijection

\operatorname{Irr}(W_{\mathbf{G}}(\mathbf{L}_{0},\theta))\to\mathcal{E}(G,(\mathbf{L}_{0},\theta)),\quad\eta\mapsto R_{L_{0}}^{G}(\theta)_{\eta}.

Corollary 3.3
.

In the situation of Corollary
3.2
, assume that a character
\chi=R_{L_{0}}^{G}(\theta)_{\eta}\in\mathcal{E}_{\ell}(G,s)
satisfies
\chi(1)_{2}=|G^{\ast}:\operatorname{C}_{G^{\ast}}(s)|_{2}
. Then
W_{G}(\mathbf{L}_{0},\theta)
contains a Sylow
2
-subgroup of
W_{G}(\mathbf{L}_{0},e_{s}^{L_{0}})
and
\eta
is of
2^{\prime}
-degree.

Proof.

We use the notation of Corollary
3.2
and
[
MS16
, Section 8.A]
. We argue as in
[
MS16
, Lemma 8.9]
.
Let
D_{\chi}\in\mathbb{Q}(X)
as in the remarks preceeding
[
MS16
, Lemma 8.2]
. As argued there, the numerator and denominator of
D_{\chi}
are coprime to
X-1
. Define
f_{\chi}^{\prime}:=(|\mathbb{G}|/|\mathbb{L}_{0}|)_{X^{\prime}}D_{\chi}f_{\theta}
, where
f_{\theta}
is the degree polynomial of the cuspidal character
\theta
. Note that
\theta\in\mathcal{E}(L_{0},st)
for some
t\in\operatorname{C}_{\mathbf{G}^{\ast}}(s)_{\ell}
. Recall that
\operatorname{C}^{\circ}_{\mathbf{L}_{0}^{\ast}}(st)=\operatorname{C}^{\circ}_{\mathbf{L}_{0}^{\ast}}(s)
is a maximal torus of
\mathbf{L}_{0}^{\ast}
and so
\theta
is a regular-semisimple character in the Lusztig series
\mathcal{E}(L_{0},st)
. For
\mathbf{H}\in\{\mathbf{L}_{0}^{\ast},\mathbf{G}^{\ast}\}
let
\mathbb{H}_{s}
be the degree polynomial of
(\operatorname{C}_{\mathbf{H}}(s),F)
, see
[
MS16
, Section 8.A]
. We deduce that
f_{\theta}=(|\mathbb{L}_{0}|/|\mathbb{L}_{0,s}|)_{X^{\prime}}
. It follows that

g_{\chi}^{\prime}:=f_{\chi}^{\prime}(|\mathbb{G}_{s}|/|\mathbb{G}|)_{X^{\prime}}=(|\mathbb{G}_{s}|/|\mathbb{L}_{0,s}|)_{X^{\prime}}D_{\chi}\in\mathbb{Q}(X).

We have
\chi(1)=|G:L_{0}|_{p^{\prime}}D_{\chi}(q)\theta(1)
. Therefore, we obtain
g_{\chi}^{\prime}(q)=f_{\chi}^{\prime}(q)/|G:G(s)|=\chi(1)/|G:G(s)|
which is an odd integer by assumption. Since this is an integer for all prime powers
q
, it follows that
g_{\chi}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Q}[X]
. Now evaluating
g_{\chi}^{\prime}
at
1
yields
g_{\chi}^{\prime}(1)=|W_{\operatorname{C}_{G^{\ast}}(s)}(\operatorname{C}_{\mathbf{L}_{0}^{\ast}}(s))|D_{\chi}(1)
by
[
Mal07
, Theorem 3.2(d)]
. Moreover,
D_{\chi}(1)=\eta(1)/|W_{G}(\mathbf{L}_{0},\theta)|
by
[
MS16
, Equation 8.2]
. For
i\geq 2
we have
\Phi_{i}(q)_{2}\geq\Phi_{i}(1)_{2}
(see the proof of
[
MS16
, Proposition 8.4]
) and so
g_{\chi}^{\prime}(q)_{2}\geq g_{\chi}^{\prime}(1)_{2}
. It follows that
g^{\prime}_{\chi}(1)
is odd as well. By Remark
1.7
we conclude that
\eta(1)
and
|W_{G}(\mathbf{L}_{0},e_{s}^{L_{0}})/W_{G}(\mathbf{L}_{0},\theta)|
have to be odd as well.
∎

For later we also state the following result about Harish-Chandra series and automorphisms. We let
\mathcal{B}\subset\mathrm{Aut}(\tilde{G})
be the subgroup generated by field and graph automorphisms as in
[
MS16
, Section 2.B]
.

Theorem 3.4
.

Assume that there exists an
\operatorname{N}_{{G}\mathcal{B}}(\mathbf{L}_{0})
-equivariant extension map
\Lambda_{0}
for
L_{0}\lhd\operatorname{N}_{G}(\mathbf{L}_{0})
. Assume that
\lambda_{0}\in\operatorname{Irr}(L_{0})
is a
\tilde{L}_{0}
-stable character.
Let
x\in\operatorname{N}_{\tilde{G}\mathcal{B}}(\mathbf{L}_{0})
and
\delta_{\lambda_{0},x}\in\operatorname{Irr}(W_{G}(\mathbf{L}_{0},{}^{x}\lambda_{0}))
such that
\delta_{\lambda_{0},x}\Lambda_{0}({}^{x}\lambda_{0})={}^{x}\Lambda_{0}(\lambda_{0})
.
Then

{}^{x}(R_{L_{0}}^{G_{0}}(\lambda_{0})_{\eta_{0}})=R_{L_{0}}^{G_{0}}({}^{x}\lambda_{0})_{{}^{x}\eta_{0}\delta_{\lambda_{0},x}^{-1}}.

Proof.

Denote by
W
the relative Weyl group
W_{G}(\mathbf{L}_{0})
.
We want to deduce the result from
[
MS16
, Theorem 5.6,Theorem 5.7]
as explained in the proof of
[
MS16
, Proposition 6.3]
. For this we need to check that the assumptions of
[
MS16
, Theorem 5.6]
hold. As in
[
MS16
, Lemma 6.1,Lemma 6.2]
it suffices to check that
R(\lambda_{0})\leq W(\tilde{\lambda}_{0})
for
\tilde{\lambda}_{0}\in\operatorname{Irr}(\tilde{L}_{0}\mid\lambda_{0})
, where
R(\lambda_{0})\leq W(\lambda_{0})
is defined as in the remarks before
[
MS16
, Lemma 5.2]
. This is however the case by
[
CSFS21
, Lemma 4.14]
.
∎

For
x\in\operatorname{N}_{G\mathcal{B}}(\mathbf{L}_{0})
the properties of
\Lambda_{0}
imply that
\delta_{\lambda_{0},x}
is trivial. In this case we have by the proof of
[
MS16
, Proposition 6.3]
that
{}^{x}(R_{L_{0}}^{G_{0}}(\lambda_{0})_{\eta_{0}})=R_{L_{0}}^{G_{0}}({}^{x}\lambda_{0})_{{}^{x}\eta_{0}}
without the assumption on
\lambda_{0}
.

4.
The Cabanes property for Sylow
2
-subgroups

4.1.
The Cabanes property

Definition 4.1
.

Let
H
be a finite group and
A
an abelian subgroup of
H
. Then
H
is said to be Cabanes with Cabanes subgroup
A
if
A
is the unique maximal abelian normal subgroup of
H
.

It was observed in
[
Cab94
]
that the Sylow
\ell
-subgroups of groups of Lie type are Cabanes provided that
\ell\geq 5
.

Let
A\lhd H
be an abelian normal subgroup. Then an element
h\in H
is said to act quadraticely on
A
if
[h,[h,A]]=1
. Equivalently, we have
(h-1)^{2}=0
in
\mathrm{End}(A)
. Quadratic actions are related to the Cabanes property as the following proposition shows.

Proposition 4.2
.

Let
A\lhd H
be an abelian normal subgroup. Then
A
is the unique maximal normal abelian subgroup of
H
if and only if no non-trivial element in
H/A
acts quadraticely on
A
.

Proof.

See
[
Cab94
, Proposition 2.3]
.
∎

The following example shows that the criterion in the previous proposition is not necessary for a group to have a characteristic maximal normal abelian subgroup.

Example 4.3
.

Assume that
H=D_{8}=C_{4}\rtimes C_{2}
. Then
H
has a unique cyclic normal subgroup
A=C_{4}
but
h\in H\setminus A
acts quadraticely on
A
. On the other hand,
H=Q_{8}
has three different maximal abelian normal subgroups, with none of them being characteristic.

Lemma 4.4
.

Let
A\lhd H
be a normal abelian subgroup such that
H/A
is a
2
-group. Then
H
is Cabanes unless there exists an involution
h\in H\setminus A
with
({}^{h}tt^{-1})^{2}=1
for all
t\in A
.

Proof.

A short calculation shows that
[h,[h,t]]={}^{h^{2}}t({}^{h}t)^{-2}t
for
h\in H
and
t\in A
. If
h
acts quadraticely on
A
then so does any power of
h
. Thus, if
H/A
is a
2
-group it suffices to determine all involutions which act quadraticely on
A
. If
h^{2}=1
then
1=[h,[h,t]]=t^{2}({}^{h}t)^{-2}
implies
{}^{h}t\in\{t,t^{-1}\}
for all
t\in A
.
∎

4.2.
Sylow
2
-subgroup of groups of Lie type in odd characteristic

In order to construct Sylow
2
-subgroups of groups of Lie type we use the following theorem of Malle, see
[
Mal07
, Theorem 5.19]
.

Theorem 4.5
.

Let
\mathbf{H}
be a simple algebraic group with Frobenius
F
and
\mathbf{T}
a Sylow
e
-torus, where
e
is the order of
q
modulo
4
. Assume that
\mathbf{H}^{F}
is not isomorphic to
\mathrm{Sp}_{2n}(q)
whenever
n\geq 1
and
q\equiv 3,5\mod 8
. Then there exists a Sylow
2
-subgroup
P
of
\mathbf{H}^{F}
with
\operatorname{N}_{\mathbf{H}^{F}}(P)\leq\operatorname{N}_{\mathbf{H}^{F}}(\mathbf{T})
.

Remark 4.6
.

It was stated erroneously in
[
Cab18
, Remark 7.9]
that the Sylow
2
-subgroup
P
of
\mathbf{H}^{F}
is always Cabanes in the situation of the previous theorem. A counterexample to this is for instance the Sylow
2
-subgroup of
\mathrm{PGL}_{2}(\mathbb{F}_{13})
which is not Cabanes. We will however see in the following that in many cases the Sylow
2
-subgroup is actually Cabanes.

Let
\mathbf{G}
be a simply connected, simple algebraic group with root system
\Phi
and base
\Delta
. As in
[
GLS98
, Theorem 1.12.1]
let
x_{\alpha}:\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{q}\to\mathbf{G}
be the Chevalley generators of
\mathbf{G}
so that
\mathbf{G}=\langle x_{\alpha}(t)\mid\alpha\in\Phi,t\in\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{q}\rangle
. Denote by
S:=\{s_{\alpha}\mid\alpha\in\Delta\}
the set of simple reflections. We let
F
be the Frobenius endomorphism acting as
x_{\alpha}(t)\mapsto x_{\tau\alpha}(t^{q})
for
t\in\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
,
\alpha\in\Delta
and
\tau
a (possibly trivial) symmetry of the Dynkin diagram associated to
\mathbf{G}
. For
t\in\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{\times}
and
\alpha\in\Phi
we define

h_{\alpha}(t):=n_{\alpha}(t)n_{\alpha}(-1)\text{ where }n_{\alpha}(t):=x_{\alpha}(t)x_{-\alpha}(-t^{-1})x_{\alpha}(t),

as in the remarks after
[
Spä06
, Definition 2.1.5]
. We let
\mathbf{T}=\langle h_{\alpha}(t)\mid\alpha\in\Delta\rangle
which is a maximal torus of
\mathbf{G}
. As
\mathbf{G}
is simply connected the torus
\mathbf{T}
is the direct product of the subgroups
h_{\alpha}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{\times})
,
\alpha\in\Delta
, see
[
GLS98
, Theorem 1.12.5]
. Let
W=\langle S\rangle
be the Weyl group of
\mathbf{T}
and
w_{0}
the longest element in
W
.

Let
I\subset S
be a subset of the set of simple reflections. We denote by
w_{I}
the longest element of the Weyl group associated to the parabolic system
W_{I}
.

Lemma 4.7
.

Let
\emptyset\neq I
be a proper subset of
S
. Then there exists
\beta\in\Delta
such that
w_{I}(\beta)\in\Phi^{+}\setminus\{\beta\}
.

Proof.

As the Dynkin diagram associated to
\Delta
is connected there exists
\beta\in\Delta\setminus\Delta_{I}
such that
(\alpha,\beta)<0
for some
\alpha\in\Delta_{I}
. We have
w_{I}(\beta)\in\Phi^{+}
by
[
MT11
, Corollary A.26(b)]
. Moreover, by
[
MT11
, Lemma B.1]
we have
\alpha+\beta\in\Phi^{+}
. The roots
\beta
and
\alpha+\beta
have the same shape
\mathcal{S}
with respect to
I
. Moreover,
\beta
is the root of minimal height of shape
\mathcal{S}
. Therefore, using the hint in
[
MT11
, Exercise A.7]
(and that
w_{I}^{2}=1
) we deduce that
w_{I}(\beta)
is the root of maximal height of shape
\mathcal{S}
. As there are roots of different height of shape
\mathcal{S}
(as clearly
\alpha
and
\alpha+\beta
have different heights) we must necessarily have
w_{I}(\beta)\neq\beta
.
∎

Lemma 4.8
.

Assume that
\Phi
is an irreducible root system of type
A_{n},D_{n},E_{6}
with base
\Delta
. Let
\alpha\in\Phi^{+}\setminus\Delta
be a positive root and
\alpha=\sum_{\beta\in\Delta}n_{\beta}\beta
. Then there exist at least two
\beta\in\Delta
with
n_{\beta}=1
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality we can replace the root system
\Phi
by the support of
\alpha
(i.e. the root system generated by the set of simple roots
\beta\in\Delta
with
n_{\beta}\neq 0
). This is an irreducible root system again of type
A_{n}
,
D_{n}
with
n>1
or
E_{6}
. Let
\Delta^{\prime}\subset\Delta
be its base. An inspection of
[
MT11
, Table B.1]
shows that the highest root
\alpha_{0}=\sum_{\gamma\in\Delta^{\prime}}m_{\gamma}\gamma
in this new root system has at least two roots
\gamma\in\Delta^{\prime}
with
m_{\gamma}=1
. Since
1\leq n_{\gamma}\leq m_{\gamma}=1
by
[
MT11
, Proposition B.5]
, we also find that
n_{\gamma}=1
.
∎

We also make use of the following well-known theorem, see the main theorem of
[
Ric82
]
.

Theorem 4.9
.

Let
W
be a Weyl group and
w\in W
an involution. Then there exists a subset
I\subset S
, unique up to
W
-conjugation, such that
w
is conjugate to
w_{I}
and
w_{I}
acts by
-1
on
W_{I}
.

If for instance
W
is of type
A_{n}
, then the longest element in
W
is conjugate to the longest element in a parabolic subgroup of type
(A_{1})^{\lfloor n-1/2\rfloor}
.

Theorem 4.10
.

Let
\mathbf{G}
be a simply connected, simple algebraic group with Frobenius endomorphism
F:\mathbf{G}\to\mathbf{G}
. Suppose that
\mathbf{G}^{F}
is not isomorphic to
{}^{3}D_{4}(q)
.
Assume that
w_{0}
does not act as
-1
on
W
or
8\mid\Phi_{e}(q)
. Then the Sylow
2
-subgroups of
\mathbf{G}^{F}
are Cabanes.

Proof.

Theorem
4.5
can be used to describe a Sylow
2
-subgroup
P
of
\mathbf{G}^{F}
, see
[
MS16
, Lemma 3.2]
. If
e=1
it follows that
P
is an extension of
\mathbf{T}^{F}
by a Sylow
2
-subgroup of
W^{F}
while for
e=2
the Sylow
2
-subgroup
P
is isomorphic to an extension of
\mathbf{T}^{w_{0}F}
by
W^{w_{0}F}
.

Let us first assume that
e=1
and
(\mathbf{G},F)
is untwisted.
By Lemma
4.4
it is enough to show that no involution
w
in
W
acts quadraticely on
A:=\mathbf{T}^{F}\cong(\mathbb{F}_{q}^{\times})^{n}
. By Theorem
4.9
we know that
w
is conjugate to the longest element
w_{I}
in
W_{I}
for some subset
I
of the simple reflections. We may thus assume
w=w_{I}
and our aim is to construct an element
a\in A
such that
{}^{w}aa^{-1}
has at least order
4
.

Assume first that
8\mid(q-1)
and
w=w_{0}
is the longest element acting as
-1
on
W
. Then for any root
\alpha\in\Delta
and
t\in\mathbb{F}_{q}^{\times}
of order
8
we have

{}^{w}h_{\alpha}(t)h_{\alpha}(t)^{-1}=h_{w(\alpha)}(t)h_{\alpha}(t^{-1})=h_{\alpha}(t^{-2}),

which has order
4
.

If
I
is a proper subset of
S
and
w=w_{I}
, then by Lemma
4.7
there exists
\alpha\in\Delta\setminus\Delta_{I}
such that
w(\alpha)\neq\pm\alpha
. We obtain

{}^{w}h_{\alpha}(t)h_{\alpha}(t^{-1})=h_{w(\alpha)}(t)h_{\alpha}(t^{-1})=h_{w(\alpha)-\alpha}(t).

We can now write
w(\alpha)=\sum_{\gamma\in\Delta}n_{\gamma}\gamma
and by Lemma
4.8
there exists some
\beta\in\Delta\setminus\{\alpha\}
with
n_{\beta}=1
. Recall that
\mathbf{T}
is the direct product of the subgroups
h_{\delta}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{\times})
,
\delta\in\Delta
. In particular, we deduce that
h_{w(\alpha)-\alpha}(t)
has the same order as
t\in\mathbb{F}_{q}^{\times}
. This shows the claim since
4\mid(q-1)
.

Assume now still that
e=1
, but
F=\sigma F_{q}
for some non-trivial graph automorphism
\sigma
of order
2
. The group
W^{\sigma}
is again an irreducible Weyl group of different type. Its type is determined by the following rule (see
[
CE04
, page 121]
or
[
MT11
, Table 23.1]
):

{}^{2}A_{n}\to BC_{[(n+1)/2]},\quad{}^{2}D_{n}\to BC_{n-1},\quad{}^{2}E_{6}\to F_{4}.

Let
\tilde{S}
(resp.
\tilde{\Delta}
) be the set of
\sigma
-orbits on
S
(resp.
\Delta
).
We have
W^{\sigma}=\langle s_{\omega}\mid\omega\in\tilde{S}\rangle
, see
[
MT11
, Lemma 23.3]
. For each
\sigma
-orbit of
\Delta
fix a representative
\alpha
and denote by
\tilde{\alpha}
its
\sigma
-orbit. Similarly, we obtain
\mathbf{T}^{F}=\prod_{\tilde{\alpha}\in\tilde{\Delta}}\mathbf{T}_{\tilde{\alpha}}^{F}
, where
\mathbf{T}_{\tilde{\alpha}}:=\prod_{\gamma\in\tilde{\alpha}}\mathbf{T}_{\gamma}
. More precisely, such an isomorphism is given by the map

\mathbf{T}_{\tilde{\alpha}}^{F}\to\mathbb{F}_{q^{|\tilde{\alpha}|}}^{\times},\,t\mapsto h_{\tilde{\alpha}}(t):=h_{\alpha}(t)h_{\sigma\alpha}(t^{q}).

Again, by Theorem
4.9
an involution in
W^{\sigma}
is
W^{\sigma}
-conjugate to an element
w_{\tilde{J}}
, where
\tilde{J}\subset\tilde{S}
. Let
I
be the union of all orbits
\omega\in\tilde{J}
. By
[
MT11
, Lemma C.3]
and
[
MT11
, Corollary A.23]
we have
w_{I}=w_{\tilde{J}}
.
Let
w_{0}\in W^{\sigma}
and assume again that
w_{0}
acts as
-1
on
W
. For
\tilde{\alpha}
we obtain
{}^{w_{0}}h_{\tilde{\alpha}}(t)h_{\tilde{\alpha}}(t)^{-1}=h_{\tilde{\alpha}}(t^{-2})
, which has order
4
again.

However, if the element
w_{0}
acts as the non-trivial graph automorphism on
W
it follows that
w_{0}\in\operatorname{Z}(W^{\sigma})
. In particular, the element
w_{0}
acts on every root subgroup
\mathbf{T}_{\omega}^{F}\cong\mathbb{F}_{q^{|\omega|}}^{\times}
as the automorphism
t\mapsto t^{q}
. Let
\alpha\in\Delta
be an element of the base which is not
\sigma
-stable. Let
t\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}}^{\times}
be an element of order
8
(this is always possible as
8\mid(q^{2}-1)
). In particular,
{}^{w_{0}}h_{\tilde{\alpha}}(t)h_{\tilde{\alpha}}(t)^{-1}=h_{\tilde{\alpha}}(t^{-(q+1)})
which has order
4
since
q\equiv 1\mod 4
.

Now let us suppose that
I
is a proper
\sigma
-stable subset of
S
and assume that
\alpha\in\Delta\setminus\Delta_{I}
is as above with
w(\alpha)\neq\pm\alpha
. If
\alpha
is
\sigma
-stable then the exact argument from the untwisted case applies here as well. Otherwise, for
t\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}}^{\times}
an element of order
8
consider

{}^{w}(h_{\alpha}(t)h_{\sigma\alpha}(t^{q}))(h_{\alpha}(t)h_{\sigma\alpha}(t^{q}))^{-1}.

Let
\beta\notin\{\pm\alpha\}
be a simple root whose coefficient equals
n_{\beta}=1
in the linear combination of
w(\alpha)
. Assume first that
\beta
is
\sigma
-stable. Hence,
h_{\beta}(t^{2})
is the contribution by
\beta
in the element
{}^{w}(h_{\alpha}(t)h_{\sigma\alpha}(t^{q}))
. On the other hand, if
\beta
is not
\sigma
-stable then a short calculation shows that
h_{\tilde{\beta}}(t^{n_{\beta}+qn_{\sigma(\beta)}})
is the contribution by
\beta+\sigma\beta
in the element
{}^{w}(h_{\alpha}(t)h_{\sigma\alpha}(t^{q}))
. As
q\equiv 1\mod 4
we deduce that
n_{\beta}+qn_{\sigma(\beta)}
is divisible by four if and only if
4\mid n_{\beta}+n_{\sigma\beta}
. However, an analysis of
[
MT11
, Table B.1]
together with the proof of Lemma
4.8
shows that there is no element
w(\alpha)\in\Phi
with this property.

We have therefore dealt with all the cases when
e=1
and
F
arbitrary. The remaining cases follow by Ennola duality observing that either
w_{0}
or
\sigma w_{0}
acts by
-1
. The previous arguments therefore remain true when replacing
q
by
-q
everywhere. ∎

In the following remark, we show that the Sylow
2
-subgroups of
{}^{3}D_{4}(q)
have a characteristic maximal abelian normal subgroup (which is sufficient for most purposes). For this recall that the Weyl group
W^{F}
of
\mathbf{G}^{F}={}^{3}D_{4}(q)
is isomorphic to
S_{3}\times C_{2}
. We have
|\mathbf{T}^{w_{0}^{e-1}F}|=\Phi_{e}^{2}(q)\Phi_{3e}(q)
in this case. The longest element
w_{0}
of
W
acts by inversion and there exists a non-central involution in
W
which acts by permuting the two copies of
\mathbf{T}^{w_{0}^{e-1}F}_{2}\cong C_{a}\times C_{a}
with
a=\Phi_{e}(q)_{2}
.

Remark 4.11
.

Assume that
H
is a
2
-group with normal subgroup
A=C_{a}\times C_{a}
where
4\mid a
and
H/A=\langle w_{1},w_{2}\rangle
where
w_{1},w_{2}\in H/A
are two commuting involutions such that
w_{1}
acts by inversion on
A
and
w_{2}
acts on
A
by permuting the two copies of
C_{a}
. Note that
[H,H]\leq A
as
H/A
is abelian. From the explicit action of
H/A
on
A
we can deduce that
[H,H]
has index
2
in
A
. Furthermore, we see that no non-trivial element of
H/A
centralizes
[H,H]
. From this it follows that
\operatorname{C}_{H}([H,H])=A
and thus
A
is a characteristic subgroup of
H
.

5.
Sylow
2
-subgroups of
G^{\circ}(s)

We want to apply the results of the previous section to the Sylow
2
-subgroups of
G^{\circ}(s)
. For this we need the following lemma:

Lemma 5.1
.

Let
\mathbf{H}
be a semisimple algebraic group with Frobenius endomorphism
F:\mathbf{H}\to\mathbf{H}
and
\mathbf{H}_{1},\mathbf{H}_{2}
be two
F
-stable components of
\mathbf{H}
such that
\mathbf{H}=\mathbf{H}_{1}\mathbf{H}_{2}
. Assume that
\ell\nmid|\mathrm{Z}(\mathbf{H}_{1})^{F}|
. Then the following holds:

(a)

\ell\nmid|\mathbf{H}^{F}:\mathbf{H}_{1}^{F}\mathbf{H}_{2}^{F}|
.

(b)

The kernel of the natural map
\mathbf{H}_{1}^{F}\times\mathbf{H}_{2}^{F}\to\mathbf{H}_{1}^{F}\mathbf{H}_{2}^{F}
is an
\ell^{\prime}
-subgroup.

In particular, the Sylow
\ell
-subgroups of
\mathbf{H}_{1}^{F}\times\mathbf{H}_{2}^{F}
and
\mathbf{H}^{F}
are isomorphic.

Proof.

By Lang’s theorem and the isomorphism theorem, we have
(\mathbf{H}_{1}\mathbf{H}_{2})^{F}/\mathbf{H}_{2}^{F}\cong(\mathbf{H}_{1}/\mathbf{H}_{1}\cap\mathbf{H}_{2})^{F}
. Since
