(\mathbf{H}_{1}\cap\mathbf{H}_{2})^{F}\leq\operatorname{Z}(\mathbf{H}_{1})^{F}
is a finite central
\ell^{\prime}
-subgroup, the group
\mathbf{H}_{1}^{F}/(\mathbf{H}_{1}\cap\mathbf{H}_{2})^{F}
is isomorphic to a normal subgroup of
\ell^{\prime}
-index of
(\mathbf{H}_{1}/\mathbf{H}_{1}\cap\mathbf{H}_{2})^{F}
by
[
CE04
, Theorem 8.1(a)]
. The lemma now follows from these observations.
∎

Recall the map
\pi:\mathbf{G}\to\mathbf{G}^{\ast}
from
2.2
. Let
b=b_{G}(\mathbf{L},\lambda)
be an isolated maximal block associated to a semisimple quasi-isolated element
s\in G^{\ast}
of odd order. Let
\mathbf{L}(s):=\pi^{-1}(\operatorname{C}_{\mathbf{L}^{\ast}}(s))
and
\mathbf{L}^{\circ}(s):=\mathbf{L}(s)^{\circ}
be the
F
-stable maximal torus of
\mathbf{G}
in duality with the torus
\operatorname{C}_{\mathbf{L}^{\ast}}^{\circ}(s)
.

Lemma 5.2
.

With the notation from above, we have
\operatorname{C}_{\mathbf{G}}(L(s)_{2})=\mathbf{L}^{\circ}(s)
, unless
b
is one of the blocks in case (2) or (8) of Table
1
.

Proof.

We have
\operatorname{Z}(\mathbf{L}^{\ast})\subset\mathbf{L}^{\circ}(s)^{\ast}
since
\operatorname{Z}(\mathbf{L}^{\ast})
centralizes
s
and so

\mathbf{L}^{\circ}(s)_{2}^{F}\geq\operatorname{Z}(\mathbf{L})_{2}^{F}
. Hence,
\mathbf{L}=\operatorname{C}_{\mathbf{G}}(\operatorname{Z}(\mathbf{L})_{2}^{F})\geq\operatorname{C}_{\mathbf{G}}(\mathbf{L}^{\circ}(s)_{2}^{F})
and so
\operatorname{C}_{\mathbf{L}}(\mathbf{L}^{\circ}(s)_{2}^{F})=\operatorname{C}_{\mathbf{G}}(\mathbf{L}^{\circ}(s)_{2}^{F})
. By an inspection of
[
KM13
]
the Levi subgroup
\mathbf{L}
has only components of type
A
and thus
2
is good for
\mathbf{L}
.

We claim that
2\nmid|\operatorname{Z}(\mathbf{L}):\operatorname{Z}(\mathbf{L})^{\circ}|
. If
G
is not of type
E_{7}(q)
this follows from
[
CE04
, Proposition 13.12]
. For
G=E_{7}(q)
, we go through the cases in
[
KM13
, Table 4]
. In case (1) in
\mathbf{L}
is a maximal torus, so there is nothing to check and in case (2) we note that
\mathbf{L}
is a Levi subgroup of type
A_{1}(q)^{3}\Phi_{1}^{4}
(or its Ennola dual) whose relative Weyl group is of type
C_{3}\times A_{1}
. This Levi subgroup corresponds to the simple roots
\{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{5}\}
(in Bourbaki labeling). From the data given on the Homepage of Lübeck
[
Lüb
]
we conclude that the center of
\mathbf{L}
is connected.

Since we are not in case (2) or (8) of Table
1
, it follows by an inspection of the tables in
[
KM13
]
that
\mathbf{L}^{\circ}(s)
is an
E_{q,\ell}
-torus of
(\mathbf{L},F)
, where
E_{q,2}=\{d:2\mid\Phi_{d}(q)\}
. By
[
CE04
, Lemma 13.17(a)]
we therefore deduce that
\operatorname{C}^{\circ}_{\mathbf{L}}(\mathbf{L}^{\circ}(s)_{2}^{F})=\operatorname{C}^{\circ}_{\mathbf{L}}(\mathbf{L}^{\circ}(s))=\mathbf{L}^{\circ}(s)
, where the last equality follows from the fact that
\mathbf{L}^{\circ}(s)
is a maximal torus of
\mathbf{L}
. By
[
CE04
, Proposition 13.16]
we deduce that
\operatorname{C}_{\mathbf{L}}(L(s)_{2})=\mathbf{L}^{\circ}(s)
since
2\nmid|\operatorname{Z}(\mathbf{L}^{\ast}):\operatorname{Z}(\mathbf{L}^{\ast})^{\circ}|
by
[
CE04
, Proposition 13.12]
.
∎

Remark 5.3
.

Note that in case (2) of Table
1
we have
L(s)_{2}=\operatorname{Z}(L)_{2}
and so
\operatorname{C}_{G}(L(s)_{2})=L
by
[
KM13
, Lemma 4.2]
.

According to
[
Mal07
, Proposition 5.20]
there exists a Sylow
2
-subgroup
{P^{\prime}}
of
G(s)
with
{P^{\prime}}\leq\mathrm{N}_{G^{\circ}(s)}(\mathbf{L}^{\circ}(s))
. In particular,
{D^{\prime}}:=L(s)_{2}
is a normal subgroup of
{P^{\prime}}

Lemma 5.4
.

Suppose that we are in the situation of Lemma
5.2
. Then the Sylow
2
-subgroup
P^{\prime}
of
G^{\circ}(s)
is Cabanes with Cabanes subgroup
D^{\prime}=L(s)_{2}
.

Proof.

We consider a simply connected covering map
\pi:\mathbf{G}^{\circ}(s)_{\operatorname{sc}}\to\mathbf{G}^{\circ}(s)
and we lift the Frobenius endomorphism
F
to a Frobenius endomorphism on
\mathbf{G}^{\circ}(s)_{\operatorname{sc}}
(also denoted by
F
) such that
\pi
is
F
-equivariant. Let
\mathbf{H}_{\operatorname{sc}}
be a minimal
F
-stable component of
\mathbf{G}^{\circ}(s)
and denote by
\mathbf{H}
its image in
\mathbf{G}^{\circ}(s)
under
\pi
. An inspection of the corresponding tables in
[
KM13
]
shows that
\operatorname{Z}(\mathbf{H}_{\operatorname{sc}})
is always a
2^{\prime}
-group
except when
\mathbf{G}
is of type
E_{7}
and
\operatorname{C}_{\mathbf{G}^{\ast}}(s)
is of type
A_{5}A_{2}
, so that exactly one component of the centralizer has a center with nontrival
2
-part. We can therefore apply Lemma
5.1
which shows that it’s enough to prove that the Sylow
2
-subgroups of
\mathbf{H}^{F}
are Cabanes. As the kernel of the natural isogeny
\mathbf{H}_{\operatorname{sc}}\to\mathbf{H}
is always a central subgroup of
2^{\prime}
-order it follows that the Sylow
2
-subgroups of
\mathbf{H}^{F}
and
\mathbf{H}_{\operatorname{sc}}^{F}
are isomorphic and the latter are Cabanes by Theorem
4.10
. It thus follows that the Sylow
2
-subgroups of
G^{\circ}(s)
are Cabanes.

We must finally show that
L(s)_{2}
is the Cabanes subgroup of
P^{\prime}
. However, by Lemma
5.2
we find that
\operatorname{C}_{P^{\prime}}(L(s)_{2})=L(s)_{2}
. Therefore,
L(s)_{2}
is a maximal normal abelian subgroup of
P^{\prime}
and consequently the Cabanes subgroup of
P^{\prime}
.
∎

6.
Local block theory

Our aim now is to discuss the local situation. Let
(\mathbf{L},\lambda)
be the quasi-central
e
-cuspidal pair associated to a quasi-isolated maximal block
b
of
G
.
By the proof of
[
KM13
, Theorem 7.12]
we have
L=C_{G}(\operatorname{Z}(L)_{2})
and
(\operatorname{Z}(L)_{2},b_{L}(\lambda))
is a
b
-Brauer pair. Moreover,
(\operatorname{Z}(L)_{2},b_{L}(\lambda))
is contained in a maximal
b
-Brauer pair
(P,b_{P})
, where
P\leq\mathrm{N}_{G}(\mathbf{L},\lambda)
has a normal series

\operatorname{Z}(L)_{2}\lhd D:=C_{P}(\operatorname{Z}(L)_{2})\lhd P.

The aim of this section is to give an alternative interpretation of the subgroup
D
and show that
D
is (in most cases) the maximal abelian normal subgroup of
P
.

By
[
KM13
, Theorem 1.2]
, the quotient group
P/D
is isomorphic to a Sylow
2
-subgroup of
W_{G}(\mathbf{L},\lambda)
. Moreover,
D/\operatorname{Z}(L)_{2}
is isomorphic to a Sylow
2
-subgroup of
\mathbf{L}^{F}/\operatorname{Z}(\mathbf{L})^{F}[\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L}]^{F}
. As observed in
[
KM13
, Proposition 2.7(g)]
this implies that
D=\operatorname{Z}(L)_{2}
whenever
\operatorname{Z}^{\circ}(\mathbf{L})^{F}\cap[\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L}]^{F}
is a
2^{\prime}
-group.

The following proposition gives (in most cases) a different interpretation of the defect group
P
.

Proposition 6.1
.

Let
b
be a maximal quasi-isolated block of
G
. Suppose that
b
is not one of the blocks associated to a semisimple element with centralizer
A_{2}(\pm q^{3}).3
or
{}^{3}D_{4}(q)
in
E_{6}(\pm q)
.
Then there exists a Sylow
2
-subgroup
P^{\prime}
of
G(s)
such that the following holds:
P^{\prime}
is a defect group of
b
and for a maximal
b
-Brauer pair
(P^{\prime},b_{P^{\prime}})
we have
\operatorname{N}_{G}(P^{\prime},b_{P^{\prime}})=\operatorname{N}_{G(s)}(P^{\prime})
.

Proof.

Note that if
s
is quasi-isolated but not isolated then
\operatorname{C}^{\circ}_{\mathbf{G}^{\ast}}(s)
is a Levi subgroup of
\mathbf{G}^{\ast}
. Hence, in this case the result follows easily from the main theorem of
[
BDR17
]
. We may therefore assume that
s
is isolated.

Denote
{D^{\prime}}:=L(s)_{2}
and note that
A_{\mathbf{L}^{\ast}}(s)^{F}=1
in all considered cases so that
\mathbf{L}(s)
is a maximal torus of
\mathbf{L}
. We therefore have a Bonnafé–Rouquier Morita equivalence, see
[
BDR17
]
, between
\Lambda\mathbf{L}^{F}e_{s}^{\mathbf{L}^{F}}
and
\Lambda\mathbf{L}(s)^{F}e_{s}^{\mathbf{L}(s)^{F}}
. By Lemma
5.2
we have

\operatorname{C}_{\mathbf{L}^{F}}({D^{\prime}})=\operatorname{C}_{\mathbf{L}(s)^{F}}({D^{\prime}})=\mathbf{L}(s)^{F}.

Let
\hat{s}\in\operatorname{Irr}(L(s))
be the linear character associated to
s
by duality and denote
b_{D^{\prime}}:=b_{L(s)}(\hat{s})
.
We deduce by the description of the Brauer morphism in Lemma
1.11

that the subpair
({D^{\prime}},b_{D^{\prime}})
is a Brauer pair of the block

b_{L}(\lambda)
. On the other hand, the pair
(\operatorname{Z}(L)_{2},b_{L}(\lambda))
is a
b
-Brauer pair, see the proof of
[
KM13
, Theorem 7.12(d)]
. Hence, by transitivity of Brauer pairs we obtain that
({D^{\prime}},b_{D^{\prime}})
is a
b
-Brauer pair. Note that
\operatorname{Z}({D^{\prime}})={D^{\prime}}
is a defect group of the block
b_{D^{\prime}}
. Hence,
({D^{\prime}},b_{D^{\prime}})
is a self-centralizing (or centric)
b
-subpair with canonical character
\hat{s}
, see
[
CE04
, Definition 22.12]
.
Recall from Remark
2.1
and Remark
1.8
that
\mathbf{L}^{\circ}(s)
is the centralizer of a Sylow
e
-torus of
\mathbf{G}^{\circ}(s)
. The canonical character
\hat{s}
of the block
b_{L(s)}(\lambda)
is
{P^{\prime}}
-stable, as
{P^{\prime}}\leq G(s)
. Let
b_{P^{\prime}}
be the unique block of
\operatorname{C}_{G}({P^{\prime}})\leq\operatorname{C}_{G}({D^{\prime}})=L(s)
below
b_{D^{\prime}}
. From
[
CE04
, Proposition 22.14, Remark 22.15]
we deduce that
({P^{\prime}},b_{P^{\prime}})
is again a centric
b
-Brauer pair. By construction
|{P^{\prime}}|=|G(s)|_{2}=|\operatorname{C}_{G^{\ast}}(s)|_{2}
and so
({P^{\prime}},b_{P^{\prime}})
is a maximal
b
-Brauer pair.

By Proposition
1.6
(b) the stabilizer of the linear character
\hat{s}\in\operatorname{Irr}(L(s))
in
\operatorname{N}_{G}(\mathbf{L}(s))
is
\operatorname{N}_{G(s)}(\mathbf{L}(s))
.
If
x\in\operatorname{N}_{G}({P^{\prime}},b_{P^{\prime}})
then by Lemma
5.4
it follows that
x\in\operatorname{N}_{G}({D^{\prime}},b_{D^{\prime}})
and thus
x
must stabilize the canonical character
\hat{s}
of the block
b_{D^{\prime}}
. We deduce that
x\in\operatorname{N}_{G(s)}({P^{\prime}})
. Conversely, any
x\in\operatorname{N}_{G(s)}({P^{\prime}})
stabilizes
\hat{s}
and therefore also its restriction to
\operatorname{C}_{G}({P^{\prime}})
. Therefore,
\operatorname{N}_{G(s)}({P^{\prime}})=\operatorname{N}_{G}({P^{\prime}},b_{P^{\prime}})
as claimed.
∎

Lemma 6.2
.

The pair
(D^{\prime},P^{\prime})
constructed in the previous proposition is
G
-conjugate to
(D,P)
.

Proof.

Note that
\operatorname{Z}(\mathbf{L})^{\circ}_{\Phi_{e}}=\mathbf{L}^{\circ}(s)_{\Phi_{e}}
by Remark
1.9
. Thus,

P^{\prime}\leq\operatorname{N}_{G^{\circ}(s)}(\mathbf{L}^{\circ}(s))\leq\operatorname{N}_{G^{\circ}(s)}(\mathbf{L})

as
\mathbf{L}
is an
e
-split Levi subgroup of
\mathbf{G}
and so
\mathbf{L}=\operatorname{C}_{\mathbf{G}}(\operatorname{Z}(\mathbf{L})^{\circ}_{\Phi_{e}})
. Moreover, note that
\lambda\in\mathcal{E}(L,s)
corresponds under Jordan decomposition to the regular-semisimple character of
\mathcal{E}(\operatorname{C}_{\mathbf{L}^{\ast}}(s),1)
. It follows that
\mathrm{N}_{G^{\circ}(s)}(\mathbf{L})\leq\mathrm{N}_{G}(\mathbf{L},\lambda)
. Consequently, the group
P^{\prime}
constructed in the previous proposition can be chosen to coincide with the defect group
P
constructed in
[
KM13
]
. Hence, we can assume that
P=P^{\prime}
.

Furthermore, by
[
KM13
, Proposition 2.7(d)]
we have
P\cap L=D
. Therefore,
D^{\prime}=L(s)_{2}\leq D
. On the other hand,
D^{\prime}=L(s)_{2}
is by Lemma
5.4
the maximal normal abelian subgroup of
P
and thus
D=D^{\prime}
.
∎

Remark 6.3
.

Let
c
be the block of
G(s)
corresponding to
b
under the Jordan decomposition
\mathcal{J}
from Lemma
2.3
. Then
\mathcal{J}
induces a bijection
\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(G(s),c)\to\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(G,b)
. We observe that the block
b_{D}=b_{L(s)}(\hat{s})
is a
c
-subpair. Let
B_{D}
be the unique block of
\operatorname{N}_{G}(D)
covering
b_{D}
. Then induction defines a bijection
\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(\operatorname{N}_{G}(D,b_{D}),b_{D})\to\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(\operatorname{N}_{G}(D),B_{D})
.
Since the block
c
has maximal defect in
G(s)
and
\operatorname{N}_{G(s)}(P)\leq\operatorname{N}_{G(s)}(D)
it follows by the main theorem of
[
BR21
]
that
|\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(G(s),c)|=|\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(\operatorname{N}_{G(s)}(D),b_{D})|
. Hence,
|\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(\operatorname{N}_{G}(D),B_{D})|=|\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(G,b)|
.

6.1.
The remaining cases

We give an analysis of the defect group in the cases which were left out in Proposition
6.1
. We need the following easy group theoretic lemma.

Lemma 6.4
.

Let
r
be a natural number and
P
a finite group with normal subgroup
A^{r}
of index
r
such that some
x\in P\setminus A^{r}
acts by transitively permuting the
r
copies of
A^{r}
. Then
P\cong A\wr C_{r}
.

Proof.

Consider the map
f:A\to A^{r}
which embedds
A
into the first component of
A^{r}
and let
\pi\in C_{r}
be the permutation induced by
x
on
A^{r}
. Since
x
acts transitively on the
r
components of
A
, we have
\operatorname{Z}(P)=\operatorname{C}_{A^{r}}(x)=\{f(a)\,{}^{x}f(a)\cdots{}^{x^{r-1}}f(a)\mid a\in A\}
. As
x^{r}\in\operatorname{C}_{A^{r}}(x)
, we thus have
x^{r}=f(a)\,{}^{x}f(a)\cdots{}^{x^{r-1}}f(a)
for some
a
. Define
y:=f(a)^{-1}x
. Then
y^{r}=f(a^{-1})\,{}^{x}f(a^{-1})\cdots{}^{x^{r-1}}f(a^{-1})x^{r}=1
. It is then easily checked that there exists a unique isomorphism
g:A\wr C_{r}\to P
with
g(a_{1},\ddots,a_{r})=f(a_{1}){}^{x}f(a_{2})\cdots{}^{x^{r-1}}f(a_{r})
and
g(\pi)=g(y)
.
∎

Lemma 6.5
.

Suppose that
b
is a maximal block of
G
associated to a semisimple element
s
, which was excluded in Proposition
6.1
. Then
D^{\prime}=L(s)_{2}
is a characteristic subgroup of a defect group
P^{\prime}
of
b
. Moreover, the subgroup
\operatorname{Z}(L)_{2}
is a characteristic subgroup of
P^{\prime}
.

Proof.

We shall first assume that
b
is quasi-isolated, which is not isolated. According to the proof of Proposition
6.1
and the main theorem of
[
BDR17
]
we can assume that
P^{\prime}
is a Sylow
2
-subgroup of
G(s)
with normal subgroup
D^{\prime}=L(s)_{2}
. Also observe that
L(s)_{2}=\operatorname{Z}(L)_{2}
by Table
1
. Moreover the information given there also shows that
P^{\prime}
is isomorphic to a Sylow
2
-subgroup of
[G^{\circ}(s),G^{\circ}(s)]\cong{}^{3}D_{4}(q)_{\mathrm{sc}}
. The claim follows therefore from Remark
4.11
.

We can therefore assume that
b
is an isolated block.
We first show that there exists indeed a defect group of the block
b
containing
L(s)_{2}
. According to the main theorem of
[
BDR17
]
there is a splendid Rickard equivalence between
\mathcal{O}Le_{s}^{L}
and
\mathcal{O}L(s)e_{s}^{L^{\circ}(s)}
. Here,
L(s)/L^{\circ}(s)
is isomorphic by duality to
\operatorname{C}_{L^{\ast}}(s)/\operatorname{C}^{\circ}_{L^{\ast}}(s)
. As there are exactly
3
blocks of
L(s)
covering the principal block of
L^{\circ}(s)
by Lemma
2.4
, we have by the main theorem of
[
Dad77
]
a splendid Rickard equivalence between
\mathcal{O}L^{\circ}(s)e_{s}^{L^{\circ}(s)}
and
\mathcal{O}Lb_{L}(\lambda)
. As
D^{\prime}=L(s)_{2}
is a defect group of the block
e_{s}^{L^{\circ}(s)}
this yields by the main theorem of
[
BDR17
]
a local Rickard equivalence between
\mathcal{O}L^{\circ}(s)e_{s}^{L^{\circ}(s)}
and
\mathcal{O}\operatorname{C}_{L}(D^{\prime})b_{D^{\prime}}
given by Deligne–Lusztig induction followed by multiplication with the idempotent
b_{D^{\prime}}
. It follows that
(D^{\prime},b_{D^{\prime}})
is a
b_{L}(\lambda)
-subpair whose canonical character
\lambda_{0}
is the unique irreducible constituent of
R_{L^{\circ}(s)}^{\operatorname{C}_{L}(D^{\prime})}(\hat{s})
which lies in
b_{D^{\prime}}
. By transitivity of the Brauer subpair inclusion we deduce that
(D^{\prime},b_{D^{\prime}})
is a
b
-Brauer pair. Therefore, there exists a maximal
b
-Brauer pair
(P^{\prime},b_{P^{\prime}})
containing it. As
(D^{\prime},b_{D^{\prime}})
is a maximal
b_{L}(\lambda)
-Brauer pair it follows that
(D^{\prime},b_{D^{\prime}})
is maximal among the
b
-subpairs with
D^{\prime}\leq\operatorname{C}_{G}(\operatorname{Z}(L)_{\ell})=L
. Therefore,
(D^{\prime},b_{D^{\prime}})
is a
b
-subpair as in
[
KM13
, Proposition 2.7]
. We can therefore assume that
(D^{\prime},P^{\prime})=(D,P)
. It follows from
[
KM13
, Proposition 2.7]
that
P/D
is isomorphic to a Sylow
2
-subgroup of
W_{G}(L,\lambda)/L
. This allows us to describe the action of
P
on
Z(L)_{2}
since
L=\operatorname{C}_{G}(Z(L)_{2})
.

In case (2) we have
D=\operatorname{Z}(L)_{2}\cong A\times A
such that any element
\sigma\in P\setminus D
acts by permuting these two components. Therefore, by Lemma
6.4
,
D\cong C_{(q-1)_{2}}\wr C_{2}
which is Cabanes. In case (8) we have that
\mathbf{L}^{\circ}(s)^{F}\cong\mathbb{F}_{q^{6}}^{\times}
is cyclic. We argue now as in case (6) of
[
KM13
, Proposition 6.4]
. As in this proof we see that
D\cong C_{(q+1)_{2}2}
and any
\sigma\in P\setminus D
acts on the cyclic subgroup
\operatorname{Z}(L)_{2}
of order
(q+1)_{2}
by inversion. From this we can conclude as in
[
KM13
, Proposition 6.4]
that
\operatorname{Z}(L)_{2}
and
D
are characteristic subgroups of
P
.
∎

We will see later in
12.4
, that
P^{\prime}
is indeed conjugate to a Sylow
2
-subgroup of
G(s)
. However we require explicit calculations to obtain this.

6.2.
Suitable subgroups for the iAM-condition

The following lemma will allow us to do calculations with the normalizer
\operatorname{N}_{G}(\mathbf{L})
instead of the less accessible subgroup
\operatorname{N}_{G}(P)
.

Lemma 6.6
.

Let
b
be a maximal block of
G
with associated
e
-cuspidal pair
(L,\lambda)
.

(a)

The subgroup
\operatorname{N}_{G}(\mathbf{L})
is a proper
\mathrm{Aut}(G)_{P,b}
-stable subgroup of
G
and
\operatorname{N}_{G}(\mathbf{L})
contains
\operatorname{N}_{G}(P)
.

(b)

There exists a unique block
B
of
\operatorname{N}_{G}(\mathbf{L})
covering the block
b_{L}(\lambda)
of
L
and
B^{G}=b
.

(c)

Let
\tilde{\lambda}
be a character of
\tilde{L}
covering
\lambda
. Then there exists a block
\tilde{b}
of
\tilde{L}
such that
(\operatorname{Z}(\tilde{L})_{2},b_{\tilde{L}}(\tilde{\lambda}))
is a
\tilde{b}
-Brauer pair and a unique block
\tilde{B}
of
\operatorname{N}_{\tilde{G}}(\tilde{\mathbf{L}})
covering the block
b_{\tilde{L}}(\tilde{\lambda})
of
\tilde{L}
and
\tilde{B}^{G}=\tilde{b}
.

Proof.

Let
\sigma:\mathbf{G}\to\mathbf{G}
be a bijective morphism commuting with
F
which stabilizes both
P
and
b
. In the situation of Lemma
6.5
,
\operatorname{Z}(L)_{2}
is characteristic in
P
and so
\sigma
stabilizes
\operatorname{Z}(L)_{2}
and hence also its centralizer
L=\operatorname{C}_{G}(\operatorname{Z}(L)_{2})
.

Assume now that we are in the situation of Proposition
6.1
. Then
\sigma
stabilizes the characteristic subgroup
D=L(s)_{2}
of
P
and also its centralizer
\mathbf{L}^{\circ}(s)=\operatorname{C}_{\mathbf{G}}(D)
, see Lemma
5.2
. As
\operatorname{Z}(\mathbf{L})^{\circ}_{\Phi_{e}}=\mathbf{L}^{\circ}(s)_{\Phi_{e}}
it follows that
\sigma
also stabilizes
\mathbf{L}=\operatorname{C}_{\mathbf{G}}(\operatorname{Z}(\mathbf{L})^{\circ}_{\Phi_{e}})
. This shows that
\operatorname{N}_{G}(\mathbf{L})
is an
\mathrm{Aut}(G)_{P,b}
-stable subgroup of
G
.
Moreover, the equality
\operatorname{N}_{G}(\mathbf{L})=G
would imply
L=G
, as
G
has no non-central proper normal subgroups. By
[
KM13
, Theorem 1.2]
this would mean that
P\leq\operatorname{Z}(G)
which is not the case. Thus
\operatorname{N}_{G}(\mathbf{L})
is a proper subgroup of
G
.

For part (b) observe that any defect group of the block
b_{L}(\lambda)
contains
\operatorname{Z}(L)_{2}
, see
[
KM13
, Proposition 2.5(e)]
. Since
L=\operatorname{C}_{G}(\operatorname{Z}(L)_{2})
it follows from
[
Nav98
, Theorem 9.19,9.20]
that there exists a unique block of
\operatorname{N}_{G}(\mathbf{L})
covering
b_{L}(\lambda)
. Since
(\operatorname{Z}(L)_{2},b_{L}(\lambda))
is a
b
-Brauer pair it follows that
B^{G}=b
.

From the proof of part (a) of
[
KM13
, Lemma 7.11]
we deduce that there exists a block
\tilde{b}
of
\tilde{G}
covering
b
such that
(\operatorname{Z}(\tilde{L})_{2},b_{\tilde{L}}(\tilde{\lambda}))
is a
\tilde{b}
-Brauer pair. Moreover, the proof of said lemma shows that
\operatorname{C}_{\tilde{G}}(\operatorname{Z}(\tilde{L})_{2})=\operatorname{C}_{\tilde{G}}(\operatorname{Z}(L)_{2})=\tilde{L}
. The last claim of part (c) follows therefore with the same arguments as in part (b).
∎

7.
Analysis of non-maximal blocks

The non-maximal blocks for quasi-isolated elements have some similarities with maximal blocks but they also require some additional arguments. Let
b
be a quasi-isolated non-maximal block of
G
. Recall that by the results of
[
KM13
]
and
[
Eng00
]
there exists some
e
-cuspidal pair
(\mathbf{L},\lambda)
with
\lambda
of quasi-central defect defining these blocks.
The following lemma describes all possible situations:

Lemma 7.1
.

There are non-maximal quasi-isolated blocks
b=b_{G}(\mathbf{L},\lambda)
of non-central defect in the following cases for
e=1
.

(i)

G=E_{7}
and
s=1
: Case (3) in
[
Eng00
, p. 354]
;
L=E_{6}\Phi_{1}
and
W_{G}(\mathbf{L})=W_{G}(\mathbf{L},\lambda)\cong C_{2}

(ii)

G=E_{8}
and
s=1
: Case (3) in
[
Eng00
, Table I]
;
L=E_{6}\Phi_{1}^{2}
and
W_{G}(\mathbf{L})=W_{G}(\mathbf{L},\lambda)\cong S_{3}\times C_{2}

(iii)

G=E_{8}
: Case (4)
[
KM13
, Table 5]
:
L=E_{6}\Phi_{1}^{2}
and
W_{G}(\mathbf{L},\lambda)\cong S_{3}
;
Case (6)
[
KM13
, Table 5]
:
L=E_{7}\Phi_{1}
and
W_{G}(\mathbf{L},\lambda)\cong C_{2}
.

The cases for
e=2
are obtained by Ennola duality.

Note that in the cases of Lemma
7.1
the character
\lambda\in\operatorname{Irr}(L)
is always of central defect. In the unipotent cases this is immediate from the parametrization of
[
Eng00
]
and in the non-unipotent case this follows by an inspection of the relevant tables in
[
KM13
]
together with
[
KM13
, Proposition 2.5(g)]
.

We collect some more common properties in the following lemma:

Lemma 7.2
.

Let
b=b_{G}(\mathbf{L},\lambda)
be a non-maximal quasi-isolated block as in Lemma
7.1
. Let
P
be a defect group of
b
which contains
\operatorname{Z}(L)_{2}
.

(a)

We have
\mathbf{L}=\operatorname{C}_{\mathbf{G}}(\operatorname{Z}(L)_{2})
and
\mathbf{L}(s)=\operatorname{C}_{\mathbf{G}}(\operatorname{Z}(L(s))_{2})
.

(b)

The subgroup
D:=\operatorname{Z}(L(s))_{2}
is a characteristic subgroup of
P
with Brauer pair
(D,b_{D})
with
b_{D}:=b_{L(s)}(\mathcal{J}_{L}(\lambda))
. Moreover,
\operatorname{N}_{G}(D,b_{D})=\operatorname{N}_{G(s)}(D)
.

Proof.

The first statement of part (a) follows from
[
KM13
, Theorem 7.12]
, while the second part follows from an inspection of
[
KM13
, Table 5]
.

We now show part (b) for the unipotent blocks.
Observe that
(\operatorname{Z}(L)_{2},b_{L}(\lambda))
is a
b
-Brauer pair. Moreover, an inspection of
[
KM13
, Table 5]
shows that
\mathbf{L}(s)
is a Levi subgroup of
\mathbf{L}
. Hence, by Bonnafé–Rouquier, we have a Morita equivalence between
\mathcal{O}Le_{s}^{L}
and
\mathcal{O}L(s)e_{s}^{L(s)}
given by Deligne–Lusztig induction. In particular, the block
b_{L(s)}(\mathcal{J}_{L}(\lambda))
corresponds to
b_{L}(\lambda)
under this Morita equivalence. Since
\mathbf{L}(s)=\operatorname{C}_{\mathbf{G}}(D)
, it follows by the main result of
[
BDR17
]
that we have a local Rickard equivalence between
\mathcal{O}L(s)b_{L(s)}(\mathcal{J}_{L}(\lambda))
and
\mathcal{O}L(s)b_{D}
, where
(D,b_{D})
is a
b_{L}(\lambda)
-subpair, which is induced by the Deligne–Lusztig induction functor from
L(s)
to
L(s)
. Since the latter functor is clearly the identity, it follows
b_{D}=b_{L(s)}(\mathcal{J}_{L}(\lambda))
, as expected. Moreover, the group
D
is a characteristic subgroup of
P
by the proof of
[
KM13
, Proposition 6.4]
. The normalizer property is clear from
[
KM13
, Table 5]
.

For the unipotent blocks observe that
(\operatorname{Z}(L)_{2},b_{L}(\lambda))
is a
b
-Brauer pair by the proof of
[
KM13
, Theorem 7.12]
and the normalizer statement is contained in Lemma
7.1
. We therefore only need to show that
\operatorname{Z}(L)_{2}
is a characteristic subgroup of
P
. For this we use the description of defect groups from
[
Eng00
, Section 3.2]
. We go through the different cases in Lemma
7.1
. In case (i) the defect groups are dihedral by
[
Eng00
, Section 3.2]
and thus the cyclic group
\operatorname{Z}(L)_{2}
of index
2
in
P
is characteristic. In case (ii)
recall that
P\leq\operatorname{N}_{G}(\mathbf{L},\lambda)
and
P/\operatorname{Z}(L)_{2}
is isomorphic to a Sylow
2
-subgroup of
W_{G}(\mathbf{L},\lambda)
. This subgroup is generated by two involutions
w_{1},w_{2}
such that
w_{1}
acts by inversion and
w_{2}
by permuting the two components of
\operatorname{Z}(L)=\Phi_{1}^{2}
. Hence, the claim follows in this case from Remark
4.11
.
∎

Let
\mathbf{H}
be a connected reductive group with Frobenius endomorphism
F:\mathbf{H}\to\mathbf{H}
defining an
\mathbb{F}_{q}
-structure.
For
d\in\{1,2\}
observe that by
[
KM15
, Lemma 3.17]
the centralizer
\mathbf{S}
of a Sylow
d
-torus of
(\mathbf{H},F)
is a maximal torus of
\mathbf{H}
. We say that a character in
\operatorname{Irr}(H)
lies in the principal
d
-Harish-Chandra series if it is a constituent of
R_{\mathbf{S}}^{\mathbf{H}}(\lambda)
for some
\lambda\in\operatorname{Irr}(\mathbf{S}^{F})
.

Lemma 7.3
.

Let
\mathbf{H}
be a connected reductive group with connected
\operatorname{Z}(\mathbf{H})
and all whose simple components are of type
A
and
F:\mathbf{H}\to\mathbf{H}
a Frobenius endomorphism defining an
\mathbb{F}_{q}
-structure. Then every
2^{\prime}
-character of
\mathbf{H}^{F}
lies in the principal
1
- and
2
-Harish-Chandra series.

Proof.

Let
d\in\{1,2\}
. We have
\chi\in\mathcal{E}(H,s)
for some semisimple element
s\in H^{\ast}
such that
\chi(1)=|H^{\ast}:\operatorname{C}_{H^{\ast}}(s)|_{p^{\prime}}J_{H,s}(\chi)(1)
. Our assumption implies by
[
CE04
, Proposition 13.16]
that
\operatorname{C}_{\mathbf{H}^{\ast}}(s)
is a Levi subgroup of
\mathbf{H}^{\ast}
. In particular, the polynomial order of
\operatorname{C}_{\mathbf{H}^{\ast}}(s)
divides the polyomial order of
\mathbf{H}^{\ast}
. Hence, in order for
\operatorname{C}_{H^{\ast}}(s)
to contain a Sylow
2
-subgroup of
\mathbf{H}^{\ast}
, the group
\operatorname{C}_{\mathbf{H}^{\ast}}(s)
must contain a Sylow
d
-torus of
\mathbf{H}^{\ast}
. Moreover, by the order formula of
[
GM20
, Corollary 4.6.25]
, the character
J_{H,s}(\chi)
must necessarily lie in the principal
d
-Harish-Chandra series since
\Phi_{d}(q)
would divide
J_{H,s}(\chi)(1)
otherwise. It follows from Theorem
1.1
(1) that
\chi
must lie in the principal
d
-Harish-Chandra series as well.
∎

Lemma 7.4
.

For every non-maximal block
b
of
G
with defect group
P
occuring in Lemma
7.1
we have
|\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(b)|=|P:P^{\prime}|
.

Proof.

We go through the cases of Lemma
7.1
. Since the defect groups in case (i) are dihedral, this equality is well-known, see
[
Sam14
, Theorem 8.1]
.
Suppose therefore now that we are in case (ii), i.e.
b
is the unipotent block of
E_{8}(q)
associated to the
e
-cuspidal character
({}^{(2)}E_{6},{}^{(2)}E_{6}[\theta])
. The description of characters in this block is given by
[
Eng08
, Theorem B]
, see also the proof of
[
Mal18
, Theorem 6.2]
. We have
\operatorname{Irr}(b)\subset\mathcal{E}_{2}(G,1)
, and for any
2
-element
t\in G^{\ast}
,
\operatorname{Irr}(b)\cap\mathcal{E}_{2}(G,t)
is in bijection with the irreducible characters in a
corresponding unipotent block of
\operatorname{C}_{G^{\ast}}(t)
, belonging to the ”same”
e
-cuspidal pair (apart
from certain exceptions as described in
[
Eng00
, Proposition 17]
). In particular, the only Lusztig series
\mathcal{E}(G,t)
, with
t
a
2
-element, that can contribute to the block
b
have centralizer
\operatorname{C}_{\mathbf{G}^{\ast}}(t)
of type

{}^{(2)}E_{6},{}^{(2)}E_{6}A_{1},E_{7},E_{7}A_{1},E_{8}

and these contribute with
|\operatorname{Irr}(b)\cap\mathcal{E}(G,t)|=1,2,2,4,6
characters. (In
[
Mal18
, Theorem 6.2]
the centralizer of type
E_{7}A_{1}
was erroneously omitted).

Let us first consider the six unipotent characters of the block
b
. There are four unipotent characters whose
2
-part is smaller than the
2
-part of the other two unipotent characters. The
2
-part of these four characters is precisely the
2
-part of
|G:P|
and these are thus of height zero.

Let’s look at the unique
2
-element
t\in G^{\ast}
(up to conjugation) whose centralizer is of type
E_{7}A_{1}
.
The
2
-part of the character degrees of the four characters in the Harish-Chandra series lying above the cuspidal character is again
|G:P|_{2}
and thus these are of height zero again.

A similar calculation shows on the other hand that characters associated with centralizers of type
E_{7}
,
E_{6}
or
E_{6}A_{1}
cannot contribute to height zero characters of
b
.

Suppose now that we are in case (iii). Here, the calculation is slightly more complicated as no description of the characters in
\mathcal{E}_{2}(G,s)
is known. Using the description of defect groups given in the proof of
[
KM13
, Proposition 6.4]
, we deduce that
P\cong C_{(q-1)_{2}}\wr C_{2}
by Lemma
6.4
in the first case and
P
is metacyclic in the second case. In particular, Olsson’s conjecture is known for these cases; i.e.
|\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(b)|\leq|P:P^{\prime}|
, see
[
Sam14
, Theorem 10.22]
respectively. We show that there are at least
|P:P^{\prime}|
characters of height zero lying in the block
b
, which then proves that these are all height zero characters.

We assume
e=1
and consider first case (4) in
[
KM13
]
. We first count the height zero characters in the unipotent block
c_{0}
associated to
b
through Lemma
2.5
. The group
G(s)
is of type
E_{6}(q)A_{2}(q)
and the block
c_{0}
is by
[
CE04
, Theorem 17.7]
isomorphic to a block of
E_{6}(q)_{\mathrm{ad}}\times A_{2}(q)_{\mathrm{ad}}
. More precisely, it is isomorphic to the tensor product of the unique unipotent block of
E_{6}(q)
of trivial defect containing the cuspidal character
E_{6}[\theta]
with the principal block of
A_{2}(q)
. Now, the principal block of
A_{2}(q)
has exactly
|P:P^{\prime}|
height zero characters in this case as can be seen easily from
[
BR21
, Lemma 3.8]
.
Recall that the bijection
\mathcal{J}
is defined as the union of the bijections
J_{G(s),st}^{-1}\circ J_{G,st}:\mathcal{E}(G,st)\to\mathcal{E}(G(s),st)
for
t\in\operatorname{C}_{G^{\ast}}(s)_{2}
. We now want to show that
\hat{s}^{-1}\mathcal{J}
induces a bijection betwen the height zero characters of
b
and its corresponding unipotent block
c_{0}=\hat{s}^{-1}c
of
G(s)
.

For this let’s first consider the unipotent characters. We observe that
L^{\ast}=\operatorname{C}_{L^{\ast}}(s)
by
[
KM13
, Table 5]
and we let
\lambda_{s}=\lambda\hat{s}^{-1}=E_{6}[\theta]\in\mathcal{E}(\operatorname{C}_{L^{\ast}}(s),1)
be the Jordan correspondent of
\lambda
. Note that every character in the principal block of
A_{2}(q)
lies in the principal Harish-Chandra series by Lemma
7.3
. Since the block
c_{0}
is the tensor product of a cuspidal character and the principal block of
A_{2}(q)
, it follows that every character in
\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(c_{0})\cap\mathcal{E}(\operatorname{C}_{G^{\ast}}(s),1)
appears as an irreducible constituent of
R_{\operatorname{C}_{\mathbf{L}^{\ast}}(s)}^{\operatorname{C}_{\mathbf{G}^{\ast}}(s)}(\lambda_{s})
. By
1.1
(5), we have
J_{G,s}R_{L}^{G}(\lambda)=R_{\operatorname{C}_{\mathbf{L}^{\ast}}(s)}^{\operatorname{C}_{\mathbf{G}^{\ast}}(s)}(J_{L,s}(\lambda))
. In other words, the Harish-Chandra series of
\lambda_{s}
is mapped via Jordan decomposition to the Harish-Chandra series of
\lambda
.

Again by the same arguments as above, the characters in
\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(c_{0})\cap\mathcal{E}(\operatorname{C}_{G^{\ast}}(s),t)
appear as irreducible constituents of
R_{\operatorname{C}_{\mathbf{L}^{\ast}}(s)}^{\operatorname{C}_{\mathbf{G}^{\ast}}(s)}(\lambda_{st})
, where
\lambda_{st}:=\lambda_{s}\hat{t}
with
t\in\operatorname{Z}(\operatorname{C}_{\mathbf{L}^{\ast}}(s))_{2}=\operatorname{Z}(L^{\ast})_{2}
.
Moreover,
\operatorname{C}_{L^{\ast}}(s)=E_{6}(q)\Phi_{1}^{2}
is contained in
\operatorname{C}_{G^{\ast}}(st)
which is a proper subgroup of
\operatorname{C}_{G^{\ast}}(s)=E_{6}(q)A_{2}(q)
. Comparing this with the list of possible centralizers in
E_{8}(q)
given in
[
Der83
, Table 1]
, we obtain that
\operatorname{C}_{\mathbf{G}^{\ast}}(st)
is necessarily a Levi subgroup of
\mathbf{G}^{\ast}
. In particular, Jordan decomposition is given by Deligne–Lusztig induction, i.e.
J_{G,st}^{-1}=R_{\operatorname{C}_{G^{\ast}}(st)}^{G^{\ast}}\widehat{st}
and
J_{L,st}(\lambda\hat{t})=\lambda_{s}
since
\operatorname{C}_{L^{\ast}}(s)=\operatorname{C}_{L^{\ast}}(st)
. From this, we deduce by transitivity of Deligne–Lusztig induction that

J_{st}^{G}R_{L}^{G}(\lambda\hat{t})=R_{\operatorname{C}_{\mathbf{L}^{\ast}}(st)}^{\operatorname{C}_{\mathbf{G}^{\ast}}(st)}(J^{L}_{st}(\lambda\hat{t}))
. Since
\operatorname{C}_{\mathbf{G}^{\ast}}(st)
is a Levi subgroup of
\operatorname{C}_{\mathbf{G}^{\ast}}(s)
, we also get
J_{G(s),st}^{-1}=R^{\operatorname{C}_{\mathbf{G}^{\ast}}(s)}_{\operatorname{C}_{\mathbf{G}^{\ast}}(st)}\widehat{st}
. We claim that the characters
R_{L}^{G}(\lambda\hat{t})
with
t\in\operatorname{Z}(L^{\ast})_{2}
all lie in the same
2
-block. For this observe that
d^{1}(R_{L}^{G}(\lambda\hat{t}))=d_{1}(R_{L}^{G}(\lambda))
, by
[
CE04
, Proposition 9.6(iii)]
. As all constituents of
R_{L}^{G}(\lambda)
lie in
b_{G}(\mathbf{L},\lambda)
the same is therefore true for the constituents of
R_{L}^{G}(\lambda\hat{t})
. This implies that
\mathcal{J}^{-1}(\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(c))\subset\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(b)
and so in particular
|P:P^{\prime}|=|\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(c_{0})|\leq|\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(b)|\leq|P:P^{\prime}|
. Hence, we must have equality everywhere and so
\mathcal{J}^{-1}(\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(c))=\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(b).

For case (6) of
[
KM13
]
we can use the same arguments as in case (4) but instead of using the Levi subgroup of case (6) we use the alternative
2
-split Levi subgroup (6b) in
[
KM13
, Table 5]
.

Again the same considerations apply by Ennola duality to the case
e=2
.
∎

8.
Equivariant Jordan decomposition for blocks with cyclic stabilizer in
\mathrm{Out}(G)

8.1.
Blocks with cyclic stabilizer

Recall that
\mathbf{G}
is a simple, simply connected algebraic group of exceptional type with Frobenius endomorphism
F:\mathbf{G}\to\mathbf{G}
. For the following lemma recall that every character
\chi\in\mathcal{E}(G,t)
for a semisimple element
t\in G^{\ast}
has the same restriction to
\operatorname{Z}(G)
, see
[
Bon06
, Equation 9.12]
. Moreover since
\operatorname{Z}(G)
is cyclic of prime order, the center
\operatorname{Z}(G)
is in the kernel of
\chi
if and only if
t\in[G^{\ast},G^{\ast}]
, see
[
MN11
, Section 3.2]
. For the splitting of the geometric classes we use
[
Lüb
]
.

Lemma 8.1
.

Let
b
be an isolated, non-unipotent
2
-block of
G
. Then
\mathrm{Out}(G)_{b}
is cyclic except possibly in the following cases:

´

(i)

Case (1) and (7) in Table
1
in type
E_{6}(q)
.

(ii)

The Ennola duals in
{}^{2}E_{6}(q)
of cases (1) and (7).

(iii)

Case (1) in
[
KM13
, Table 4]
when
G=E_{7}(q)
and
q\equiv 1\mod 12
.

Moreover, in these cases we have
\mathrm{Out}(G)_{b}=\mathrm{Out}(G).

Proof.

Let us first assume that
G=E_{6}(q)
.
Case (1) (resp. (7)) occurs when
q\equiv 1\mod 3
. Furthermore, in this case the semisimple element
s
is the unique element in its rational class with this precise centralizer. Thus, its rational conjugacy class is stable under all automorphisms and so is the unique block
b
associated to
s
.
The two rational conjugacy classes associated to case (2) (resp. (8)) (still for
q\equiv 1\mod 3
) are geometrically conjugate to the semisimple element of case (1). These elements don’t lie in
[G^{\ast},G^{\ast}]
and therefore the characters in the corresponding block are non-trivial on the center. Therefore, the graph automorphism must necessarily interchange their rational conjugacy classes. Moreover, there are three blocks associated to each of the two rational conjugacy classes. Since these blocks are conjugate under diagonal automorphisms none of them is fixed under a diagonal automorphism. In particular, no combination of a graph and diagonal automorphism stabilizes a block. Since
\mathrm{Out}(G)\cong S_{3}\times C_{a}
for some
a
we can use
[
FR21
, Lemma 2.4]
to conclude that
\mathrm{Out}(G)_{b}
is cyclic.
Case (6) (resp. (12)) occurs when
q\equiv 2\mod 3
, i.e. when
\operatorname{Z}(G)=1
. Moreover, the group of field automorphisms has odd order since
q
cannot be a square. Therefore,
\mathrm{Out}(G)
is cyclic in this case.

The case
G={}^{2}E_{6}(q)
follows by Ennola duality: Here
\mathrm{Out}(G)
is cyclic unless
q\equiv 2\mod 3
. If
q\equiv 2\mod 3
then
\mathrm{Out}(G)\cong S_{3}\times C_{a}
with odd
a
. Therefore, the same arguments from before apply as well. This leaves a rational conjugacy class with centralizer
({}^{2}A_{2}(q))^{3}.3
, i.e. the Ennola dual of cases (1) resp. (7).

Let us assume that
G=E_{7}(q)
. Case (1) appears whenever
q\equiv 1\mod 3
and case (2) whenever
q\equiv 2\mod 3
. Thus, in case (2) and whenever
q\equiv 3\mod 4
the group of field automorphisms has odd order again. This leave case (1) with
q\equiv 1\mod 12
.

When
\mathbf{G}
is not of type
E_{6}
or
E_{7}
observe that
\mathrm{Out}(G)
is cyclic and so there is nothing to show.
∎

We also discuss the blocks of
G=E_{6}(q)
which are strictly quasi-isolated but not isolated. For this recall that the semisimple element
s\in(\mathbf{G}^{\ast})^{F}
is called strictly quasi-isolated if
\operatorname{C}^{\circ}_{\mathbf{G}^{\ast}}(s)\operatorname{C}_{\mathbf{G}^{\ast}}(s)^{F}
is not contained in a proper Levi subgroup of
\mathbf{G}^{\ast}
.

Lemma 8.2
.

Let
b
be a strictly-quasi isolated but not isolated block of
G=E_{6}(q)
. Then
\mathrm{Out}(G)_{b}
is cyclic unless we are in case (3) or (9) in Table
1
(or its Ennola dual for
G={}^{2}E_{6}(q)
).

Proof.

We need to consider the geometric conjugacy class of the quasi-isolated element in
\mathbf{G}=E_{6}
whose centralizer is of type
D_{4}.3
. For
q\equiv 1\mod 3
this geometric conjugacy class splits into one rational class with centralizer
D_{4}(q)\Phi_{1}^{2}.3
(case (3) resp. case (9) in Table
1
) and two rational classes with centralizer
({}^{3}D_{4}(q)\Phi_{3}).3
(case (5) resp. case (10) in Table
1
). In the latter case, there are three blocks of
G(s)
covering the block of
G^{\circ}(s)
. Furthermore, the central characters of these blocks are non-trivial. Again, we deduce that no combination of a graph and diagonal automorphism can fix such a block, i.e. the block stabilizer is cyclic again.

On the other hand, for
q\equiv 2\mod 3
the geometric conjugacy class doesn’t split and a rational representative of it has centralizer
{}^{2}D_{4}(q)\Phi_{1}\Phi_{2}
(case (4) resp. (11) in Table
1
). In this case the element is quasi-isolated but not strictly quasi-isolated, as
\operatorname{C}_{(\mathbf{G}^{\ast})^{F}}(s)
is the set of fixed points of a Levi subgroup of
\mathbf{G}^{\ast}
.

Again, the case
G={}^{2}E_{6}(q)
can be obtained by Ennola duality.
∎

8.2.
Equivariant Jordan decomposition

Recall that we have a surjective morphism
\pi:\mathbf{G}\to\mathbf{G}^{\ast}
. Therefore, for any bijective morphism
\phi:\mathbf{G}\to\mathbf{G}
commuting with the action of
F
, we can define a dual morphism
\phi^{\ast}:\mathbf{G}^{\ast}\to\mathbf{G}^{\ast}
as the unique morphism which satisfies
\pi\circ\phi=\phi^{\ast}\circ\pi
.

Now assume that
\phi
stabilizes the idempotent
e_{s}^{\mathbf{G}^{F}}
. Then its dual morphism fixes the
(\mathbf{G}^{\ast})^{F}
-conjugacy class of the semisimple element
s
, see
[
NTT08
, Corollary 2.4]
. Thus, by possibly changing
\phi
by an inner automorphism of
\mathbf{G}^{F}
we can assume that its dual
\phi^{\ast}
stabilizes the semisimple element
s
. Hence,
\phi^{\ast}
stabilizes
\mathrm{C}_{\mathbf{G}^{\ast}}(s)
. By construction the group
\mathbf{G}(s)=\pi^{-1}(\mathrm{C}_{\mathbf{G}^{\ast}}(s))
and the idempotent
e_{s}^{G(s)}
are therefore
\phi
-stable.
Recall that
\mathcal{B}\subset\mathrm{Aut}(\tilde{G})
denotes the subgroup generated by field and graph automorphisms as in
[
MS16
, Section 2.B]
.

Using this we can show the following:

Proposition 8.3
.

Let
b
be a quasi-isolated block of
G
associated to the semisimple element
s\in G^{\ast}
.
Assume that
\mathrm{Out}(G)_{b}
is cyclic and
\mathrm{C}_{\mathbf{G}^{\ast}}(s)^{F}=\mathrm{C}^{\circ}_{\mathbf{G}^{\ast}}(s)^{F}
. Then there exists an
\operatorname{N}_{\tilde{G}\mathcal{B}}(G(s),s)
-equivariant bijection
\mathcal{E}_{2}(G,s)\to\mathcal{E}_{2}(G(s),s)
which induces a bijection
\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(G,b)\to\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(G(s),c)
.

Proof.

Recall that by Lemma
2.3
we have a
\tilde{G}(\tilde{s})
-equivariant bijection
\mathcal{J}:\mathcal{E}_{2}(G,s)\to\mathcal{E}_{2}(G(s),s)
. By the degree properties of Jordan decomposition, the height zero characters of a maximal block are mapped to the height zero characters of a maximal block. By Lemma
2.5
and its proof, the maximal blocks of
\mathcal{E}_{2}(G,s)
form one
\tilde{G}(\tilde{s})
-orbit and the maximal blocks of
\mathcal{E}_{2}(G(s),s)
form a
\tilde{G}(\tilde{s})
-orbit as well. Moreover, by Lemma
7.4
, the height zero characters of a non-maximal block of
\mathcal{E}_{2}(G,s)
are always mapped to height zero characters of the non-maximal block of
\mathcal{E}_{2}(G(s),s)
. From these considerations it’s clear that we can choose
\mathcal{J}
with the additional property that
\mathcal{J}(\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(b))=\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(c)
, where
c
corresponds to
b
under the bijection from Lemma
2.5
.

We are left to show that the so-obtained bijection is equivariant on the blocks with cyclic outer automorphism group. For this let
\phi:\mathbf{G}\to\mathbf{G}
be a morphism with
\phi^{\ast}(s)=s
such that
\phi
together with
\tilde{G}_{b}
generates
\mathrm{Out}(G)_{b}
and the order of
\phi
is coprime to the order of
\tilde{G}_{b}/G\operatorname{Z}(\tilde{G})\cong\tilde{G}(\tilde{s})_{c}/G(s)\operatorname{Z}(\tilde{G})
. We fix a
\tilde{G}_{b}\langle\phi\rangle
-transversal
\mathcal{T}
of
\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(b)
and wish to define a bijection
\mathcal{J}^{\prime}:\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(b)\to\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(c)
by defining
\mathcal{J}^{\prime}
as
\mathcal{J}
on
\mathcal{T}
and extending it
\tilde{G}_{b}\langle\phi\rangle
-equivariantly. To show that the so-obtained map is a well-defined bijection it suffices to show that
\chi
and
\mathcal{J}(\chi)
have the same stabilizer in
\tilde{G}_{b}\langle\phi\rangle
. By construction of the map
\mathcal{J}
, for
\chi\in\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(b)
and
\tau\in\langle\phi\rangle_{\chi}
it follows that
\tau(\mathcal{J}(\chi))
and
\mathcal{J}(\tau(\chi))
are in the same
\tilde{G}(\tilde{s})_{c}
-orbit. However since the order of
\phi
is coprime to the order of
\tilde{G}(\tilde{s})_{c}/G(s)\operatorname{Z}(\tilde{G})
it follows that necessarily
\tau(\mathcal{J}(\chi))=\mathcal{J}(\tau(\chi))
.
∎

This deals with all cases where the block stabilizer is cyclic except for case (2) and (8) in type
E_{6}
and their Ennola duals.

Lemma 8.4
.

Suppose that we are either in case (2) or (8) of type
E_{6}
.
Let
c_{0}
be the unique block associated to the union
\mathcal{E}_{2}(G^{\circ}(s),s)
of Lusztig series. Then there exists an
\operatorname{N}_{\tilde{G}\mathcal{B}}(G(s))_{b}
-equivariant bijection
\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(G^{\circ}(s),c_{0})\to\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(G,b)
.

Proof.

Using Theorem
1.2
and the proof of Lemma
2.3
, we find that the Jordan decomposition
\tilde{\mathcal{J}}:\mathcal{E}_{2}(\tilde{G},\tilde{s})\to\mathcal{E}_{2}(\tilde{G}(\tilde{s}),\tilde{s})
is
\operatorname{N}_{\tilde{G}\mathcal{B}}(G(s),s)\times\operatorname{Irr}(\tilde{G}/G)_{2}
-equivariant.

Our assumption implies by Lemma
2.5
that every
\tilde{G}
-orbit of a character in
\mathcal{E}_{2}(G,s)
has size three and there exists a unique character in this orbit which lies in the block
b
.
It follows that the map
b\operatorname{Res}_{G}^{\tilde{G}}:\mathcal{E}_{2}(\tilde{G},\tilde{s})/\operatorname{Irr}(\tilde{G}/G)_{2}\to\operatorname{Irr}(G,b)
is a bijection. It follows from the proof of Lemma
2.5
that the map
\operatorname{Res}_{G^{\circ}(s)}^{\tilde{G}(\tilde{s})}:\mathcal{E}_{2}(\tilde{G}(\tilde{s}),\tilde{s})/\operatorname{Irr}(\tilde{G}(\tilde{s})/G^{\circ}(s))_{2}\to\operatorname{Irr}(G^{\circ}(s),c_{0})
is a bijection as well. The composition of these bijections therefore yields an
\mathrm{N}_{\tilde{G}\mathcal{B}}(G(s))_{b}
-equivariant bijection as claimed.
∎

8.3.
Central characters

Let
\mathbf{H}
be a semisimple algebraic group with Frobenius endomorphism
F:\mathbf{H}\to\mathbf{H}
definining an
\mathbb{F}_{q}
-structure. Recall that by
[
MT11
, Proposition 9.15]
there exist natural isogenies
\tau:\mathbf{H}_{\operatorname{sc}}\to\mathbf{H}
and
\pi:\mathbf{H}\to\mathbf{H}_{\operatorname{ad}}
.

Lemma 8.5
.

Assume that
\mathbf{H}
is semisimple and
\mathbf{H}_{\operatorname{sc}}^{F}
is perfect and suppose that
\tau(\operatorname{Z}(\mathbf{H}_{\operatorname{sc}})^{F})=\operatorname{Z}(\mathbf{H})^{F}
. Then
\operatorname{Z}(\mathbf{H})^{F}\subset[\mathbf{H}^{F},\mathbf{H}^{F}]
.

Proof.

By
[
MT11
, Proposition 24.21]
(whose assumption can be weakened to
\mathbf{H}
semisimple)
\tau(\mathbf{H}_{\operatorname{sc}}^{F})=[\mathbf{H}^{F},\mathbf{H}^{F}]
. Therefore,
\operatorname{Z}(\mathbf{H})^{F}=\tau(\operatorname{Z}(\mathbf{H}_{\operatorname{sc}})^{F})\subseteq\tau(\mathbf{H}_{\operatorname{sc}}^{F})=[\mathbf{H}^{F},\mathbf{H}^{F}]
.
∎

The assumption
\tau(\operatorname{Z}(\mathbf{H}_{\operatorname{sc}})^{F})=\operatorname{Z}(\mathbf{H})^{F}
on the center in the previous lemma is for instance satisfied if
\mathbf{H}=\mathbf{H}_{\operatorname{sc}},\mathbf{H}_{\operatorname{ad}}
or
\operatorname{Z}(\mathbf{H}_{\operatorname{sc}})^{F}=\operatorname{Z}(\mathbf{H}_{\operatorname{sc}})
.

The following lemma can be compared to
[
Mal08
, Proposition 4.8]
:

Lemma 8.6
.

Let
H
be as in the previous lemma and
\ell\neq p
be arbitrary. Then the characters of
\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(B_{0}(H))
have
\operatorname{Z}(H)
in their kernel.

Moreover, there exists a bijection between the set of
H^{1}(F,\mathrm{Z}(\mathbf{H}))_{\ell}
-stable characters of
\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(B_{0}(H))
and orbits
\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(B_{0}(H_{\operatorname{ad}}))/\operatorname{Irr}(H_{\operatorname{ad}}/\pi(H))_{\ell}
.

Proof.

Let
\chi\in\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(B_{0}(H))
and
\lambda=\mathrm{det}(\chi)
its associated determinantal character. Since
\lambda
is linear it follows from Lemma
8.5
that
\lambda
has
\operatorname{Z}(H)
in its kernel. For
z\in\operatorname{Z}(H)_{\ell}
there exists an
\ell
-power root of unity
\zeta
such that
\chi(z)=\chi(1)\zeta
and
1=\lambda(z)=\zeta^{\chi(1)}
. Since
\chi
is of
\ell^{\prime}
-degree, it follows that
\zeta=1
and we conclude that
\operatorname{Z}(H)_{\ell}
is in the kernel of
\chi
. Moreover,
\chi
has
\operatorname{Z}(H)_{\ell^{\prime}}
in its kernel since it lies in the principal block.
Hence, any
\ell^{\prime}
-character of the principal block of
H
is trivial on the center of
H
.

Now we proceed as in the proof of
[
CE04
, Section 17]
. Take
\iota:\mathbf{H}\to\tilde{\mathbf{H}}
a regular embedding and
\pi:\tilde{\mathbf{H}}\to\mathbf{H}_{\operatorname{ad}}
the quotient map.
We define a map from the set of
H^{1}(F,\mathrm{Z}(\mathbf{H}))_{\ell}
-invariant characters of
\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(B_{0}(H))
to
\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(B_{0}(H_{\mathrm{ad}}))
as follows. Any
H^{1}(F,\mathrm{Z}(\mathbf{H}))_{\ell}
-stable character
\chi\in\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(B_{0}(H))
extends to an
\ell^{\prime}
-character
\tilde{\chi}
of
\tilde{H}
. This character can be assumed to have
\operatorname{Z}(\tilde{H})
in its kernel and to lie in the principal block of
\tilde{H}
by
[
CE04
, Theorem 15.11]
. The character
\tilde{\chi}
considered as a character of
\tilde{H}/\operatorname{Z}(\tilde{H})
lies again in the principal block of
H_{\operatorname{ad}}
. Note that if we were to choose a different character
\tilde{\chi}^{\prime}
with the same properties as
\tilde{\chi}
, then
\tilde{\chi}^{\prime}=\lambda\tilde{\chi}
for some
\lambda\in\operatorname{Irr}(\tilde{H}/H\mathrm{Z}(\tilde{H}))
of
\ell
-power order. As
H_{\operatorname{ad}}/\pi(H)\cong\tilde{H}/H\mathrm{Z}(\tilde{H})
by
[
CE04
, Equation 15.4]
it follows that the images of
\tilde{\chi}^{\prime}
and
\tilde{\chi}
in
H_{\operatorname{ad}}
are in the same
\operatorname{Irr}(H_{\operatorname{ad}}/\pi(H))_{\ell}
-orbit. The bijectivity of the map follows easily from this.
∎

In particular, if
\ell\nmid|H^{1}(F,\operatorname{Z}(\mathbf{H}))|
then the sets
\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(B_{0}(H))
and
\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(B_{0}(H_{\operatorname{ad}}))
are in bijection. We obtain the following consequence for our situation:

Lemma 8.7
.

As before, let
G
be an exceptional group of Lie type and
b
be a quasi-isolated
2
-block of
G
. Then
\operatorname{Z}(G)_{2}\leq\mathrm{Ker}(\chi)
for every height zero character
\chi\in\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(G,b)
.

Proof.

We can assume that
G=E_{7}(q)
, since
\operatorname{Z}(G)_{2}=1
otherwise. Let
s\in G^{\ast}
be the quasi-isolated element associated to the block
b
.
By Lemma
2.3
it suffices to show that every height zero character in the principal block
B_{0}(G(s))
has
\operatorname{Z}(G)_{2}
in its kernel. Let
\tau:\mathbf{G}(s)_{\operatorname{sc}}\to\mathbf{G}(s)
be the simply connected covering of the connected reductive group
\mathbf{G}(s)
. An inspection of
[
KM13
, Table 4]
shows that
\operatorname{Z}(\mathbf{G}(s)_{\operatorname{sc}})=\operatorname{Z}(\mathbf{G}(s)_{\operatorname{sc}})^{F}
. Therefore, by Lemma
8.6
every
2^{\prime}
-character of
G(s)
has
\operatorname{Z}(G(s))
in its kernel.
∎

9.
The inductive AM-condition

9.1.
A criterion for the iAM-condition

We recall the definition of AM-good, see also
[
Ruh22
, Definition 1.9]
. For the precise definition of the order relation ”
\geq_{b}
” (which we will not need in the following) see also
[
Ruh22
, Section 1]
.

Definition 9.1
.

Let
H
be a finite group and
b
a block of
H
with non-central defect group
D
. Assume that for
\Gamma:=\mathrm{N}_{\mathrm{Aut}(H)}(D,b)
there exist

(i)

a
\Gamma
-stable subgroup
M
with
\mathrm{N}_{H}(D)\leq M\lneq H
;

(ii)

a
\Gamma
-equivariant bijection
\Psi:\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(H,b)\to\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(M,B)
where
B\in\mathrm{Bl}(M\mid D)
is the unique block with
B^{H}=b
;

(iii)

(H/Z\rtimes\Gamma_{\chi},H/Z,\overline{\chi})\geq_{b}(M/Z\rtimes\Gamma_{\chi},M/Z,\overline{\Psi(\chi)}),

for every
\chi\in\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(H,b)
and
Z=\mathrm{Ker}(\chi)\cap\mathrm{Z}(H)
, where
\overline{\chi}
and
\overline{\Psi(\chi)}
lift to
\chi
and
\Psi(\chi)
, respectively.

Then we say that
b
is AM-good or satisfies the iAM-condition (with respect to the subgroup
M
). Moreover, we say that
\Psi:\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(H,b)\to\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(M,B)
is a
strong iAM-bijection for the block

b
with respect to the subgroup
M
.

Remark 9.2
.

If
\Psi
is a strong iAM-bijection as in the definition above, then we always have (see
[
NS14
, Lemma 3.12]
)

(H\rtimes\Gamma_{\chi},H,\chi)\geq_{b}(M\rtimes\Gamma_{\chi},M,\Psi(\chi)).

If
\Psi
satisfies only this weaker condition, we say that
\Psi
is an
iAM-bijection
.

9.2.
Groups of Lie type

We now assume again that
G
is an exceptional group of Lie type as in Section
2
.
We recall the following theorem:

Theorem 9.3
.

Let
b
be a block of
G
with defect group
P

and
M
a proper
\mathrm{Aut}(G)_{b}
-stable subgroup of
G
containing
\operatorname{N}_{G}(P)
. In addition, denote by
B
the unique block of
M
with
B^{G}=b
and we set
\tilde{M}:=M\operatorname{N}_{\tilde{G}}(P)
.
Let
\chi\in\operatorname{Irr}(G,b)
and
\chi^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Irr}(M,B)
such that the following holds:

(i)

We have
(\tilde{G}\mathcal{B})_{\chi}=\tilde{G}_{\chi}\mathcal{B}_{\chi}
and

\chi
extends to
G\mathcal{B}_{\chi}
.

(ii)

We have
(\operatorname{N}_{\tilde{G}}(M)\mathrm{N}_{G\mathcal{B}}(M))_{\chi^{\prime}}=\operatorname{N}_{\tilde{G}}(M)_{\chi^{\prime}}\mathrm{N}_{G\mathcal{B}}(M)_{\chi^{\prime}}
and
\chi^{\prime}
extends to
\mathrm{N}_{G\mathcal{B}}(M)_{\chi^{\prime}}
.

(iii)

(\tilde{G}\mathcal{B})_{\chi}=G(\operatorname{N}_{\tilde{G}}(M)\mathrm{N}_{G\mathcal{B}}(M))_{\chi^{\prime}}
.

(iv)

There exists
\tilde{\chi}\in\operatorname{Irr}(\tilde{G}\mid\chi)
and
\tilde{\chi}^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Irr}(\operatorname{N}_{\tilde{G}}(M)\mid\chi^{\prime})
such that the following holds:

•

For all
m\in{\mathrm{N}_{G\mathcal{B}}(M)}_{\chi^{\prime}}
there exists
\nu\in\operatorname{Irr}(\tilde{G}/G)
with
\tilde{\chi}^{m}=\nu\tilde{\chi}
and
\tilde{\chi}^{\prime m}=\mathrm{Res}^{\tilde{G}}_{\operatorname{N}_{\tilde{G}}(M)}(\nu)\tilde{\chi}^{\prime}
.

•

The characters
\tilde{\chi}
and
\tilde{\chi}^{\prime}
cover the same underlying central character of
\mathrm{Z}(\tilde{G})
.

(v)

The Clifford correspondents
\tilde{\chi}_{0}\in\operatorname{Irr}(\tilde{G}_{\chi}\mid\chi)
and
\tilde{\chi}^{\prime}_{0}\in\operatorname{Irr}(\operatorname{N}_{\tilde{G}}(M)_{\chi^{\prime}}\mid\chi^{\prime})
of
\tilde{\chi}
and
\tilde{\chi}^{\prime}
respectively satisfy
\mathrm{bl}(\tilde{\chi}_{0})=\mathrm{bl}(\tilde{\chi}_{0}^{\prime})^{\tilde{G}_{\chi}}
.

Let
\mathrm{Z}:=\mathrm{Ker}(\chi)\cap\mathrm{Z}(G)
.
Then

((\tilde{G}\mathcal{B})_{\chi}/Z,G/Z,\overline{\chi})\geq_{b}((\operatorname{N}_{\tilde{G}}(M)\mathrm{N}_{G\mathcal{B}}(M))_{\chi^{\prime}}/Z,M/Z,\overline{\chi^{\prime}}),

where
\overline{\chi}
and
\overline{\chi^{\prime}}
are the characters which inflate to
\chi
, respectively
\chi^{\prime}
.

Proof.

This is a consequence of
[
Ruh22
, Theorem 2.1]
and
[
Ruh22
, Lemma 2.2]
.
∎

Assumption (i) can essentially always be satisfied:

Theorem 9.4
.

In every
\tilde{G}
-orbit of
\operatorname{Irr}(G)
there exists a character
\chi
satisfying assumption (i) of Theorem
9.3
.

Proof.

See
[
CS19
, Theorem B]
.
∎

Lemma 9.5
.

Let
\chi\in\operatorname{Irr}(G,b)
.
Then block induction yields a bijective map
\mathrm{Bl}(\tilde{G}_{\chi}\mid b)\to\mathrm{Bl}(\tilde{G}\mid b)
.

Proof.

By
[
Nav98
, Corollary 6.2]
we deduce that block induction yields a surjective map
\mathrm{Bl}(\tilde{G}_{\chi}\mid b)\to\mathrm{Bl}(\tilde{G}\mid b)
. We therefore only need to show injectivity.

If
\tilde{G}_{b}=\tilde{G}_{\chi}
, then this follows from
[
Nav98
, Theorem 9.14]
. Otherwise, since
\tilde{G}/G\operatorname{Z}(\tilde{G})
has prime order (or order
1
) in our situation we have
\tilde{G}_{b}=\tilde{G}
and
\tilde{G}_{\chi}=G\operatorname{Z}(\tilde{G})
. Since
\tilde{G}_{\chi}=G\operatorname{Z}(\tilde{G})
is a central product, we deduce that
\mathrm{Bl}(\tilde{G}_{\chi}\mid b)
is in bijection with
(\operatorname{Z}(\tilde{G})/\operatorname{Z}(G))_{2^{\prime}}
. On the other hand, by
[
Bon05
, Corollary 2.8, Corollary 2.9]
we have
|\mathrm{Bl}(\tilde{G}\mid b)|\geq|(\operatorname{Z}(\tilde{G})/\operatorname{Z}(G))|_{2^{\prime}}
. Hence, the map is necessarily bijective.
∎

As a consequence of the previous lemma, we can in Theorem
9.3
replace condition (v) by the requirement that
\mathrm{bl}(\tilde{\chi}^{\prime})^{\tilde{G}}=\mathrm{bl}(\tilde{\chi})
.

9.3.
A criterion for blocks with cyclic stabilizer

In cases where we have additional information about the block the criterion of Theorem
9.3
simplifies.

Lemma 9.6
.

Let
b
be a strictly quasi-isolated
2
-block of
G
such that
\mathrm{Out}(G)_{b}
is cyclic and let
D
be a characteristic subgroup of a defect group
P
of
b
. Suppose that
f:\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(G,b)\to\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(\operatorname{N}_{G}(D),B)
is an
\operatorname{N}_{\tilde{G}\mathcal{B}}(D,B)
-equivariant bijection which preserves central characters. Assume that one of the following holds:

(a)

\tilde{G}_{b}=G\operatorname{Z}(\tilde{G})
.

(b)

\operatorname{Z}(G)
is a
2
-group and every character of
\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(G,b)
has
\operatorname{Z}(G)
in its kernel.

Then the block
b
is AM-good with respect to the subgroup
\operatorname{N}_{G}(D)
.

Proof.

We show that
\chi\in\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(G,b)
and
\chi^{\prime}:=f(\chi)
satisfy the assumptions of Theorem
9.3
. Firstly, by Theorem
9.4
we can assume by possibly replacing
\chi
by a
\tilde{G}
-conjugate of it that
\chi
and
\chi^{\prime}
satisfy assumptions (i)-(iii) of Theorem
9.3
.

Let’s suppose first that (a) holds. By assumption
\chi
and
\chi^{\prime}
lie over the same character of
\operatorname{Z}(G)
. Observe that
G\operatorname{Z}(\tilde{G})
is a central product of
G
and
\operatorname{Z}(\tilde{G})
. Hence, there exist extensions
\tilde{\chi}_{0}
and
\tilde{\chi}_{0}^{\prime}
which lie over the same character of
\operatorname{Z}(\tilde{G})
. Therefore, we have
\mathrm{bl}(\tilde{\chi}_{0})=\mathrm{bl}(\tilde{\chi}_{0}^{\prime})^{\operatorname{Z}(\tilde{G})G}
. Hence, condition (v).
Since the extension of
\chi
to
G\operatorname{Z}(\tilde{G})
is uniquely determined by its values on
\operatorname{Z}(\tilde{G})
it follows that the characters
\tilde{\chi}
and
\tilde{\chi}^{\prime}
satisfy assumption (iv).

Assume now that (b) holds. We can consider
\chi
as a character of the simple group
S=G/\operatorname{Z}(G)
. As
\mathrm{Out}(G)_{b}
is cyclic, the character
\chi
extends to a character
\hat{\chi}
of
(\tilde{G}\mathcal{B})_{\chi}
with
\operatorname{Z}(\tilde{G})
in its kernel. Denote by
\tilde{\chi}_{0}
the restriction of
\hat{\chi}
to
\tilde{G}_{\chi}
. Let
\overline{b}
be the unique block of
G/\operatorname{Z}(G)
which is dominated by
b
, see
[
Nav98
, Theorem 7.6]
. By assumption
G/\operatorname{Z}(G)
is a normal subgroup of
2
-power index in
\tilde{G}/\operatorname{Z}(\tilde{G})
. Hence, there exists a unique block
\overline{\tilde{b}}
of
\tilde{G}/\operatorname{Z}(\tilde{G})
which covers
\overline{b}
, see
[
Nav98
, Corollary 9.6]
. Thus, the character
\tilde{\chi}
necessarily lies in the block
\tilde{b}
dominating
\overline{\tilde{b}}
. We observe that the same arguments apply to the local character
\chi^{\prime}
.

Now assumption (iv) in Theorem
9.3
follows from observing that
\tilde{\chi}
and
\tilde{\chi}^{\prime}
are both
\operatorname{N}_{G\mathcal{B}}(D)_{\chi^{\prime}}
-stable as
\tilde{\chi}
extends to the character
\hat{\chi}\in\operatorname{Irr}((\tilde{G}\mathcal{B})_{\chi})
and similarly
\chi^{\prime}
extends to
\operatorname{N}_{(\tilde{G}\mathcal{B})_{\chi}}(D)
. For assumption (v) observe that
\tilde{\chi}
and
\tilde{\chi}^{\prime}
lie in the unique block covering
b
resp.
B_{D}
whose characters have
\operatorname{Z}(\tilde{G})_{2^{\prime}}
in their kernel.
∎

10.
Blocks with non cyclic-stablizer

10.1.
Quasi-isolated blocks with non-cyclic stabilizer

As before, let
b
be a
2
-block associated to a quasi-isolated semisimple element
s
. In this section, we assume that
\mathrm{Out}(G)_{b}
is non-cyclic. In particular, this means by Lemma
8.1
and Lemma
8.2
that we are in one of the following cases, where
\varepsilon\in\{\pm 1\}
:

•

G=E_{6}(\varepsilon q)
and
s
has centralizer
A_{2}(\varepsilon q)^{3}.3

•

G=E_{6}(\varepsilon q)
and
s
has centralizer
D_{4}(\varepsilon q).3

•

G=E_{7}
with
e=1
and
s
with centralizer
A_{5}(q)A_{2}(q)
.

We make the following useful observation:

Corollary 10.1
.

Let
b
be a quasi-isolated block of
G
associated to the semisimple element
s
such that

\mathrm{Out}(G)_{b}
is non-cyclic. Then the following hold:

(a)

We have
b=e_{s}^{\mathbf{G}^{F}}
and
\lambda\in\mathcal{E}(L,s)
is a character of central
2
-defect with
b_{L}(\lambda)=e_{s}^{\mathbf{L}^{F}}
.

(b)

Assume that
s
is not a semisimple element in
E_{6}
with centralizer of type
A_{2}^{3}
and let
\mathbf{L}_{0}
be the
1
-split Levi subgroup associated to
s
. Then we have
\mathbf{L}^{\ast}=\operatorname{C}_{\mathbf{L}^{\ast}}(s)
and
\mathbf{L}_{0}^{\ast}=\operatorname{C}_{\mathbf{L}_{0}^{\ast}}(s)
. In particular,
\mathbf{L}^{\ast}
(resp.
\mathbf{L}_{0}^{\ast}
) is the centralizer of a Sylow
e
-torus (resp. Sylow
1
-torus) of
(\mathbf{G}^{\ast},F)
.

Proof.

That
b=e_{s}^{\mathbf{G}^{F}}
follows from Lemma
2.5
. Moreover,
\lambda
is of central defect by using
[
KM13
, Proposition 2.7(g)]
and consulting the relevant tables in
[
KM13
]
. Moreover, we see from these tables that
\operatorname{C}_{\mathbf{L}^{\ast}}(s)=\operatorname{C}^{\circ}_{\mathbf{L}^{\ast}}(s)
is a torus and hence
e_{s}^{\mathbf{L}^{F}}
is a
2
-block which yields
b_{L}(\lambda)=e_{s}^{\mathbf{L}^{F}}
. Part (b) follows again by an inspection of the relevant tables in
[
KM13
]
.
∎

We let
B
be the unique block of
\operatorname{N}_{G}(\mathbf{L})
covering
e_{s}^{\mathbf{L}^{F}}
such that
B^{G}=b
, see Lemma
6.6
. Our aim in this and the next section is to show that there exists an iAM-bijection
\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(G,b)\to\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(\operatorname{N}_{G}(\mathbf{L}),B)
. Because of part (b) of Corollary
10.1
we will need to focus particulary on the semisimple element in
E_{6}(\varepsilon q)
with centralizer
A_{2}(\varepsilon q)^{3}.3
.

10.2.
Chevalley presentation and the extended Weyl group

Let
\mathbf{G}
be a simply connected simple algebraic group with root system
\Phi
and base
\Delta
. We let
x_{\alpha}:\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{q}\to\mathbf{G}
,
\alpha\in\Phi
, be the Chevalley generators of
\mathbf{G}
so that
\mathbf{G}=\langle x_{\alpha}(t)\mid\alpha\in\Phi,t\in\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{q}\rangle
. We let
\mathbf{X}_{\alpha}
denote the image of
x_{\alpha}
and set
\mathbf{T}:=\langle h_{\alpha}(t)\mid\alpha\in\Delta\rangle
. As
\mathbf{G}
is simply connected the torus
\mathbf{T}
is the direct product of the subgroups
h_{\alpha}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{\times})
,
\alpha\in\Delta
. Let
W:=\operatorname{N}_{\mathbf{G}}(\mathbf{T})/\mathbf{T}
the Weyl group of
\mathbf{G}
with respect to the maximal torus
\mathbf{T}
and
w_{0}\in W
the longest element in
W
. Denote by
S:=\{s_{\alpha}\mid\alpha\in\Delta\}
the set of simple reflections such that
W=\langle s_{\alpha}\mid\alpha\in W\rangle
.

Set
V:=\langle n_{\alpha}(1)\mid\alpha\in\Phi\rangle\subset\operatorname{N}_{\mathbf{G}}(\mathbf{T})
. We have a surjective map
V\to W
with kernel
H:=V\cap\mathbf{T}
which is an elementary abelian
2
-group of rank
|\Delta|
. By Matsumotos’s lemma we have a canonical set-theoretic splitting
r:W\to V,w\,\mapsto n_{\alpha_{1}}(1)\cdots n_{\alpha_{r}}(1)
, where
w=s_{\alpha_{1}}\cdots s_{\alpha_{r}}
is a reduced expression of
w
.

We will slightly deviate from the definition of the Frobenius endomorphism used in Section
4.2
, which will make it easier to exploit Ennola duality. For this let
F_{q}
be the Frobenius endomorphism acting as
x_{\alpha}(t)\mapsto x_{\alpha}(t^{q})
for
t\in\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
,
\alpha\in\Delta
. Moreover, if
\mathbf{G}
is of type
E_{6}
, let
\gamma:\mathbf{G}\to\mathbf{G}
be the graph automorphism given by
\gamma(x_{\alpha}(t))=x_{\tau(\alpha)}(t)
for
t\in\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
,
\alpha\in\Delta
. Here,
\tau
denotes the unique non-trivial symmetry of the associated Dynkin diagram. For
\varepsilon\in\{1,-1\}
we then define the Frobenius endomorphism
F
to be

F:=F_{q}\text{ if }\varepsilon=1\text{ and }F:=F_{q}\gamma_{0}\text{ if }\varepsilon=-1,

where
\gamma_{0}:=\gamma\tilde{w}_{0}
with
\tilde{w}_{0}:=r(w_{0})\in V
the canonical representative of the longest element in the Weyl group. By
[
MS16
, Lemma 3.12]
we know that

\operatorname{C}_{V}(\tilde{w}_{0}\gamma)=V
.

Let
s\in(\mathbf{T}^{\ast})^{F}
be the representative constructed in
[
Bon05
, Theorem 5.1]
of the rational conjugacy class of one of the elements relevant in Corollary
10.1
. Set
W(s):=\{w\in W^{\ast}\mid{}^{w}s=s\}
and let
W^{\circ}(s)
the Weyl group of
\operatorname{C}^{\circ}_{\mathbf{G}^{\ast}}(s)
relative to the maximal torus
\mathbf{T}^{\ast}
. We let
v\in V
be the canonical representative (of a
W^{\circ}(s)
-conjugate) of the longest element of the Weyl group
W^{\circ}(s)
. For
s
with centralizer of type
A_{2}(\varepsilon q)^{3}.3
the above constructions will be made more explicit later. As the image of
v
in
W
lies in
W^{\circ}(s)
there exists by Lang’s theorem an element
g\in\mathbf{G}^{\circ}(s)
such that
gF(g)^{-1}=v
.

Notation 10.2
.

For an integer
d\in\{1,2\}
and a sign
\varepsilon\in\{\pm\}
we define a new integer
d_{\varepsilon}
as
d_{\varepsilon}:=d
if
\varepsilon=1
and
d_{\varepsilon}:=d+(-1)^{d+1}
if
\varepsilon=-1
. We define
v_{(d_{\varepsilon})}:=1
if
d=1
and
v_{(d_{\varepsilon})}:=v
if
d=2
.

10.3.
Construction of the semisimple element

In this subsection, let
\mathbf{G}
be of type
E_{6}
. We will now focus on the element
s
whose centralizer has type
A_{2}(\varepsilon q)^{3}.3
. We will first construct a concrete representative of the conjugacy class of
s
.
For this, let
\tilde{\alpha}
be the highest root of
\Phi
and set
\alpha_{0}=-\tilde{\alpha}
.
We label the simple roots in the affine Dynkin diagram of
E_{6}
as follows:

\alpha_{\mathrlap{1}}
\alpha_{2}
\alpha_{\mathrlap{3}}
\alpha_{\mathrlap{4}}
\alpha_{\mathrlap{5}}
\alpha_{\mathrlap{6}}
\alpha_{0}

Recall that the highest root satisfies
\tilde{\alpha}=\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{6}+2\alpha_{3}+2\alpha_{5}+2\alpha_{2}+3\alpha_{4}

and that

\operatorname{Z}(\mathbf{G})=\{h_{\alpha_{1}}(\omega)h_{\alpha_{3}}(\omega^{2})h_{\alpha_{5}}(\omega)h_{\alpha_{6}}(\omega^{2})\mid\omega^{3}=1\}

by
[
GLS98
, Table 1.12.6]
.

For
\omega\in\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}^{\times}
of order
3
, we consider the semisimple element

s_{0}:=h_{\alpha_{1}}(\omega^{2})h_{\alpha_{3}}(\omega)h_{\alpha_{5}}(\omega)h_{\alpha_{6}}(\omega^{2})\in\mathbf{T}^{F}

with image
s:=\pi(s_{0})\in\mathbf{T}^{\ast}
.

It follows that
s_{0}
is
\gamma
-stable, where
\gamma
is the graph automorphism of order
2
of
E_{6}
, and
F_{p}(s_{0})s_{0}^{-1}\in\operatorname{Z}(\mathbf{G})
if and only if
p\equiv 1\mod 3
. In particular,
s
is
F
-stable if and only if
q\equiv 1_{\varepsilon}\mod 3
. In this case, the element
s
is isolated with connected centralizer of type
(A_{2}(\varepsilon q))^{3}
. On the other hand, if
q\equiv 2_{\varepsilon}\mod 3
, then
s
is
w_{0}F
-stable.

A base of the root system of
\operatorname{C}^{\circ}_{\mathbf{G}^{\ast}}(s)
is given by
\Delta(s)=(\Delta\setminus\{\alpha_{4}\})\cup\{\alpha_{0}\}.
Let
A_{1}:=\{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{3}\}
,
A_{2}:=\{\alpha_{5},\alpha_{6}\}
and
A_{3}:=\{\alpha_{0},\alpha_{2}\}
. Denote by
\mathbf{G}_{i}:=\langle x_{\alpha}(t)\mid t\in\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p},\alpha\in A_{i}\rangle
the reductive group with base
A_{i}
. Recall that
\mathrm{C}^{\circ}_{\mathbf{G}^{\ast}}(s)
is a semisimple algebraic group as the element
s
is isolated. We obtain
\mathbf{G}^{\circ}(s)=\mathbf{G}_{1}\mathbf{G}_{2}\mathbf{G}_{3}
, where
\mathbf{G}_{i}\cong\operatorname{SL}_{3}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p})
. There exists an
F
-stable central subgroup
\mathbf{Z}
of order
3
of
\mathbf{G}(s)
such that
\mathbf{G}_{i}\cap\mathbf{G}_{j}=\mathbf{Z}
for all
i\neq j
. We let
\mathbf{\mathbf{T}}_{i}=\mathbf{T}\cap\mathbf{G}_{i}
such that
\mathbf{T}
is a central product
\mathbf{T}=\mathbf{T}_{1}\mathbf{T}_{2}\mathbf{T}_{3}
. We define
v_{1}:=n_{\alpha_{1}}(1)
,
v_{2}:=n_{\alpha_{0}}(1)
,
v_{3}:=n_{\alpha_{6}}(1)
, and denote

v:=v_{1}v_{2}v_{3}.

As before, we let
g\in\mathbf{G}^{\circ}(s)
such that
gF(g)^{-1}=v
.

10.4.
Construction of Levi subgroups

Let
\mathbf{G}
be again of exceptional type. For a subset
I
of the simple roots
S
we denote by
\mathbf{L}_{I}=\mathbf{T}\langle\mathbf{X}_{\alpha}\mid\alpha\in\Phi_{I}\rangle
the standard Levi subgroup of
\mathbf{G}
associated to it.

Lemma 10.3
.

Assume the notation from above and let
s\in(\mathbf{T}^{\ast})^{F}
be one of the quasi-isolated elements appearing in Corollary
10.1
. Then the following hold:

(a)

The torus
\mathbf{T}
is the
1_{\varepsilon}
-split Levi subgroup associated to the semisimple element
s\in(\mathbf{G}^{\ast})^{F}
.

(b)

Assume that
\mathbf{G}
is of type
E_{6}
and let
I=\{\alpha_{4}\}
if
s
has rational type
A_{2}(\varepsilon q)^{3}.3
and
I=\emptyset
otherwise. Then the Levi subgroup
{}^{g}\mathbf{L}_{I}
is the
2_{\varepsilon}
-split Levi subgroup associated to the semisimple element
s\in(\mathbf{G}^{\ast})^{F^{\ast}}
with
F
-fixed points isomorphic to
({}^{g}\mathbf{L}_{I})^{F}\cong\mathbf{L}_{I}^{vF}
.

Proof.

Recall that by construction
s\in(\mathbf{T}^{\ast})^{F}
and
(\mathbf{T}^{\ast})^{F}
has polynomial order
\Phi_{1_{\varepsilon}}^{6}
. Since
\mathbf{T}
is a maximal torus of
\mathbf{G}
it follows that
\operatorname{C}_{\mathbf{G}}(\mathbf{T})=\mathbf{T}
, whence part (a).

For part (b) recall that the Lang image under
F
of the element
g\in\mathbf{G}^{\circ}(s)
is
v
and so
\mathbf{L}^{F}\cong\mathbf{L}_{I}^{vF}
.
If
\operatorname{C}_{G^{\ast}}(s)
has type
D_{4}(\varepsilon q)^{3}.3
the result in part (b) follows from Corollary
10.1
(b) and the fact that
v\in W^{\circ}(s)
is conjugate to the longest element in
W
. If
s
has centralizer of type
A_{2}(\varepsilon q)^{3}.3
, then
the torus
(\mathbf{T}^{g})^{F}\leq\operatorname{C}_{\mathbf{G}^{\ast}}(s)
has order

|\mathbf{T}^{vF}|=|\mathbf{T}_{1}^{vF}||\mathbf{T}_{2}^{vF}||\mathbf{T}_{3}^{vF}|=\Phi_{1}(q)^{3}\Phi_{2}(q)^{3}.

In particular,
\mathbf{T}^{g}
contains a Sylow
2_{\varepsilon}
-torus of
(\mathbf{G}^{\circ}(s),F)
. Hence, by Lemma
1.8
the centralizer
\operatorname{C}_{\mathbf{G}}(({}^{g}\mathbf{T})_{\Phi_{2_{\varepsilon}}})
in
\mathbf{G}
of the Sylow
\Phi_{2_{\varepsilon}}
-torus of
({}^{g}\mathbf{T},F)
is the
2_{\varepsilon}
-split Levi subgroup associated to
s
. We compute that
\mathbf{L}_{I}\cong\operatorname{C}_{\mathbf{G}}(\mathbf{T}_{\Phi_{2_{\varepsilon}}})
, where

\mathbf{T}_{\Phi_{2_{\varepsilon}}}=\{h_{\alpha_{0}}(t_{0})h_{\alpha_{1}}(t_{1})h_{\alpha_{6}}(t_{6})\mid t_{0},t_{1},t_{6}\in\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{\times}\}

is the Sylow
\Phi_{2_{\varepsilon}}
-torus of
(\mathbf{T},vF)
. It follows from this that
\mathbf{L}={}^{g}\mathbf{L}_{I}
is the
2_{\varepsilon}
-split Levi subgroup associated to
s
.
∎

In view of the previous lemma, we define
L_{(1_{\varepsilon})}:=\mathbf{T}^{F}
and
N_{(1_{\varepsilon})}:=\operatorname{N}_{\mathbf{G}^{F}}(\mathbf{T},e_{s}^{\mathbf{T}^{F}})
. Whereas, we define
L_{(2_{\varepsilon})}:=\mathbf{L}_{I}^{vF}
and
N_{(2_{\varepsilon})}:=\operatorname{N}_{\mathbf{G}^{vF}}(\mathbf{L}_{I},e_{s}^{\mathbf{L}_{I}^{vF}})
. For
d\in\{1,2\}
we let
W_{(d)}:=N_{(d)}/L_{(d)}
and observe that
W_{(d)}\cong\operatorname{C}_{W(s)}(v_{(d)})
.

10.5.
Construction of the normalizer

Our aim in this section is to construct a subgroup
V_{(2_{\varepsilon})}\leq\operatorname{C}_{V}(v)
such that
N_{(2_{\varepsilon})}=L_{(2_{\varepsilon})}V_{(2_{\varepsilon})}
.

For this, we first need the following two general observations about Chevalley relations.

Lemma 10.4
.

Let
n\in V
representing
w\in W
. For
\beta\in\Phi
and
u\in\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{\times}
we have
{}^{n}n_{\beta}(u)=n_{w(\beta)}(\pm u)
and similarly
{}^{n}x_{\beta}(u)=x_{w(\beta)}(\pm u)
. In particular, if
n
has odd order and
w(\beta)=\beta
, then
{}^{n}x_{\beta}(u)=x_{\beta}(u)
.

Proof.

The group
V
is generated by
\{n_{\alpha}(1)\mid\alpha\in\Phi\}
, so that we can write
n=n_{\alpha_{1}}(1)\cdots n_{\alpha_{l}}(1)
for some
l
. If
n=n_{\alpha}(1)
for some
\alpha
we have
{}^{n}x_{\beta}(u)=x_{w_{\alpha}(\beta)}(\pm u)
(and
{}^{n}n_{\beta}(u)=n_{w_{\alpha}(\beta)}(\pm u)
) by
[
Spä06
, Satz 2.1.6]
. The statement follows now by induction on
l
.
∎

Recall that
r:W\to V
denotes the canonical set theoretic splitting of the quotient map
V\to W
.
We have the following lemma, see
[
AH17
, Lemma 3.2]
.

Lemma 10.5
.

Suppose
I\subset S
is a set of simple roots with corresponding Levi factor
\mathbf{L}_{I}
. Let
\rho^{\vee}(I)
be one-half the sum of the positive coroots of

\mathbf{L}_{I}
and let
z_{I}:=(2\rho^{\vee}(I))(-1)
be the principal involution in
\mathbf{L}_{I}
. Then

r(w_{0}(I))^{2}=z_{I}.

Proposition 10.6
.

Let
s
be the quasi-siolated semisimple element with centralizer
A_{2}(\varepsilon q)^{3}.3
constructed above.
There exists a section
V_{(2_{\varepsilon})}
of
\operatorname{C}_{W(s)}(v)
under the natural map
V\to W
which has the following properties:

(a)

V_{(2_{\varepsilon})}
is
\gamma
-stable.

(b)

V_{(2_{\varepsilon})}\cong C_{4}\wr C_{3}
.

(c)

V_{(2_{\varepsilon})}\cap H=\langle h_{\alpha_{1}}(-1),h_{\alpha_{4}}(-1),h_{\alpha_{6}}(-1)\rangle\cong C_{2}^{3}
.

(d)

V_{(2_{\varepsilon})}
centralizes the subgroup
\langle\mathbf{X}_{\alpha_{4}},\mathbf{X}_{-\alpha_{4}}\rangle
.

Proof.

Let
V^{\circ}_{(2_{\varepsilon})}=\langle v_{1},v_{2},v_{3}\rangle
. For
i\neq j
the sets
A_{i}
and
A_{j}
are orthogonal by definition and all the roots of the root system
E_{6}
have the same length. Hence,
[
Spä06
, Bemerkung 2.1.7(b)]
ensures that
[v_{i},v_{j}]=1
. Hence,
V^{\circ}_{(2_{\varepsilon})}
is abelian.

Let
\tilde{w}_{0}=r(w_{0})
be the canonical representative of the longest element of the Weyl group
W
. Take
\tilde{w}_{0}^{\prime}=r(w_{0}^{\prime})
to be the canonical representative of the longest element
w_{0}^{\prime}
of the Weyl group of type
D_{5}
associated to the base
\Delta^{\prime}=\{\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3},\alpha_{4},\alpha_{5},\alpha_{6}\}
.
We define
x:=\tilde{w}_{0}\tilde{w}_{0}^{\prime}
. By construction, the element
x
induces a triality graph automorphism
\sigma
on the extended Dynkin diagram of
E_{6}
. It follows from Lemma
10.5
that
\tilde{w}_{0}
and
\tilde{w}_{0}^{\prime}
both have order
2
. Consequently,

\gamma(x)=\tilde{w}_{0}{}^{\tilde{w}_{0}}\tilde{w}_{0}^{\prime}=\tilde{w}_{0}^{\prime}\tilde{w}_{0}=x^{-1}.

The image of
x
in
W
has order
3
. Observe that the kernel of the natural map
V\to W
is the elementary
2
-group
H
and thus
x
has order
3
or
6
. We define
v^{\prime}:=x^{4}
if
x
has order
6
and
v^{\prime}:=x
if
x
has order
3
. Thus, the element
v^{\prime}
has order
3
and still induces the graph automorphism
\sigma
on the extended Dynkin diagram. We denote
V_{(2_{\varepsilon})}:=\langle V^{\circ}_{(2_{\varepsilon})},v^{\prime}\rangle
. By construction, the group
V_{(2_{\varepsilon})}
is a
\gamma
-stable section of
\operatorname{C}_{W(s)}(v)
.
From the Chevalley relations
[
Spä06
, Satz 2.1.6]
(see also Lemma
10.4
) it follows that
{}^{v^{\prime}}n_{\delta}(1)=n_{\sigma(\delta)}(\varepsilon_{\delta})
for
\delta\in\{\alpha,\beta\}
and signs
\varepsilon_{\delta}\in\{\pm 1\}
(possibly depending on the choice of structure constants in the Chevalley groups).
This ensures that
v^{\prime}
normalizes
V^{\circ}_{(2_{\varepsilon})}
. Since
V^{\circ}_{(2_{\varepsilon})}\cong C_{4}^{3}
it follows from this that
V_{(2_{\varepsilon})}\cong C_{4}\wr C_{3}
(see Lemma
6.4
). From this, we also see that
V_{(2_{\varepsilon})}\cap H=\langle h_{\alpha_{1}}(-1),h_{\alpha_{0}}(-1),h_{\alpha_{6}}(-1)\rangle\cong C_{2}^{3}
with
h_{\alpha_{0}}(-1)=h_{\alpha_{6}}(-1)h_{\alpha_{4}}(-1)h_{\alpha_{1}}(-1)
.

We are left to show that
V_{(2_{\varepsilon})}
centralizes the root subgroup
\mathbf{X}_{\pm\alpha_{4}}
.
Note that the roots
\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1},\alpha_{6}
are all orthogonal to
\alpha_{4}
. Hence, by the Chevalley relations (see
[
Spä06
, Bemerkung 2.1.7(b)]
) the elements
n_{\delta}(1)
with
\delta\in\{\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1},\alpha_{6}\}
centralize the root subgroup
\mathbf{X}_{\pm\alpha_{4}}
. Moreover, the element
v^{\prime}
has odd order and thus it centralizes
\mathbf{X}_{\pm\alpha_{4}}
by Lemma
10.4
.
∎

10.6.
Automorphisms and Lusztig series of twisted groups

The following notation which follows
[
MS16
, Notation 3.3]
will be used for working with twisted groups.

Notation 10.7
.

As before let
F
be the fixed Frobenius endomorphism from
10.2
defining an
\mathbb{F}_{q}
-structure on
\mathbf{G}
with
q=p^{m}
.
Set
h:=\mathrm{o}(\gamma)\mathrm{o}(v)
. Let
E:=\operatorname{C}_{hm}\times\langle\gamma\rangle
if
\varepsilon=1
and
E:=\operatorname{C}_{hm}
if
\varepsilon=-1
such that the first factor of
E
acts on

{\tilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{F_{p}^{2hm}}
by
\langle F_{p}\rangle
and the second by the group
\langle\gamma\rangle
generated by graph automorphisms.
Note that this action is faithful. Let
\widehat{F}_{p},\widehat{\gamma},\widehat{F}\in E
be
the elements that act on
{\tilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{F_{p}^{2hm}}
by
F_{p}
,
\gamma
and
F
,
respectively.

By construction, the element
\hat{F}
centralizes
\mathbf{\tilde{G}}^{F}
. We can therefore identify the subgroup
\mathcal{B}\subset\mathrm{Aut}(\mathbf{\tilde{G}}^{F})
from before with the quotient group
E/\langle\hat{F}\rangle
. The following lemma is a consequence of the proof of
[
MS16
, Proposition 3.4]
.

Lemma 10.8
.

Let
v\in V
be as before and
g\in\mathbf{G}
with
v=gF(g)^{-1}
. Then the map

\iota:\tilde{\mathbf{G}}^{F_{q}^{2e}}\rtimes E\to\tilde{\mathbf{G}}^{F_{q}^{2e}}\rtimes E,\quad x\mapsto{}^{g}x,

is an isomorphism which maps
\mathbf{G}^{F}\rtimes E
onto
\mathbf{G}^{vF}\rtimes E
.

It is a natural question to ask whether the isomorphism from Lemma
10.8
preserves Lusztig series.

Lemma 10.9
.

Let
s
be one of the the semisimple elements in Corollary
10.1
and
v\in G^{\circ}(s)
as before.
Then the isomorphism
\iota:\mathbf{G}^{F}\to\mathbf{G}^{vF},x\mapsto{}^{g}x,
induces a bijection
\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{G}^{vF},s)\to\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{G}^{F},s)
.

Proof.

Recall that
s\in\mathbf{T}^{\ast}
and
W^{\circ}(s)
is defined as the Weyl group of
\operatorname{C}^{\circ}_{\mathbf{G}^{\ast}}(s)
with respect to its maximal torus
\mathbf{T}^{\ast}
. Assume that for
z\in\{1,v\}
the duality between
(\mathbf{G},zF)
and
(\mathbf{G}^{\ast},Fz^{\ast})
is defined around the maximal tori
\mathbf{T}
and
\mathbf{T}^{\ast}
with the same choices, see
[
DM91
, Definition 13.10]
and the remarks following it. The characters in
\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{G}^{vF},s)
are the constituents of
R_{\mathbf{S}}^{\mathbf{G}}(\theta^{\prime})
, where
(\mathbf{S},\theta^{\prime})
is in duality with
(\mathbf{S}^{\ast},s^{\prime})
and
s^{\prime}
is rationally conjugate to
s
. In other words, by
[
DM91
, 3.25]
there exists
x\in\mathbf{G}
such that
\mathbf{T}={}^{x^{-1}}\mathbf{S}
such that the image
w
of
x^{-1}(vF)(x)
in
W^{\ast}
lies in
W^{\circ}(s)
and the character
\theta:={}^{x^{-1}}\theta^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Irr}(\mathbf{T}^{wvF})
corresponds to
s\in(\mathbf{T}^{\ast})^{w^{\ast}v^{\ast}F^{\ast}}
under the isomorphism
\operatorname{Irr}(\mathbf{T}^{wvF})\to\mathbf{T}^{F^{\ast}v^{\ast}w^{\ast}}
induced by duality, see
[
DM91
, Proposition 13.11]
. It therefore suffices to show that the irreducible constituents of
{}^{g}R_{\mathbf{S}}^{\mathbf{G}}(\theta^{\prime})
lie in
\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{G}^{F},s)
. We have
{}^{g}R_{\mathbf{S}}^{\mathbf{G}}(\theta^{\prime})=R_{{}^{g}\mathbf{S}}^{\mathbf{G}}({}^{g}\theta^{\prime})
by the proof of
[
CE04
, Proposition 7.13]
. It therefore suffices to show that the pair
({}^{g}\mathbf{S},{}^{g}\theta^{\prime})
corresponds to
(\mathbf{S}^{\ast},s)
under duality. We have
{}^{(gx)^{-1}}({}^{g}\mathbf{S},{}^{g}\theta^{\prime})=(\mathbf{T},\theta)
with
wv=(gx)^{-1}F(gx)\in W^{\circ}(s)
. By the consistent choice of duality, the character
\theta
corresponds to the element
s\in(\mathbf{T}^{\ast})^{w^{\ast}v^{\ast}F^{\ast}}
under the duality isomorphism
\operatorname{Irr}(\mathbf{T}^{wvF})\to(\mathbf{T}^{\ast})^{Fv^{\ast}w^{\ast}}
. Thus,
({}^{g}\mathbf{S},{}^{g}\theta^{\prime})
corresponds to
(\mathbf{S}^{\ast},s)
under duality.
∎

The proof of Lemma
10.9
also shows the following:

Corollary 10.10
.

Let
{}^{g}\mathbf{L}_{I}
be the
2_{\varepsilon}
-split Levi subgroup associated to
s
as in Lemma
10.3
. The isomorphism
\iota:\mathbf{L}_{I}^{F}\to\mathbf{L}_{I}^{vF},x\mapsto{}^{g}x,
induces a bijection
\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{L}_{I}^{vF},s)\to\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{L}_{I}^{F},s)
.

Recall that
W_{(e)}=N_{(e)}/L_{(e)}
. We have
W_{(1_{\varepsilon})}=W(s)
and
W_{(2_{\varepsilon})}=\operatorname{C}_{W(s)}(v)
by Lemma
1.10
. The groups
W_{(1)}
and
W_{(e)}
have a common Sylow
2
-subgroup
W_{2}
with
v_{(1)},v_{(e)}\in\operatorname{Z}(W_{2})
.

Lemma 10.11
.

There exists a natural bijection
\Xi:\mathcal{E}_{2}(L_{(1_{\varepsilon})},s)^{W_{2}}\to\mathcal{E}_{2}(L_{(2_{\varepsilon})},s)^{W_{2}}
.

Proof.

Let
z\in\{v_{(1)},v_{(e)}\}
. By duality we have a bijection
\operatorname{Irr}(\mathbf{T}^{zF})\to(\mathbf{T}^{\ast})^{F^{\ast}z^{\ast}}
, which restricts to a
\operatorname{C}_{W(s)}(z)
-equivariant bijection

\mathcal{E}_{2}(\mathbf{T}^{zF},s)\to A_{z}:=\{t\in(\mathbf{T}^{\ast})^{F^{\ast}z^{\ast}}\mid t_{2^{\prime}}=s\}.

As
z\in W_{2}
it follows that
A_{v_{(1)}}^{W_{2}}=A_{v_{(e)}}^{W_{2}}
. Since
W_{2}\leq\operatorname{C}_{W(s)}(z)
