we therefore obtain a bijection

\mathcal{E}_{2}(\mathbf{T}^{F},s)^{W_{2}}\to\mathcal{E}_{2}(\mathbf{T}^{vF},s)^{W_{2}}.

Moreover, by definition
\mathbf{T}^{\ast}=\operatorname{C}_{\mathbf{L}_{I}^{\ast}}(s)
and so Deligne–Lusztig induction yields a bijection
R_{\mathbf{T}^{vF}}^{\mathbf{L}_{I}^{vF}}:\mathcal{E}_{2}(\mathbf{T}^{vF},s)\to\mathcal{E}_{2}(\mathbf{L}_{I}^{vF},s)
, which is equivariant for all automorphisms of
\mathbf{L}_{I}
which commute with
vF
and stabilize
\mathbf{T}
.
The composition of these bijections yields a
bijection
\mathcal{E}_{2}(\mathbf{T}^{F},s)^{W_{2}}\to\mathcal{E}_{2}(\mathbf{L}_{I}^{vF},s)^{W_{2}}
as desired.
∎

10.7.
Extension maps

Whenever
\mathbf{L}_{(d)}
is a maximal torus for
d\in\{1,2\}
an extension map
L_{(d)}\lhd N_{(d)}
an automorphism-equivariant extension map is known by the work of Malle–Späth.

By Corollary
10.1
we therefore only need to consider the case when the centralizer of
s
is of type
A_{2}(\varepsilon q)^{3}.3
and
d=2_{\varepsilon}
.
The aim of this section is therefore to give an automorphism-equivariant extension map for the extension
L_{(2_{\varepsilon})}\lhd N_{(2_{\varepsilon})}
. For this, first observe that the semisimple element
s
is
E^{\prime}:=E
-stable if
p\equiv 1\mod 3
and
E^{\prime}:=\langle\gamma,w_{0}F_{p}\rangle
-stable if
p\equiv 2\mod 3
. Hence, for
d\in\{1,2\}
and
G_{(d)}:=\mathbf{G}^{v_{(d)}F}
we have

\operatorname{N}_{G_{(d)}E}(L_{(d)},e_{s}^{L_{(d)}})=N_{(d)}E^{\prime}.

To prove the existence of such an extension map, we need the following lemma.

Lemma 10.12
.

Let
s
be the semisimple element with centralizer
A_{2}(\varepsilon q)^{3}.3
.
Then there exists an
E^{\prime}
-equivariant extension map
\Gamma
for
Z:=V_{(2_{\varepsilon})}\cap H\lhd V_{(2_{\varepsilon})}
.

Proof.

Observe that
w_{0}F_{p}
acts in the same way as
\gamma
on
V
. We identify
V^{\circ}_{(2_{\varepsilon})}=\langle v_{1},v_{2},v_{3}\rangle
with
C_{4}^{3}
. For
\nu=\nu_{1}\times\nu_{2}\times\nu_{3}
we let
\theta=\theta_{1}\times\theta_{2}\times\theta_{3}\in\operatorname{Irr}(V^{\circ}_{(2_{\varepsilon})}\mid\nu)
such that
\theta_{i}=\theta_{j}
whenever
\nu_{i}=\nu_{j}
. Hence,
(V_{(2_{\varepsilon})}E^{\prime})_{\theta}=(V_{(2_{\varepsilon})}E^{\prime})_{\nu}
. If
v^{\prime}\notin V_{(2_{\varepsilon})}
we let
\Gamma(\nu):=\theta
. Otherwise, since
V_{(2_{\varepsilon})}\cong C_{4}\wr C_{3}
, there exists a character
\Gamma(\nu)\in\operatorname{Irr}(V_{(2_{\varepsilon})}\mid\theta)
with
v^{\prime}
in its kernel. We show that there exists a choice of
\theta
such that the so-obtained character
\Gamma(\nu)
is
E^{\prime}_{\nu}
-stable. For this we can assume that
\nu
is
\langle\gamma,v^{\prime}\rangle
since the claim is clear otherwise.
The character
\theta:=\theta_{1}\times\theta_{2}\times\theta_{3}
in
\operatorname{Irr}(V^{\circ}_{(2_{\varepsilon})}\mid\nu)
is
\gamma
-stable if and only if
\theta_{1}=\theta_{3}
. Moreover,
\theta
can only be
v^{\prime}
-stable if all
\theta_{i}
have order
4
. Now, we know that for
i=1,2,3
there are signs
\varepsilon_{i}\in\{\pm 1\}
such that
{}^{v^{\prime}}v_{i}=v_{i+1}^{\varepsilon_{i}}
. Since
v^{\prime}
has order
3
this forces
\varepsilon_{1}\varepsilon_{2}\varepsilon_{3}=1
. Moreover,
\gamma(v^{\prime})=v^{\prime-1}
. In particular,
{}^{\gamma v^{\prime}\gamma}v_{2}={}^{v^{\prime-1}}v_{2}
. Since
\gamma(v_{2})=v_{2}
and
\gamma(v_{1})=v_{3}
, we obtain
v_{1}^{\varepsilon_{2}}=v_{1}^{\varepsilon_{1}}
. We deduce
\varepsilon_{1}\varepsilon_{2}=1
which forces
\varepsilon_{3}=1
. In particular, for any character
\theta_{1}\in\operatorname{Irr}(C_{4})
of order
4
, the character
\theta=\theta_{1}\times\theta_{1}^{\varepsilon_{1}}\times\theta_{1}
is
\langle\gamma,v^{\prime}\rangle
-stable and covers the character
\nu
.
∎

For constructing extensions we use the following lemma, which is a consequence of
[
Spä12
, Lemma 2.11]
.

Lemma 10.13
.

Let
\tilde{X}
be a normal subgroup of a finite group
\tilde{Y}
and
Y
a subgroup of
\tilde{Y}
with
\tilde{Y}=\tilde{X}Y
. Assume that
\vartheta\in\operatorname{Irr}(\tilde{X})
is a
\tilde{Y}
-stable character which restricts irreducibly to the intersection
X:=\tilde{X}\cap Y
. If the character
\mathrm{Res}_{X}^{\tilde{X}}(\vartheta)
extends to a character
\mu\in\operatorname{Irr}(Y)
, then
\vartheta
extends to a character of
\operatorname{Irr}(\tilde{Y}\mid\mu)
.

Lemma 10.14
.

Let
s
be the semisimple element with centralizer
A_{2}(\varepsilon q)^{3}.3
.
There exists an extension map
\Lambda_{(2_{\varepsilon})}
for
\mathcal{E}_{2}(L_{(2_{\varepsilon})},s)
with respect to
L_{(2_{\varepsilon})}\lhd N_{(2_{\varepsilon})}
such that

\Lambda_{(2_{\varepsilon})}
is
\operatorname{N}_{G_{(2_{\varepsilon})}E}(L_{(2_{\varepsilon})},e_{s}^{L_{(2_{\varepsilon})}})
-equivariant.

Proof.

Let
V_{(2_{\varepsilon})}
be the section of
\operatorname{C}_{W(s)}(v)
constructed in Proposition
10.6
.
We can therefore write
N_{(2_{\varepsilon})}
as
N_{(2_{\varepsilon})}=L_{(2_{\varepsilon})}V_{(2_{\varepsilon})}
.
The intersection

Z=L_{(2_{\varepsilon})}\cap V_{(2_{\varepsilon})}=T_{(2_{\varepsilon})}\cap V_{(2_{\varepsilon})}=H\cap V_{(2_{\varepsilon})}=\langle h_{\alpha_{0}}(-1),h_{\alpha_{1}}(-1),h_{\alpha_{6}}(-1)\rangle

is a central subgroup of
\mathbf{L}_{I}
by Proposition
10.6
. Note that
\mathbf{L}_{I}=\operatorname{Z}^{\circ}(\mathbf{L}_{I})[\mathbf{L}_{I},\mathbf{L}_{I}]
and
[\mathbf{L}_{I},\mathbf{L}_{I}]\cong\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p})
.
By Bonnafé–Rouquier, see the main theorem of
[
BDR17
]
, the block
e_{s}^{L_{(2_{\varepsilon})}}
is Morita equivalent to the principal block of the torus
T_{(2_{\varepsilon})}
. In particular, this block has
|(T_{(2_{\varepsilon})})|_{2}
many irreducible characters.

Let
s_{0}
be the image of
s
under the map
\mathbf{L}_{I}^{\ast}\to[\mathbf{L}_{I},\mathbf{L}_{I}]^{\ast}
with central kernel obtained by duality from the inclusion map
[\mathbf{L}_{I},\mathbf{L}_{I}]\subset\mathbf{L}_{I}
, see
[
CE04
, Section 15.1]
. Since
1\neq s_{0}
is a
2^{\prime}
-element it follows that its centralizer in
[\mathbf{L}_{I},\mathbf{L}_{I}]^{\ast}\cong\mathrm{PGL}_{2}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p})
is again a torus. In particular,
e_{s_{0}}^{[L_{(2_{\varepsilon})},L_{(2_{\varepsilon})}]}
is again a block with
|(T_{(2_{\varepsilon})})\cap[L_{(2_{\varepsilon})},L_{(2_{\varepsilon})}]|_{2}
many irreducible characters. Since
e_{s_{0}}^{[L_{(2_{\varepsilon})},L_{(2_{\varepsilon})}]}
is the unique block below
e_{s}^{L_{(2_{\varepsilon})}}
we deduce by Clifford theory that every character in
\operatorname{Irr}([L_{(2_{\varepsilon})},L_{(2_{\varepsilon})}],e_{s_{0}}^{[L_{(2_{\varepsilon})},L_{(2_{\varepsilon})}]})
is
L_{(2_{\varepsilon})}
-stable.

Let us now first construct an extension map
\Lambda_{0}
for
[L_{(2_{\varepsilon})},L_{(2_{\varepsilon})}]Z\lhd[L_{(2_{\varepsilon})},L_{(2_{\varepsilon})}]V_{(2_{\varepsilon})}
.
The group
V_{(2_{\varepsilon})}
centralizes
[\mathbf{L}_{I},\mathbf{L}_{I}]=\langle\mathbf{X}_{\alpha_{4}},\mathbf{X}_{-\alpha_{4}}\rangle
by Proposition
10.6
. Hence,
[L_{(2_{\varepsilon})},L_{(2_{\varepsilon})}]V_{(2_{\varepsilon})}
is a central product. Recall the extension map
\Gamma
for
Z\lhd V_{(2_{\varepsilon})}
from Lemma
10.12
. We can therefore define an extension map
\Lambda_{0}
by sending a character
\psi_{0}\in\operatorname{Irr}([L_{(2_{\varepsilon})},L_{(2_{\varepsilon})}]Z)
with
\nu\in\operatorname{Irr}(Z\mid\psi_{0})
below
\psi_{0}
to the character
\psi_{0}.\Gamma(\nu)\in\operatorname{Irr}([L_{(2_{\varepsilon})},L_{(2_{\varepsilon})}]V_{(2_{\varepsilon})})
. By the equivariance properties of
\Gamma
, it follows that
\Lambda_{0}
is
\operatorname{N}_{G_{(2_{\varepsilon})}E}(L_{(2_{\varepsilon})},e_{s}^{L_{(2_{\varepsilon})}})
-equivariant as well.
We now define an extension map
\Lambda_{(2_{\varepsilon})}
for
\mathcal{E}_{2}(L_{(2_{\varepsilon})}s)
with respect to
L_{(2_{\varepsilon})}\lhd N_{\Lambda_{(2_{\varepsilon})}}=L_{(2_{\varepsilon})}V_{(2_{\varepsilon})}
as follows. For
\psi\in\mathcal{E}_{2}(L_{(2_{\varepsilon})},s)
its restriction
\psi_{0}:=\mathrm{Res}_{[L_{(2_{\varepsilon})},L_{(2_{\varepsilon})}]Z}^{L_{(2_{\varepsilon})}}(\psi)
to
[L_{(2_{\varepsilon})},L_{(2_{\varepsilon})}]
is irreducible. By Lemma
10.13
we can therefore define
\Lambda_{(2_{\varepsilon})}(\psi)\in\operatorname{Irr}(L_{(2_{\varepsilon})}(V_{(2_{\varepsilon})})_{\psi})
as the unique character which restricts to both
\psi\in\operatorname{Irr}(L_{(2_{\varepsilon})})
and
\Lambda_{0}(\psi_{0})
. By construction the map
\Lambda_{(2_{\varepsilon})}
is
\operatorname{N}_{GE}(L_{(2_{\varepsilon})},e_{s}^{L_{(2_{\varepsilon})}})
-equivariant.
∎

10.8.
Construction of the AM-bijection

To show the automorphism equivariance of the map constructed in Theorem
10.17
we need the following statements which are analogues of
[
BR21
, Proposition 4.4, Proposition 4.5]
. For
d\in\{1,2\}
we define
\tilde{T}_{(d)}:=\mathbf{\tilde{T}}^{v_{(d)}F}
and let
\tilde{L}_{(d)}:=L_{(d)}\tilde{T}_{(d)}
and
\tilde{N}_{(d)}:=N_{(d)}\tilde{L}_{(d)}
.

Proposition 10.15
.

Let
d\in\{1,2\}
and
\Lambda_{(d)}
be the extension map for
\mathcal{E}_{2}(L_{(d)},s)
from
[
MS16
, Corollary 3.13]
or Corollary
10.14
with respect to
L_{(d)}\lhd N_{(d)}
. Then the map

\Pi:\mathcal{P}:=\{(\mu,\eta)\mid\mu\in\operatorname{Irr}(L_{(d)},e_{s}^{L_{(d)}}),\eta\in\operatorname{Irr}(W_{(d)}(\mu))\}\to\operatorname{Irr}(N_{(d)},e_{s}^{L_{(d)}})

with
\Pi(\mu,\eta)=\operatorname{Ind}_{N_{(d)}(\mu)}^{N_{(d)}}(\Lambda_{(d)}(\mu)\eta)

is surjective and satisfies

(a)

\Pi(\mu,\eta)=\Pi(\mu^{\prime},\eta^{\prime})
if and only if there exists some
n\in N_{(d)}
such that
{}^{n}\mu=\mu^{\prime}
and
{}^{n}\eta=\eta^{\prime}
.

(b)

{}^{\sigma}\Pi(\mu,\eta)=\Pi({}^{\sigma}\mu,{}^{\sigma}\eta)
for all
\sigma\in E^{\prime}
.

(c)

Let
t\in\tilde{T}_{(d)}
,
\tilde{\mu}\in\operatorname{Irr}(\tilde{L}_{(d)}\mid\mu)
and
\nu_{t}\in\operatorname{Irr}(N_{(d)}(\mu)/N_{(d)}({\tilde{\mu}}))
is the faithful linear character given by
{}^{t}\Lambda(\mu)=\Lambda(\mu)\nu_{t}
. Then we have
{}^{t}\Pi(\mu,\eta)=\Pi(\mu,\eta\nu_{t})
.

Proof.

The proof of part (a) and (b) works as in
[
MS16
, Proposition 3.15]
. For part (c) observe that our assumption on the semisimple element
s
implies that
\operatorname{C}_{\mathbf{L}^{\ast}}(s)
is a torus. Hence, for all elements
t\in\operatorname{C}_{\mathbf{G}^{\ast}}(s)_{2}
the centralizer
\operatorname{C}_{\mathbf{L}^{\ast}}(st)
is connected. Thus, every character of
\mathcal{E}_{2}(L_{(d)},s)
is
\tilde{T}_{(d)}
. Hence, also part (c) follows as in
[
MS16
, Proposition 3.15]
.
∎

For the following proposition recall that
W_{(2_{\varepsilon})}
can be viewed as a subgroup of
W_{(1_{\varepsilon})}
. The formulation of the following proposition is slightly different from
[
BR21
, Proposition 4.5]
but the proof is identical.

Proposition 10.16
.

Let
\mu_{0}\in\mathcal{E}_{2}(L_{(1_{\varepsilon})},s)^{W_{2}}

with image
\mu=\Xi(\mu_{0})\in\mathcal{E}_{2}(L_{(2_{\varepsilon})},s)^{W_{2}}
.
Suppose that
\nu_{0}\in\operatorname{Irr}(W_{(1_{\varepsilon})}(\mu_{0}))
and
t_{0}\in\tilde{T}_{(1_{\varepsilon})}
satisfy
{}^{t_{0}}\Lambda_{(1_{\varepsilon})}(\mu_{0})=\Lambda_{(1_{\varepsilon})}(\mu_{0})\nu_{0}
. Then we have

{}^{t}\Lambda_{(2_{\varepsilon})}(\mu)=\Lambda_{(2_{\varepsilon})}(\mu)\nu,

where
t:=\iota(t_{0})
and
\nu:=\operatorname{Res}^{W_{(1_{\varepsilon})}(\mu_{0})}_{W_{(2_{\varepsilon})}(\mu_{0})}(\nu_{0})
.

Theorem 10.17
.

Let
b=e_{s}^{G}
be one of the blocks in Corollary
10.1
and
B
the unique block of
\operatorname{N}_{G}(\mathbf{L})
covering
e_{s}^{L}
. Then there exists an
\operatorname{N}_{\tilde{G}\mathcal{B}}(\mathbf{L})_{B}
-equivariant bijection
f:\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(\operatorname{N}_{G}(\mathbf{L}),B)\to\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(G,e_{s}^{G})
.

Proof.

We first parametrize the local characters. For this we use the
e
-split Levi subgroup
\mathbf{L}_{(e)}
.
Let
\Lambda^{(e)}
be the extension map associated to
L_{(e)}\lhd N_{(e)}
from Lemma
10.14
.
Recall that we denote
W_{(e)}=N_{(e)}/L_{(e)}
. By Proposition
10.15
we obtain a surjective map

\Pi:\mathcal{P}=\{(\mu,\eta)\mid\mu\in\operatorname{Irr}(L_{(e)},e_{s}^{L_{(e)}}),\eta\in\operatorname{Irr}(W_{(e)}(\mu))\}\to\operatorname{Irr}(N_{(e)},e_{s}^{L_{(e)}}),(\mu,\eta)\to\operatorname{Ind}_{N_{(e)}(\mu)}^{N_{(e)}}(\Lambda_{(e)}(\mu)\eta).

The characters of height zero in the unique block
N_{(e)}
covering the block
e_{s}^{L_{(e)}}
are precisely those which correspond to tuples
(\mu,\eta)\in\mathcal{P}
with
2\nmid|W_{(e)}:W_{(e)}(\mu)|
and
\eta\in\operatorname{Irr}_{2^{\prime}}(W_{(e)}(\mu))
. By Proposition
10.15
(c) we can assume that the pair
(\mu,\eta)
satisfies additionally
W_{2}\leq W_{(e)}(\mu)
.

Let us now consider the global characters. We abbreviate
G_{(1)}:=\mathbf{G}^{v_{(1)}F}
. Every height zero character
\chi
of
\mathcal{E}_{2}(G_{(1)},s)
lies in the principal series, i.e. it is of the form
\chi=R_{L_{(1)}}^{G_{(1)}}(\mu_{0})_{\eta_{0}}
for some
\mu_{0}\in\mathcal{E}_{2}(L_{(1)},s)
and
\eta_{0}\in\operatorname{Irr}(W_{(1)}(\mu_{0}))
, see Corollary
3.2
. For such a character to have height zero we must by Corollary
3.3
necessarily have that
\mu_{0}
is of height zero with
2\nmid|W_{(1)}:W_{(1)}(\mu_{0})|
and
\eta_{0}\in\operatorname{Irr}_{2^{\prime}}(W_{(1)}(\mu_{0}))
.

We now construct our bijection. We can choose the Sylow
2
-subgroup
W_{2}
of
W_{(1)}
and
W_{(e)}
to be
E^{\prime}
-stable and we observe that
\operatorname{N}_{W_{(e)}}(W_{2})E^{\prime}=\operatorname{N}_{W_{(1)}}(W_{2})E^{\prime}
. Moreover, recall that by Lemma
10.11
and its proof we have an
\operatorname{N}_{W_{(e)}}(W_{2})E^{\prime}
-equivariant bijection

\Xi:\mathcal{E}_{2}(L_{(e)},s)^{W_{2}}\to\mathcal{E}_{2}(L_{(1)},s)^{W_{2}}.

Fix an
\operatorname{N}_{W_{(e)}}(W_{2})E^{\prime}
-transversal
\mathcal{T}
of
\mathcal{E}_{2}(L_{(e)},s)^{W_{2}}
.
For
\mu\in\mathcal{T}
, the group
W_{2}
is a common Sylow
2
-subgroup of
W_{(e)}(\mu)
and
W_{(1)}(\mu_{0})
where
\mu_{0}:=\Xi(\mu)
.
As in
[
BR21
, Lemma 4.3]
we construct for every
\mu\in\mathcal{T}
an
(\operatorname{N}_{W_{(e)}}(W_{2})E^{\prime})_{\mu}
-equivariant McKay-bijection

f_{\mu}:\operatorname{Irr}_{2^{\prime}}(W_{(e)}(\mu))\to\operatorname{Irr}_{2^{\prime}}(W_{(1)}(\mu_{0})),

compatible with the multiplication of linear characters.
For
\mu\in\mathcal{E}_{2}(L_{(e)},s)^{W_{2}}
with
{}^{x}\mu\in\mathcal{T}
for
x\in\operatorname{N}_{W_{(e)}}(W_{2})E^{\prime}
we define
{}^{x}f_{\mu}:=f_{{}^{x}\mu}
.
Denote by
B_{(e)}
the unique block of
N_{(e)}
covering
e_{s}^{L_{(e)}}
and set
b_{(1)}=e_{s}^{G_{(1)}}
. By Proposition
10.15
we obtain a bijection

\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(N_{(e)},B_{(e)})\to\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(G_{(1)},b_{(1)}),\quad\operatorname{Ind}_{N_{(e)}(\mu)}^{N_{(e)}}(\Lambda^{(e)}(\mu)\eta)\mapsto R_{L_{(1)}}^{G_{(1)}}(\Xi(\mu))_{f_{\mu}(\eta)}.

Note that it is actually not clear that the so-obtained map is well-defined as the character
R_{L_{(1)}}^{G_{(1)}}(\Xi(\mu))_{f_{\mu}(\eta)}
is not necessarily of height zero.
However, by Lemma
10.9
and Corollary
10.10
, using the map
\iota
, we obtain bijections
\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(N_{(e)},B_{(e)})\to\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(\operatorname{N}_{G}(\mathbf{L}),B)
and
\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(G_{(1)},b_{(1)})\to\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(G,b)
and by Remark
6.3
we have
|\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(B)|=|\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(b)|
. In addition, the map is injective by the properties of Harish-Chandra series and its image contains all height zero characters by the arguments from above. Therefore, the map is necessarily a well-defined bijection and yields by applying
\iota
a bijection

f:\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(\operatorname{N}_{G}(\mathbf{L}),B)\to\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(G,b)
.

It is therefore left to show that
f
is
\operatorname{N}_{\tilde{G}E}(\mathbf{L})_{B}
-equivariant. This can however by checked exactly as in the proof of
[
BR21
, Theorem 4.8]
, where we replace

[
BR21
, Proposition 4,4, Proposition 4.5]
by
Propositions
10.15
and
10.16
.
∎

11.
Verifying the inductive condition

In this section we show the bijection
f
from Theorem
10.17
is a strong iAM-bijection. For this we need the following auxiliary result:

Proposition 11.1
.

In the situation of Theorem
10.17
, a character
\chi\in\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(G,b)
satisfies assumption (i) of Theorem
9.3
if and only if its AM-correspondent
\psi:=f(\chi)
satisfies assumption (ii).

Proof.

By the equivariance of
f
it is clear that
(\tilde{G}\mathcal{B})_{\chi}=\tilde{G}_{\chi}\mathcal{B}_{\chi}
if and only if
(\operatorname{N}_{\tilde{G}}(L)\mathrm{N}_{G\mathcal{B}}(L))_{\chi^{\prime}}=\operatorname{N}_{\tilde{G}}(L)_{\chi^{\prime}}\mathrm{N}_{G\mathcal{B}}(L)_{\chi^{\prime}}
. To finish the proof of the proposition we show that every character in
\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(G,b)
(resp.
\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(\operatorname{N}_{G}(\mathbf{L}),B)
) extends to its inertia group in
G\mathcal{B}_{\chi}
(resp.
\operatorname{N}_{G\mathcal{B}}(\mathbf{L})
).

Let
\chi\in\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(G,b)\cup\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(\operatorname{N}_{G}(\mathbf{L}),B)
and observe that
\mathcal{B}_{\chi}
is only non-cyclic if
\mathbf{G}
is of type
E_{6}
and
\gamma\in\mathcal{B}_{\chi}
. However if
\gamma\in\mathcal{B}_{\chi}
then for
g\in\tilde{G}
we have
\gamma\in\mathcal{B}_{{}^{g}\chi}
if and only if
\gamma(g)g^{-1}\in\tilde{G}_{\chi}
. Since
H^{1}(F,\mathrm{Z}(G))
has odd order and
\gamma
acts by inversion on it this is equivalent to
g\in\tilde{G}_{\chi}
. Hence it suffices to show that there exists a character in the
\tilde{L}
-orbit of
\chi
which satisfies condition (i) resp. (ii) of Theorem
9.3
.

In particular for
\chi\in\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(G,b)
this follows from Theorem
9.4
. For the characters of
\operatorname{N}_{G}(\mathbf{L})
, the claim is a consequence of
[
MS16
, Proposition 3.18]
whenever the associated
e
-split Levi subgroup
\mathbf{L}
is a maximal torus. On the other hand, if
\varepsilon=-1
then
\mathcal{B}
is cyclic. If
\varepsilon=1
, then we only need to consider the case
e=2
, i.e. when
q\equiv 3\mod 4
, since
\mathbf{L}
is a maximal torus otherwise. In that case
q
is not a square and thus the group of field automorphisms has odd order. It thus follows again that
\mathcal{B}
is a cyclic group.
∎

We now discuss the Clifford theory for the normal inclusion
G\lhd\tilde{G}
. For this, we fix a character
\psi=\operatorname{Ind}_{N_{(e)}(\mu_{(e)})}^{N_{(e)}}(\Lambda^{(e)}(\mu_{(e)})\eta_{(e)})
and we let
\chi:=R_{L_{(1)}}^{G_{(1)}}(\mu_{(1)})_{\eta_{(1)}}
be its image under the map
f
. Moreover, we fix a semisimple element
\tilde{s}\in(\mathbf{\tilde{G}}^{\ast})^{F}
of
2^{\prime}
-order with
\iota^{\ast}(\tilde{s})=s
. By the proof of Proposition
10.15
we find for every character
\mu_{(d)}\in\mathcal{E}_{2}(L_{(d)},s)
an extension
\tilde{\mu}_{(d)}\in\mathcal{E}_{2}(\tilde{L}_{(d)},\tilde{s})
. We also fix characters
\tilde{\eta}_{(d)}\in\operatorname{Irr}(W_{(d)}(\tilde{\mu}_{(d)})\mid\eta_{(d)})
below
\eta_{(d)}
.

Corollary 11.2
.

For
d\in\{1,2\}
, there exists an
N_{(d)}E
-equivariant extension map
\tilde{\Lambda}_{(d)}
for the set
\mathcal{E}_{2}(\tilde{L}_{(d)},\tilde{s})
with respect to
\tilde{L}_{(d)}\lhd\tilde{N}_{(d)}
by sending
\tilde{\mu}\in\mathcal{E}_{2}(\tilde{L}_{(d)},\tilde{s})
to the unique common extension of
\tilde{\mu}
and
\operatorname{Res}_{N_{\tilde{\mu}}}^{N_{\mu}}(\Lambda(\mu))
where
\mu\in\mathcal{E}_{2}(L_{(d)},s)
is the restriction of
\tilde{\mu}
to
L_{(d)}
.

Proof.

This follows exactly as in the proof
[
MS16
, Proposition 3.20]
.
∎

We can now complete the proof of the inductive conditions:

Theorem 11.3
.

Let
b
be one of the blocks from Theorem
10.17
.
Then
b
is AM-good relative to the subgroup
\operatorname{N}_{G}(\mathbf{L})
.

Proof.

Let
B
be the unique block of
\operatorname{N}_{G}(\mathbf{L})
covering
e_{s}^{L}
. We show that the bijection
f:\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(b)\to\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(B)
from Theorem
10.17
is a strong AM-bijection, see Definition
9.1
. To show this we want to use Theorem
9.3
. First observe that the subgroup
M:=\operatorname{N}_{G}(\mathbf{L})
satisfies the assumptions of Theorem
9.3
by Lemma
6.6
. Let
\chi\in\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(b)
and
\chi^{\prime}:=f(\chi)
. By possibly conjugating
\chi
by an element of
\tilde{G}
we can assume by Theorem
9.4
that the character
\chi
satisfies condition (i) of Theorem
9.3
. Using the Butterfly Theorem
[
Ruh22
, Theorem 1.10]
we see that it’s enough to show that
\chi
and
\chi^{\prime}
satisfy the remaining conditions in Theorem
9.3
. Since
f
is equivariant we deduce that conditions (ii) and (iii) hold, see Proposition
11.1
.

As in
[
BR21
, Lemma 5.3]
one sees that
\tilde{\chi}:=R_{\tilde{L}_{(1)}}^{\tilde{G}_{(1)}}(\tilde{\mu}_{(1)})_{\tilde{\eta}_{(1)}}\in\operatorname{Irr}(\tilde{G},e_{\tilde{s}}^{\tilde{G}})
and
\tilde{\psi}:=\tilde{\Lambda}(\tilde{\mu})\tilde{\eta}\in\operatorname{Irr}(\tilde{N}_{(e)},\tilde{B}_{e})
. These characters satisfy condition (iv) of Theorem
9.3
by Proposition
10.15
. Finally for condition (v) by the remarks after Lemma
9.5
it suffices to show that
\mathrm{bl}(\tilde{\psi})^{\tilde{G}}=\mathrm{bl}(\tilde{\chi})
. This is however guaranteed by Proposition
1.11
.
∎

12.
Centralizers of type
A_{2}(\varepsilon q^{3}).3

12.1.
Setup and notation

After having introduced the notation for dealing with the semisimple element
s
with centralizer of type
A_{2}(\varepsilon q)^{3}.3
in
E_{6}(\varepsilon q)
, we use this subsection to complete the proof of the iAM-condition in cases (2) and (8) of Table
1
, i.e. of the blocks associated to the semisimple element of
E_{6}(\varepsilon q)
with centralizer of type
A_{2}(\varepsilon q^{3}).3
. As before, we let
\mathbf{G}
be a simple, simply connected algebraic group of type
E_{6}
with Frobenius endomorphism
F
as in Section
10
. In addition, we let
s\in\mathbf{T}^{\ast}
be the semisimple element with centralizer
A_{2}^{3}.3
as in Section
10
, but we will consider
\mathbf{G}
with a different Frobenius endomorphism in order to simplify the calculations for these cases. Throughout this section, we will consider the Frobenius endomorphism

F:=F_{q}v^{\prime}\text{ if }\varepsilon=1\text{ and }F:=F_{q}\gamma_{0}v^{\prime}\text{ if }\varepsilon=-1,

where
v^{\prime}\in V
is the Weyl group element constructed in the proof of Proposition
10.6
. We observe that
\mathbf{G}(s)^{F}
is of type
A_{2}(\varepsilon q^{3}).3
and
(\mathbf{T},F)
has polynomial order
\Phi_{1}^{3}\Phi_{2}^{3}
.

We define

m:=n_{\alpha_{0}}(1)\,{}^{v^{\prime}}n_{\alpha_{0}}(1)\,{}^{v^{\prime 2}}n_{\alpha_{0}}(1)

and

n:=n_{\alpha_{2}}(1)\,{}^{v^{\prime}}n_{\alpha_{2}}(1)\,{}^{v^{\prime 2}}n_{\alpha_{2}}(1).

Our construction together with
[
Spä06
, Bemerkung 2.1.7(b)]
implies that
m,n\in V^{F}=\operatorname{C}_{V}(v^{\prime})
.
We fix an element
g\in\mathbf{G}
such that
m=g^{-1}F(g)
. Moreover,
m_{(d_{\varepsilon})}:=1
if
d=1
and
m_{(d_{\varepsilon})}:=m
if
d=2
.

Notation 12.1
.

Set
h:=\mathrm{o}(\gamma_{0})\mathrm{o}(m)\mathrm{ord}(v^{\prime})
. Let
E:=\operatorname{C}_{hm}\times\langle\gamma_{0}\rangle
if
\varepsilon=1
and
E:=\operatorname{C}_{hm}
if
\varepsilon=-1
such that the first factor of
E
acts on

{\tilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{F_{p}^{2hm}}
by
\langle F_{p}\rangle
and the second by the group
\langle\gamma_{0}\rangle
generated by graph automorphisms.

Note that we changed the graph automorphism
\gamma
to
\gamma_{0}
in order to ensure that
E
stabilizes
\mathbf{G}^{F}
, where
F
is defined as above.
Again we denote by

\iota:\tilde{\mathbf{G}}^{F_{q}^{2e}}\rtimes E\to\tilde{\mathbf{G}}^{F_{q}^{2e}}\rtimes E,\quad x\mapsto{}^{g}x,

the isomorphism induces by conjugation with the element
g
. Observe, that this is an isomorphism which maps
\mathbf{G}^{F}\rtimes E
onto
\mathbf{G}^{mF}\rtimes E
.

12.2.
Construction of Levi subgroups

We now construct the Levi subgroups associated to the semisimple element
s\in(\mathbf{G}^{\ast})^{F}
.
The Levi subgroup
\mathbf{L}_{(1_{\varepsilon})}
of
\mathbf{G}
associated to
s\in(\mathbf{T}^{\ast})^{F}
is the centralizer of the
\Phi_{1_{\varepsilon}}
-torus

\mathbf{S}_{(1_{\varepsilon})}:=\{h_{\alpha_{2}+\alpha_{3}+\alpha_{5}}(t_{1})h_{\alpha_{0}+\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{6}}(t_{2})\mid t_{1},t_{2}\in\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{\times}\}.

Calculations in the root system of
E_{6}
show that

\mathbf{L}_{(1_{\varepsilon})}=\mathbf{G}_{1}\mathbf{G}_{2}\mathbf{S}_{(1_{\varepsilon})},

where
\mathbf{G}_{1}
has base

A_{2}^{(1)}=\{\alpha_{2}+\alpha_{4}+\alpha_{5}+\alpha_{6},\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{3}+\alpha_{4}+\alpha_{5}\}

and
\mathbf{G}_{2}
has base

A_{2}^{(2)}=\{\alpha_{3}+\alpha_{4}+\alpha_{5}+\alpha_{6},\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+\alpha_{3}+\alpha_{4}\}.

Observe that the roots in the sets
A_{1}^{(1)},A_{2}^{(2)}
are orthogonal to
\alpha_{2}+\alpha_{3}+\alpha_{5}
and
\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{0}+\alpha_{6}
. Moreover, the second root in each of theses sets is the image under
v^{\prime}
of the first. In particular,
\mathbf{L}_{(1_{\varepsilon})}^{F}
has rational type
A_{2}(\varepsilon q)^{2}\Phi_{1_{\varepsilon}}^{2}
, as already predicted by Table
1
.

The
2_{\varepsilon}
-split Levi subgroup
\mathbf{L}_{(2_{\varepsilon})}
associated to
s\in(\mathbf{T}^{\ast})^{mF}
is the
2_{\varepsilon}
-split Levi subgroup associated to the
2_{\varepsilon}
-split torus
\mathbf{S}_{(2_{\varepsilon})}:=\{h_{\alpha_{0}+\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{6}}(t)\mid t\in\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{\times}\}
of
(\mathbf{G},mF)
. In particular, we have

\mathbf{L}_{(2_{\varepsilon})}=\mathbf{G}_{1}\mathbf{G}_{2}\mathbf{G}_{3}\mathbf{S}_{(2_{\varepsilon})},

where
\mathbf{G}_{1},\mathbf{G}_{2}
are defined as above and
\mathbf{G}_{3}
is of type
A_{1}
with base
\{\alpha_{4}\}
. Here, the roots of the root system of
[\mathbf{L}_{(2_{\varepsilon})},\mathbf{L}_{(2_{\varepsilon})}]
are orthogonal to the root
\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{0}+\alpha_{6}
. Observe that the element
m
interchanges
\mathbf{G}_{1}
and
\mathbf{G}_{2}
so that the Levi subgroup
\mathbf{L}_{(2_{\varepsilon})}^{mF}
has rational type
A_{2}(q^{2})A_{1}(\varepsilon q)\Phi_{(2_{\varepsilon})}
in accordance with Table
1
.

Summarizing our computations we have thus proved:

Lemma 12.2
.

Assume the notation from above. Then the following hold:

(a)

The Levi subgroup
\mathbf{L}_{(1_{\varepsilon})}
is the
1_{\varepsilon}
-split Levi subgroup associated to the semisimple element
s\in(\mathbf{G}^{\ast})^{F^{\prime}}
.

(b)

The Levi subgroup
({}^{g}\mathbf{L}_{(2_{\varepsilon})})
is the
2_{\varepsilon}
-split Levi subgroup associated to the semisimple element
s\in(\mathbf{G}^{\ast})^{F}
.

As before for
d\in\{1,2\}
we define
L_{(d)}:=\mathbf{L}_{(d)}^{m_{(d)}F}
and
N_{(d)}:=\operatorname{N}_{\mathbf{G}^{v_{(d)}F}}(\mathbf{L}_{(d)},e_{s}^{L_{(d)}})
and
W_{(d)}:=N_{(d)}/L_{(d)}
.

Proposition 12.3
.

The subgroup
V_{(d_{\varepsilon})}=\langle m,n^{d^{2}}\rangle
is a section of
W_{(d_{\varepsilon})}
.
In particular,
V_{(d_{\varepsilon})}
has central intersection

Z_{(d_{\varepsilon})}:=\mathbf{L}_{(d_{\varepsilon})}\cap V_{d_{\varepsilon}}=\langle h_{\alpha_{0}}(-1)h_{\alpha_{1}}(-1)h_{\alpha_{6}}(-1),(h_{\alpha_{2}}(-1)h_{\alpha_{3}}(-1)h_{\alpha_{5}}(-1))^{d}\rangle\subset\mathbf{S}_{(d_{\varepsilon})}

with
\mathbf{L}_{(d_{\varepsilon})}
.

Proof.

From Lemma
1.10
it follows that
V_{(d_{\varepsilon})}
is a section of
W_{(d_{\varepsilon})}
. Moreover, the Chevalley relations
[
Spä06
, Bemerkung 2.1.7(b)]
imply that the unique map
\langle n_{\alpha_{0}}(1),n_{\alpha_{2}}(1)\rangle\to\langle m,n\rangle
with
n_{\alpha_{0}}(1)\mapsto m
and
n_{\alpha_{2}}(1)\mapsto n
is an isomorphism. Observe that
H\cap\langle n_{\alpha_{0}}(1),n_{\alpha_{2}}(1)\rangle=\langle h_{\alpha_{0}}(-1),h_{\alpha_{2}}(-1)\rangle
. The claim follows from this.
∎

As before, for
d\in\{1,2\}
we define
G_{(d)}:=\mathbf{G}^{v_{(d)}F}
.

Remark 12.4
.

By the tables in
[
How80
]
, we have

\operatorname{N}_{G_{(1_{\varepsilon})}}(\mathbf{L}_{(1_{\varepsilon})})/L_{(1_{\varepsilon})}\cong S_{3}\times C_{2},

where the
S_{3}
component centralizes
[\mathbf{L}_{(1_{\varepsilon})},\mathbf{L}_{(1_{\varepsilon})}]
and the central
C_{2}
copy permutes the two simple components of
\mathbf{L}_{(1_{\varepsilon})}
.
As
W_{(1_{\varepsilon})}\cong S_{3}
does not centralize
[\mathbf{L}_{(1_{\varepsilon})},\mathbf{L}_{(1_{\varepsilon})}]
, we observe that
W_{(1_{\varepsilon})}
embedds diagonally in that decomposition of the normalizer.

We now consider the action of the automorphisms in
E
on the semisimple element
s
. Recall that
{}^{w_{0}F_{p}}s=s
if
p\equiv 2\mod 3
while
{}^{F_{p}}s=s
if
p\equiv 1\mod 3
. Moreover,
w_{0}\gamma_{0}
stabilizes
s
. However,
w_{0}\notin\operatorname{C}_{W}(v^{\prime})
and so either
\operatorname{C}_{WE}(v^{\prime},s)=\operatorname{C}_{W(s)}(v^{\prime})\langle F_{p}\gamma_{0}\rangle
or
\operatorname{C}_{WE}(v^{\prime},s)=\operatorname{C}_{W(s)}(v^{\prime})\langle F_{p}\rangle
. This agrees with the computation in the proof of Lemma
8.1
. We set
E^{\prime}:=\operatorname{C}_{WE}(v^{\prime},s)
and deduce that
\operatorname{N}_{G_{(d)}E}(L_{(d)},e_{s}^{L_{(d)}})=N_{(d)}E^{\prime}
.

Lemma 12.5
.

There exists an
E^{\prime}
-equivariant extension map
\Gamma
for
Z_{(d)}\lhd V_{(d)}
.

Proof.

Observe that
E^{\prime}
centralizes
V
. In particular, every extension map for
Z_{(d)}\lhd V_{(d)}
is
E^{\prime}
-equivariant and therefore an equivariant extension map
\Gamma
always exists by
[
Isa06
, Corollary 11.13]
since
V_{(d)}/Z_{(d)}\cong W_{(d)}
has only cyclic Sylow subgroups.
∎

The groups
W_{(1)}
and
W_{(e)}
have a common (
E^{\prime}
-stable) Sylow
2
-subgroup
W_{2}
with
v_{(1)},v_{(e)}\in\operatorname{Z}(W_{2})
. Note that in contrast to the situation of Section
10
the group
W_{2}
is selfnormalizing in
W_{(1)}
and
W_{(e)}
.

Lemma 12.6
.

For
d\in\{1,e\}
there exists an extension map
\Lambda_{(d)}
for
\mathcal{E}_{2}(L_{(d)},s)^{W_{2}}
with respect to
L_{(d)}\lhd N_{(d)}
such that

\Lambda_{(d)}
is
\operatorname{N}_{G_{(d)}E}(L_{(d)},e_{s}^{L_{(d)}})
-equivariant.

Proof.

Observe that it suffices to construct
\Lambda_{(d)}
on an
E^{\prime}
-transversal
\mathcal{T}
of
\mathcal{E}_{2}(L_{(d)},s)^{W_{2}}
and extend it
E^{\prime}
-equivariantly. Hence, for
\psi\in\mathcal{T}
it is enough to construct an extension
\Lambda(\psi)\in\operatorname{Irr}((N_{(d)})_{\psi}\mid\psi)
with the same stabilizer in
E^{\prime}
. Observe furthermore that the Levi subgroup
\mathbf{L}_{(2_{\varepsilon})}
is only relevant, when
q\equiv 3\mod 4
or
\varepsilon=-1
and therefore
q\equiv 2\mod 3
. In both cases
q
is not a square. Hence, the group
E^{\prime}/\langle m_{(2_{\varepsilon})}\hat{F}\rangle
has always odd order. Since
N_{(2_{\varepsilon})}/L_{(2_{\varepsilon})}\cong C_{2}
, an extension map with the desired properties always exists.
We therefore only need to consider the case
d=1_{\varepsilon}
. Let
V_{(d)}
be the section of
W_{(d)}
constructed in Proposition
12.3
.
We can therefore write
N_{(d)}
as
N_{(d)}=L_{(d)}V_{(d)}
. Since
W_{(d)}\cong S_{3}
, any character of
\mathcal{T}
has
2
extensions to its inertia group in
N_{(d)}
. Hence, we may replace
E^{\prime}
without loss of generality by its Sylow
2
-subgroup.
The intersection
Z_{(d)}=L_{(d)}\cap V_{(d)}
is a central subgroup of
L_{(d)}
by Proposition
12.3
. Denote
L_{0}:=[L_{(d)},L_{(d)}]\cong\operatorname{SL}_{3}(q)\times\operatorname{SL}_{3}(q)
. We let
\psi\in\mathcal{E}_{2}(L_{(d)},s)
and fix
\psi_{0}\in\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(L_{0}\mid\psi)
and denote by
\psi^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Irr}((L_{0})_{\psi_{0}}\mid\psi_{0})
the Clifford correspondent of
\psi
. Note that the involutions in
W_{(d)}
act by permuting the two components of
L_{0}
, while the Sylow
3
-subgroup of
W_{(d)}
centralizes
L_{0}
, see also Remark
12.4
. Since
L_{(d)}/\operatorname{Z}(L_{0})L_{0}
is a
2^{\prime}
-group and
E^{\prime}
is a
2
-group it follows that we can choose
\psi_{0}
with the additional property that

(W_{(d)}E^{\prime})_{\psi}\leq(W_{(d)}E^{\prime})_{\psi_{0}}.

Let
\nu\in\operatorname{Irr}(Z_{(d)}\mid\psi)
be the unique character below
\psi
.
We consider the semidirect product
L_{0}\rtimes W_{(d)}
. We have a surjective group homomorphism
L_{0}\rtimes W_{(d)}\to L_{0}\rtimes W_{(d)}/\operatorname{C}_{W_{(d)}}(L_{0})
. Since
W_{(d)}/\operatorname{C}_{W_{(d)}}(L_{0})
acts by permuting the two components of
L_{0}
, we can extend
\psi_{0}
to a representation of
L_{0}\rtimes W_{(d)}
with
W_{(d)}
in its kernel. By
[
BS19
, Proposition 4.2]
, the character
\psi_{0}\Gamma(\nu)\in\operatorname{Irr}(L_{0}V_{(d)}\mid\psi_{0})
is therefore a well-defined character which extends
\psi_{0}
. By Lemma
10.13
there exists a unique character
\hat{\psi}^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Irr}((L_{0}V_{(d)})_{\psi^{\prime}})
extending both
\psi^{\prime}
and
\psi_{0}\Gamma(\nu)
.

We then define
\Lambda(\psi):=\operatorname{Ind}_{(L_{0}V_{(d)})_{\psi^{\prime}}}^{L(V_{(d)})_{\psi}}(\hat{\psi}^{\prime})
. Note that
(V_{(d)})_{\psi}=(V_{(d)})_{\psi^{\prime}}
since
\psi_{0}
is
V_{(d)}
-stable. Hence, the so-constructed character is indeed irreducible. By Mackey’s formula
\Lambda(\psi)
is indeed an extension of
\psi
. We must check that
\Lambda(\psi)
is
E^{\prime}_{\psi}
-invariant. This follows however from the fact that
\psi^{\prime}
,
\nu
are both
E^{\prime}_{\psi}
-invariant and
\Gamma
is an
E^{\prime}_{\psi}
-equivariant extension map.
∎

12.3.
Construction of the AM-bijection

Let
b
be one of the
2
-blocks of
G=\mathbf{G}^{F}
associated to the semisimple element
s\in G^{\ast}
. We let
b_{(d)}
be the block of
G_{(d)}
corresponding to the block
b
of
\mathbf{G}^{F}
under the isomorphism induced by
\iota
. Furthermore, we let
(\mathbf{L}_{(e)},\lambda_{(e)})
be the
e
-cuspidal pair of
(\mathbf{G},m_{(e)}F)
associated to the block
b_{(e)}
of
\mathbf{G}^{m_{(e)}F}
. By the proof of
[
KM13
, Proposition 4.3]
, there exists a unique character
\lambda_{(1)}\in\mathcal{E}(L_{(1)},s)
such that all constituents of
R_{L_{(1)}}^{G_{(1)}}(\lambda_{(1)})
lie in the
2
-block
b_{(1)}
.

Lemma 12.7
.

There exists a natural bijection

\Xi:\operatorname{Irr}(L_{(1)},b_{L_{(1)}}(\lambda_{(1)}))^{W_{2}}\to\operatorname{Irr}(L_{(e)},b_{L_{(e)}}(\lambda_{(e)}))^{W_{2}}.

Proof.

We follow the proof of Lemma
10.11
. For
z\in\{1,m\}
, we obtain again
\operatorname{C}_{W(s)}(z)
-equivariant bijections

\mathcal{E}_{2}(\mathbf{T}^{zF},s)\to A_{z}:=\{t\in(\mathbf{T}^{\ast})^{F^{\ast}z^{\ast}}\mid t_{2^{\prime}}=s\}.

and from this we get again a bijection

\mathcal{E}_{2}(\mathbf{T}^{F},s)^{W_{2}}\to\mathcal{E}_{2}(\mathbf{T}^{zF},s)^{W_{2}}.

Moreover, Deligne–Lusztig induction yields for
d\in\{1,2\}
a bijection
b_{L}(\lambda_{(d)})R_{T_{(d)}(s)}^{L_{(d)}}:\mathcal{E}_{2}(T_{(d)},s)\to\operatorname{Irr}(L_{(d)},b_{L_{(d)}}(\lambda_{(d)}))
, see the proof of Lemma
6.5
. The composition is again the sought bijection.
∎

Theorem 12.8
.

Let
b
be one of the blocks of Table
1
associated to the
e
-cuspidal pair
(\mathbf{L},\lambda)
and
B
the unique block of
\operatorname{N}_{G}(\mathbf{L})
covering
b_{L}(\lambda)
. Then there exists an
\operatorname{N}_{\tilde{G}\mathcal{B}}(\mathbf{L})_{B}
-equivariant bijection
f:\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(\operatorname{N}_{G}(\mathbf{L}),B)\to\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(G,b)
.
In particular, the block
b
is AM-good.

Proof.

The proof is similar to the proof of Theorem
10.17
. We will therefore only sketch the proof with emphasis on the differences to the proof of Theorem
10.17
.
We first parametrize the local characters.
Let
\Lambda^{(e)}
be the extension map associated to
L_{(e)}\lhd N_{(e)}
from Lemma
12.6
.
Again we have a parametrization

\Pi:\mathcal{P}=\{(\mu,\eta)\mid\mu\in\operatorname{Irr}(L_{(e)},b_{L_{(e)}}(\lambda{(e)})),\eta\in\operatorname{Irr}(W_{(e)}(\mu))\}\to\operatorname{Irr}(N_{(e)},b_{L_{(e)}}(\lambda_{e}))

with
\Pi(\mu,\eta)=\operatorname{Ind}_{N_{(e)}(\mu)}^{N_{(e)}}(\Lambda_{(e)}(\mu)\eta)
. Observe that
W_{2}
is self-normalizing in
W_{(e)}
and
\mathcal{E}_{2}(T_{(e)},s)^{W_{2}}
is in bijection with
\operatorname{Irr}(L_{(e)},b_{L_{(e)}}(\lambda_{(e)}))^{W_{2}}
. A count of the height zero characters therefore gives
|\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(N_{(e)},b_{L_{(e)}}(\lambda))|=(q-\varepsilon)_{2}2
.

Hence, every height zero character is of the form
\chi=R_{L_{(1)}}^{G_{(1)}}(\mu_{0})_{\eta_{0}}
for some
\mu_{0}\in\mathcal{E}_{2}(L_{(1)},b_{L_{(1)}}(\lambda))
and
\eta_{0}\in\operatorname{Irr}(W_{(1)}(\mu_{0}))
, see Corollary
3.2
. For such a character to have height zero we must by Corollary
3.3
necessarily have that
\mu_{0}
is of height zero with
2\nmid|W_{(1)}:W_{(1)}(\mu_{0})|
and
\eta_{0}\in\operatorname{Irr}_{2^{\prime}}(W_{(1)}(\mu_{0}))
.

By Lemma
12.7
we have a bijection

\Xi:\mathcal{E}_{2}(L_{(e)},s)^{W_{2}}\to\mathcal{E}_{2}(L_{(1)},s)^{W_{2}}.

For
(\mu,\eta)\in\mathcal{P}
a pair with
W_{2}\leq W_{(e)}(\mu)
and
\mu_{0}:=\Xi(\mu)
,
we have again an
E^{\prime}_{\mu}
-equivariant McKay-bijection

f_{\mu}:\operatorname{Irr}_{2^{\prime}}(W_{(e)}(\mu))\to\operatorname{Irr}_{2^{\prime}}(W_{(1)}(\mu_{0})).

Denote by
B_{(e)}
the unique block of
N_{(e)}
covering
b_{L}(\lambda_{(e)})
. By Proposition
10.15
we obtain a bijection

\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(N_{(e)},B_{(e)})\to\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(G_{(1)},b_{(1)}),\quad\operatorname{Ind}_{N_{(e)}(\mu)}^{N_{(e)}}(\Lambda^{(e)}(\mu)\eta)\mapsto R_{L_{(1)}}^{G_{(1)}}(\Xi(\mu))_{f_{\mu}(\eta)}.

Again, we need to check that the so-obtained map is well-defined.
For this, observe that the number of height zero characters in the block
b
is equal to the number of height zero characters in the principal block of
A_{2}(\varepsilon q^{3})
, see Lemma
8.4
. By
[
BR21
, Lemma 3.8]
, the latter number is equal to
(q-\varepsilon)_{2}2
. Comparing this with the number of height zero characters of the block
B
obtained above, we deduce that
|\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(B)|=|\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(b)|
. In addition, the map is injective by the properties of Harish-Chandra series and its image contains all height zero characters by the arguments from above. Therefore, the map is necessarily a well-defined bijection and yields by applying
\iota
a bijection

f:\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(\operatorname{N}_{G}(\mathbf{L}),B)\to\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(G,b)
. It is left to check that
f
is
\operatorname{N}_{\tilde{G}\mathcal{B}}(\mathbf{L},B)=\operatorname{N}_{G\mathcal{B}}(\mathbf{L},B)
-equivariant. But this is again checked as in Theorem
10.17
. Hence, the block
b
is AM-good by Proposition
9.6
.
∎

12.4.
Some more information on defect groups

We now turn to the description of defect groups in case (2) or (8) of Table
1
. We keep the notation from the beginning of this section. We denote
F^{\prime}:=Fm_{(e)}
, where the Frobenius
F
and
m_{(e)}\in V^{F}
are defined as before. Let
s\in(\mathbf{T}^{\ast})^{F^{\prime}}
the semisimple element with centralizer
A_{2}(\varepsilon q^{3}).3
from before and
b
one of the blocks of
\mathbf{G}^{F^{\prime}}
associated to
s
. By Theorem
4.5
, the group
P^{\prime}=\mathbf{T}_{2}^{F^{\prime}}\langle m\rangle
is therefore a Sylow
2
-subgroup of
\mathbf{G}(s)^{F^{\prime}}
.
Let
D^{\prime}:=\mathbf{T}_{2}^{F^{\prime}}
and
\mathbf{M}:=\operatorname{C}_{\mathbf{L}}(D^{\prime})
. Using
[
CE04
, Lemma 13.17]
we obtain
\mathbf{M}=\mathbf{G}_{1}\mathbf{G}_{2}\mathbf{T}
in both cases. We use the description of Lemma
1.11
to see that one of the blocks
b_{D^{\prime}}
of
e_{s}^{\mathbf{M}^{F^{\prime}}}
associated to
s
is a
b
-Brauer pair. Finally, observe that the unique character
\lambda^{\prime}\in\mathcal{E}({\mathbf{M}}^{F^{\prime}},s)\cap\operatorname{Irr}(\mathbf{M}^{F^{\prime}},b_{D^{\prime}})
is the canonical character associated to the self-centralizing Brauer pair
(D^{\prime},b_{D^{\prime}})
and that
D^{\prime}=\operatorname{Z}(\mathbf{M})_{2}^{F^{\prime}}
.

We need the following representation theoretic lemma:

Lemma 12.9
.

Let
H_{0}
be a normal subgroup of a finite group
H
and
U\leq H_{0}
with
\operatorname{Z}(U)\cong C_{3}
and
\operatorname{Z}(U)\leq\operatorname{Z}(H)
. Let
Z\subset\operatorname{Z}(H)
and
\chi\in\operatorname{Irr}(H)
with
\operatorname{Res}_{U}^{H}(\chi)=\sum_{\phi\in\operatorname{Irr}(U)}a_{\phi}\phi
. Then

(a)

\operatorname{Res}^{U}_{\operatorname{Z}(U)}(\phi)\phi(1)=\operatorname{Res}^{H}_{\operatorname{Z}(U)}(\chi)\chi(1)
for all
\phi\in\operatorname{Irr}(U)
with
a_{\phi}\neq 0
.

(b)

\operatorname{Res}_{UZ}^{H}(\chi)=\sum_{\phi\in\operatorname{Irr}(U)}a_{\phi}\hat{\phi}
, where
\hat{\phi}
is the unique extension of
\phi
with
\operatorname{Res}^{U}_{Z\operatorname{Z}(U)}(\phi)\hat{\phi}(1)=\operatorname{Res}^{H}_{Z\operatorname{Z}(U)}(\chi)\hat{\chi}(1)

Proof.

Let
1\neq z\in\operatorname{Z}(U)
. Assume first that
\chi(z)=n\omega
for
n=\chi(1)
and a primitive third root of unity
\omega
. For
\xi\in\{1,\omega^{\pm 1}\}
denote by
a_{\xi}
the sum of degrees of constituents
\phi
of the restriction
\operatorname{Res}^{H}_{U}(\chi)
with
\phi(z)=\phi(1)\xi
. We obtain
n=\chi(1)=a_{1}+a_{\omega}+a_{\omega^{2}}
and
\omega n=a_{1}+a_{\omega}\omega+a_{\omega^{2}}\omega^{2}
. Since
-1-\omega=\omega^{2}
the second equation rewrites as
0=(a_{1}-a_{\omega^{2}})+(a_{\omega}-a_{\omega^{2}}-n)\omega
. This forces
a_{1}-a_{\omega^{2}}=0
and
a_{\omega}-a_{\omega^{2}}-n=0
which together with the first equation implies
a_{1}=a_{\omega^{2}}=0
. Hence, in the restriction of
\chi
only characters with the same character on the center appear.

If on the other hand
\operatorname{Z}(U)
is in the kernel of
\chi
, then we obtain the two equations
n=a_{1}+a_{\omega}+a_{\omega^{2}}
and
n=a_{1}+a_{\omega}\omega+a_{\omega^{2}}\omega^{2}
. This forces again
a_{\omega}=a_{\omega^{2}}=0
. This shows part (a) also in this case.

Part (b) is now obtained by comparing the character values on
Z
on both sides.
∎

Proposition 12.10
.

There exists a character
\zeta^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Irr}(\operatorname{C}_{G}(P^{\prime})\mid\zeta)
which appears with odd multiplicity in
\zeta
and such that
\zeta^{\prime}(1)_{2}=|\operatorname{C}_{G}(P^{\prime}):\operatorname{Z}(D^{\prime})\cap\operatorname{C}_{G}(P^{\prime})|_{2}
.

Proof.

We start by examining the right hand side of the equality in the proposition. We note that
\operatorname{Z}(D^{\prime})\cap\operatorname{C}_{G}(P^{\prime})=\operatorname{Z}(P^{\prime})
, as
\operatorname{Z}(P^{\prime})\leq D^{\prime}
. Denote
M:=\mathbf{M}^{F^{\prime}}
. We observe that
\operatorname{C}_{[M,M]}(P^{\prime})\operatorname{Z}(P^{\prime})
has odd index in
\operatorname{C}_{M}(P^{\prime})
. This is because
M/[M,M]\operatorname{Z}(M)
,
[M,M]\cap\operatorname{Z}(M)
are both of odd order and
\operatorname{Z}(M)_{2}=D^{\prime}
.

Moreover, we have
\operatorname{C}_{[M,M]}(P^{\prime})\operatorname{Z}(P^{\prime})/\operatorname{Z}(P^{\prime})\cong\operatorname{C}_{[M,M]}(P^{\prime})/\operatorname{C}_{[M,M]}(P^{\prime})\cap\operatorname{Z}(P^{\prime})

and
\operatorname{C}_{[M,M]}(P^{\prime})\cap\operatorname{Z}(P^{\prime})\leq\operatorname{Z}(\operatorname{C}_{[M,M]}(P^{\prime}))
which is a
2^{\prime}
-group. Hence, we need to find a constituent with degree
\zeta^{\prime}(1)_{2}=|\operatorname{C}_{[M,M]}(P^{\prime})|_{2}
.

We first consider case (2).
Let
\zeta_{0}=\chi\times\chi\in\operatorname{Irr}([M,M]\mid\zeta)
. Our aim is to construct a constituent
\zeta_{0}^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Irr}(\operatorname{C}_{[M,M]}(P^{\prime})\mid\zeta_{0})
with odd multiplicity. The subgroup
\operatorname{C}_{[M,M]}(P^{\prime})\cong\operatorname{SL}_{3}(q)
embedds diagonally into
[M,M]\cong\operatorname{SL}_{3}(q)\times\operatorname{SL}_{3}(q)
. Hence, the constituents of the restriction of a character
\zeta=\chi\times\chi\in\operatorname{Irr}([M,M])
are precisely the constituents
\zeta^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Irr}(\mathrm{SL}_{3}(q))
of the character
\chi^{2}\in\operatorname{Irr}(\mathrm{SL}_{3}(q))
. Here, the character
\chi
is a character of degree
\frac{1}{3}\Phi_{1}(q)^{2}\Phi_{2}(q)
of
\operatorname{SL}_{3}(q)
, lying in a Lusztig series with centralizer of type
\Phi_{3}(q).3
. Also observe that the diagonal automorphisms induced by
M
on
[M,M]\cong\operatorname{SL}_{3}(q)\times\operatorname{SL}_{3}(q)
are the ones induced by the diagonal action of
\Delta\operatorname{GL}_{3}(q)
. It can now be checked with CHEVIE
[
GHL+96
]
, that there exists a unique semisimple character
\zeta_{0}^{\prime}
of degree
\frac{1}{3}\Phi_{1}(q)^{2}\Phi_{2}(q)
which appears as a constituent (of odd multiplicity) in
\zeta_{0}
.

Denote
C:=\operatorname{C}_{M}(P^{\prime})
and observe that
C
induces all diagonal automorphisms of
M
on
[M,M]
. We let
\hat{\zeta}\in\operatorname{Irr}(M_{\zeta_{0}}\mid\zeta_{0})
be the Clifford correspondent of
\zeta
and we denote by
\hat{\zeta}_{0}^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Irr}(C_{\zeta_{0}}\mid\zeta_{0}^{\prime})
the character with the same values as
\zeta_{0}
on
\operatorname{Z}(M)\cap C
as in Lemma
12.9
. Denote
\zeta^{\prime}:=\operatorname{Ind}_{C_{\zeta_{0}}}^{C}(\hat{\zeta}_{0}^{\prime})
. Observe that
M=M_{\zeta_{0}}C
and
C_{\zeta_{0}}=C_{\zeta_{0}^{\prime}}
. Hence, for the scalar product of
\zeta
and
\zeta^{\prime}
, we have by Mackey’s formula

\langle\zeta,\zeta^{\prime}\rangle=\langle\operatorname{Res}_{C}^{M}\operatorname{Ind}_{M_{\zeta_{0}}}^{M}(\hat{\zeta}_{0}),\operatorname{Ind}_{C_{\zeta_{0}^{\prime}}}^{C}(\hat{\zeta}_{0}^{\prime})\rangle=\langle\operatorname{Ind}_{C_{\zeta_{0}^{\prime}}}^{C}(\operatorname{Res}_{C_{\zeta_{0}}}^{M_{\zeta_{0}}}(\hat{\zeta}_{0})),\operatorname{Ind}_{C_{\zeta_{0}^{\prime}}}^{C}(\hat{\zeta}_{0}^{\prime})\rangle.

By construction, the character
\hat{\zeta}_{0}^{\prime}
is the unique character in its
C
-orbit that appears with non-zero multiplicity in
\operatorname{Res}_{C_{\zeta_{0}}}^{M_{\zeta_{0}}}(\hat{\zeta}_{0})
. Hence, by Clifford correspondence, we have
\langle\zeta,\zeta^{\prime}\rangle=\langle\operatorname{Res}_{C_{\zeta_{0}}}^{M_{\zeta_{0}}}(\hat{\zeta}_{0}),\zeta^{\prime}_{0}\rangle
, which is odd.

Let us now consider case (8). Note that here
M=[M,M]\operatorname{Z}(M)
. Let
\zeta_{0}=\operatorname{Res}_{[M,M]}^{M}(\zeta)
. This character of
[M,M]\cong\operatorname{SL}_{3}(q^{2})
lies in a Lusztig series with centralizer of type
\Phi_{3}(q^{2}).3
. Moreover, it is non-trivial on the center and has degree
\frac{1}{3}\Phi_{1}(q^{2})^{2}\Phi_{2}(q^{2})
. We now consider its restriction to the centralizer
\operatorname{C}_{[M,M]}(P^{\prime})\cong\operatorname{SL}_{3}(q)
.

We let
x\in\operatorname{SL}_{3}(q)
such that
x
is conjugate to the diagonal matrix
\mathrm{diag}(\tau,\tau^{q},\tau^{q^{2}})
with
\tau\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{3}}^{\times}
of order
q^{2}+q+1
. By
[
SF73
]
,
\zeta_{0}(x)
is a third root of unity. Moreover in the restriction of
\zeta_{0}
to
\operatorname{SL}_{3}(q)
by
[
SF73
]
and Lemma
12.9
only characters
\psi
of degree
\psi(1)_{2}=(\Phi_{1}^{2}(q)\Phi_{2}(q))_{2}
contribute to
\zeta(x)
(as all the other characters either vanish on
x
or are trivial on the center). If all these characters were to appear with even multiplicity in the restriction of
\zeta_{0}
, then
\zeta_{0}(x)/2
would be an algebraic integer. This is however not the cases as
\zeta_{0}(x)
is a third root of unity. Hence, there exists a constituent with odd multiplicity as required. We conclude with Lemma
12.9
.
∎

Corollary 12.11
.

Let
b
be one of the blocks of
G
associated to case (2) or (8) in
1
and
P^{\prime}
a Sylow
2
-subgroup of
G(s)
. Then there exists a maximal
b
-Brauer pair
(P^{\prime},b_{P^{\prime}})
with
(D^{\prime},b_{D^{\prime}})\leq(P^{\prime},b_{P^{\prime}})
. Moreover, the pair
(D^{\prime},P^{\prime})
is conjugate to the pair
(D,P)
from
[
KM13
, Theorem 1.2]
.

Proof.

Let
b
be associated to the quasi-central
e
-cuspidal pair
(\mathbf{L},\lambda)
with
\lambda\in\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{L}^{F^{\prime}},s)
and
b_{P}^{\prime}
be the block associated to the character
\zeta^{\prime}
constructed in Proposition
12.10
.
Hence,
[
CE04
, Proposition 22.14]
is applicable and we obtain that
(P^{\prime},b_{P^{\prime}})
is a
b
-Brauer pair. Since
|P^{\prime}|=|\mathbf{G}(s)^{F^{\prime}}|_{2}
it follows that
(P^{\prime},b_{P^{\prime}})
is a maximal
b
-Brauer pair.

Comparing
(D^{\prime},P^{\prime})
with the description of the tuple
(D,P)
from
[
KM13
, Proposition 2.7]
shows again that these are
\mathbf{G}^{F^{\prime}}
-conjugate.
∎

13.
Proof of the inductive condition for non-maximal unipotent blocks

In this section, we consider the remaining non-maximal unipotent blocks. In particular,
b=b_{G}(\mathbf{L},\lambda)
is a unipotent block of
G
associated to a unipotent
e
-cuspidal pair
(\mathbf{L},\lambda)
of central defect.

Lemma 13.1
.

Let
b=b_{G}(\mathbf{L},\lambda)
be a unipotent non-maximal block of
G
associated to a unipotent
e
-cuspidal pair
(\mathbf{L},\lambda)
of central defect. Moreover, let
\mathbf{H}\in\{\mathbf{L},\mathbf{G}\}
and
\mu\in\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{H},1)
a unipotent character. Then
\mu
extends to
\mathrm{N}_{\tilde{G}\mathcal{B}}(\mathbf{H})
.

Proof.

By
[
CE04
, Proposition 15.9]
, the character
\mu\in\mathcal{E}(H,1)
restricts irreducibly to a character
\mu_{0}
of
H_{0}
, where
\mathbf{H}_{0}=[\mathbf{H},\mathbf{H}]
. Note that by the classification of Lemma
7.1
, the group
\mathbf{H}_{0}
is always simple of simply connected type and we have an injective map
H_{0}/\operatorname{Z}(H_{0})\hookrightarrow\mathbf{H}_{\operatorname{ad}}^{F}
, which factors through
H/\operatorname{Z}(H)
.
Since
\mu_{0}
is unipotent, we can consider it as a character of
S:=H_{0}/\operatorname{Z}(H_{0})
, which is a non-abelian simple group. Now, the group
N:=\operatorname{N}_{\tilde{G}\mathcal{B}}(\mathbf{H})
acts by automorphisms on
S
. Hence, we obtain
N/\operatorname{C}_{N}(S)\cong A
, where
S\leq A\leq\mathrm{Aut}(S)
. By
[
Mal08
, Theorem 2.4, Theorem 2.5]
and Lemma
7.1
the unipotent character
\mu_{0}\in\operatorname{Irr}(S)
extends to
\mathrm{Aut}(S)
. Therefore, the character
\mu_{0}
extends to a character
\hat{\mu}\in\operatorname{Irr}(N)
, which is trivial on
\operatorname{C}_{N}(S)
. Moreover, by the proof of
[
Mal08
, Theorem 2.4, Theorem 2.5]
, the so-obtained extension
\hat{\mu}
is an extension of
\mu
.
∎

Let
\mathbf{T}
be an
F
-stable maximal torus of
\mathbf{L}
. As
\mathbf{L}^{F}=\mathbf{T}^{F}[\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L}]^{F}
we can consider
\operatorname{Irr}(\mathbf{L}^{F}/[\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L}]^{F})
as a subgroup of
\operatorname{Irr}(\mathbf{T}^{F})
. By duality we obtain a bijection
(\mathbf{T}^{\ast})^{F^{\ast}}\to\operatorname{Irr}(\mathbf{T}^{F})
which by
[
CE04
, Equation 8.19]
restricts to a bijection

\operatorname{Irr}(\operatorname{Z}(\mathbf{L}^{\ast})^{F^{\ast}})\to\operatorname{Irr}(\mathbf{L}^{F}/[\mathbf{L},\mathbf{L}]^{F}),\quad z\mapsto\hat{z}.

With this in mind, we can formulate the next lemma.

Lemma 13.2
.

Let
\mu=\hat{z}\in\operatorname{Irr}_{(}L)
be a linear character.

(a)

The character
\mu
extends to its stabilizer in
\operatorname{N}_{G\mathcal{B}}(\mathbf{L})
.

(b)

Any extension of
\mu
to
\operatorname{N}_{G}(\mathbf{L})_{\mu}
is
\tilde{L}
-stable if and only if
\mathrm{N}_{\operatorname{C}_{G^{\ast}}(z)}(\mathbf{L}^{\ast})=\operatorname{C}^{\circ}_{G^{\ast}}(z)
.

Proof.

As
\mathbf{L}
is an
e
-split Levi subgroup it follows that
\mathbf{L}
is the centralizer of an
e
-split torus
\mathbf{S}_{0}
of
\mathbf{G}
. Consequently,
\mathbf{S}_{0}
is contained in a Sylow
e
-torus
\mathbf{S}
of
\mathbf{G}
. Its centralizer
\mathbf{T}:=\operatorname{C}_{\mathbf{G}}(\mathbf{S})
is by
[
KM15
, Lemma 3.17]
a maximal torus of
\mathbf{G}
and by construction it centralizes
\mathbf{S}_{0}
. In particular,
\mathbf{T}
is a maximal torus of
\mathbf{L}
. Consider the restriction
\mu_{0}:=\operatorname{Res}_{\mathbf{T}^{F}}^{\mathbf{L}^{F}}(\mu)\in\operatorname{Irr}(\mathbf{T}^{F})
of
\mu
. By
[
MS16
, Theorem 3.1]
, the character
\mu_{0}
extends to a character
\mu_{1}\in\operatorname{Irr}(\operatorname{N}_{G\mathcal{B}}(\mathbf{T})_{\mu_{0}})
. Since all Sylow
e
-tori of
\mathbf{L}
are
L
-conjugate by
[
MT11
, Theorem 25.11]
, it follows that
\operatorname{N}_{G\mathcal{B}}(\mathbf{L})=\mathbf{L}^{F}\operatorname{N}_{G\mathcal{B}}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{T})
.
From this we conclude that
\mu
extends to
\operatorname{N}_{G\mathcal{B}}(\mathbf{L})_{\mu}
by Lemma
10.13
.

For part (b), let
\hat{z}^{\prime}
be an extension of
\hat{z}
to its inertia group in
\operatorname{N}_{G}(\mathbf{L})
and fix
t\in\tilde{L}
with
\tilde{L}=\langle L,t\rangle
. Observe that by
[
CS13
, Lemma 5.8 (b)]
there exists a character
\nu\in\operatorname{Irr}(\operatorname{N}_{G}(\mathbf{L})_{\hat{z}})
with
{}^{t}\hat{z}^{\prime}=\hat{z}^{\prime}\nu
, whose kernel is equal to
\mathrm{ker}(\nu)=\operatorname{N}_{G}(\mathbf{L})_{\widehat{\tilde{z}}}
, where
\tilde{z}\in\operatorname{Z}(\tilde{L}^{\ast})
satisfies
\iota^{\ast}(\tilde{z})=z
. By Clifford theory, the extension
\hat{z}^{\prime}
is
\tilde{L}
-stable if and only if
\operatorname{N}_{G}(\mathbf{L})_{\hat{z}}=\operatorname{N}_{G}(\mathbf{L})_{\widehat{\tilde{z}}}
. By Proposition
1.6
, the subgroup
W_{G}(\mathbf{L})_{\hat{z}}
corresponds via duality to the subgroup
\mathrm{N}_{\operatorname{C}_{G^{\ast}}(z)}(\mathbf{L}^{\ast})/L^{\ast}
and for
w\in W_{G}(\mathbf{L})_{\hat{z}}
we have
w\in W_{G}(\mathbf{L})_{\widehat{\tilde{z}}}
if and only if
w^{\ast}\in\operatorname{C}^{\circ}_{G^{\ast}}(z)=\iota^{\ast}(\operatorname{C}^{\circ}_{\tilde{G}^{\ast}}(\tilde{z}))
.
∎

Using the previous two statements we can now parametrize the height zero characters of a local block associated to
b
. The character
\lambda
is of central defect and so the
block
b_{L}(\lambda)
is nilpotent with defect group
\operatorname{Z}(L)_{2}
. We deduce that

\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(L,b_{L}(\lambda))=\{\lambda\hat{z}\mid z\in\operatorname{Z}(\mathbf{L}^{\ast})_{2}^{F}\}.

For each
\mu=\lambda\hat{z}
choose a character
\Lambda(\mu)
of
\operatorname{N}_{G}(L,{\mu})
extending it (such a character always exists whenever the Sylow subgroups of
W_{G}(L,\lambda)
are cyclic; for the remaining cases this follows from Lemma
13.1
and Lemma
13.2
)
According to (the proof of) Lemma
6.6
(ii) there exists a unique block
B
of
\operatorname{N}_{G}(\mathbf{L})
covering
b_{L}(\lambda)
.
By Clifford theory, we obtain

\operatorname{Irr}(\operatorname{N}_{G}(\mathbf{L}),B)=\{\operatorname{Ind}_{{\operatorname{N}_{G}(\mathbf{L},\lambda)_{\eta}}}^{\operatorname{N}_{G}(\mathbf{L})}(\Lambda(\mu)\eta)\mid\eta\in\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(W_{G}(\mathbf{L},\lambda)_{\mu})\text{ and }\mu\in\operatorname{Irr}(L,b_{L}(\lambda))\}.

We deduce that a character
\operatorname{Ind}_{{\operatorname{N}_{G}(\mathbf{L},\lambda)_{\eta}}}^{\operatorname{N}_{G}(\mathbf{L})}(\Lambda(\mu)\eta)
has height zero if and only if the inertia group of
\mu
in
W_{G}(\mathbf{L},\lambda)
contains a Sylow
2
-subgroup of
W_{G}(\mathbf{L},\lambda)
. Fix a Sylow
2
-subgroup
W_{2}
of
W_{G}(\mathbf{L},\lambda)
and observe that
W_{2}
is self-normalizing in
W_{G}(\mathbf{L},\lambda)
. Therefore, we obtain

\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(\operatorname{N}_{G}(\mathbf{L}),B)=\{\operatorname{Ind}_{{\operatorname{N}_{G}(\mathbf{L},\lambda)_{\eta}}}^{\operatorname{N}_{G}(\mathbf{L})}(\Lambda(\mu)\eta)\mid\eta\in\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(W_{G}(\mathbf{L},\lambda)_{\mu})\text{ and }\mu\in\operatorname{Irr}(L,b_{L}(\lambda))^{W_{2}}\},

where every character in the set on the right-hand side appears exactly once.

Lemma 13.3
.

Let
B
be the unique block of
\operatorname{N}_{G}(\mathbf{L})
covering
b_{L}(\lambda)
. Then the number of height zero characters in
B
for the unipotent blocks in Lemma
7.1
is as follows:

(i)

|\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(B)|=|P/P^{\prime}|=4
;

(ii)

|\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(B)|=|P/P^{\prime}|=8
.

Proof.

As already observed in Lemma
7.4
the defect groups in case (i) are dihedral and the equality follows again from
[
Sam14
, Theorem 8.1]
or by a similar argument as in case (ii).
Suppose therefore that we are in case (ii): The action of
W_{G^{\ast}}(\mathbf{L}^{\ast})\cong S_{3}\times C_{2}
on
\operatorname{Z}(L^{\ast})=\Phi_{1}^{2}
is described in the proof of
[
KM13
, Proposition 6.4]
. We may therefore choose a Sylow
2
-subgroup
W_{2}\cong C_{2}\times C_{2}
of
W_{G^{\ast}}(\mathbf{L}^{\ast})
such that
C_{2}\leq S_{3}
permutes the two copies of
\Phi_{1}
and the central subgroup
C_{2}
of
W_{G}(\mathbf{L})
acts on
\operatorname{Z}(\mathbf{L})_{2}^{F}
by inversion. The only characters
\mu=\lambda\hat{z}\in\operatorname{Irr}(L,b_{L}(\lambda))
which are therefore fixed under
W_{2}
(isomorphic to
C_{2}\times C_{2}
) are the ones with
z=\pm(1,1)\in\operatorname{Z}(L^{\ast})\cong\mathbb{F}_{q}^{\times}\times\mathbb{F}_{q}^{\times}
. Both of these characters contribute four height zero characters to the block
B
. Hence, there are
8
height zero characters in total.
∎

Proposition 13.4
.

Assume that we are either in case (i) or (ii) of Lemma
7.1
. Then there exists an
\operatorname{N}_{G\mathcal{B}}(\mathbf{L})_{B}
-equivariant bijection
\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(\operatorname{N}_{G}(\mathbf{L}),B)\to\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(G,b)
. Moreover, the block
b
is AM-good.

Proof.

Let us first describe the action of automorphisms on the height zero characters of the block
b
. In case (i) the same reasoning applies as in
[
Ruh21
]
. In particular, the two unipotent characters are invariant under
\mathrm{Out}(G)
and the two non-unipotent characters are permuted by the diagonal automorphisms, see also Lemma
13.2
(b). In case (ii) we note that by Example
1.3
all 8 characters are necessarily stable under all automorphisms of
G
.

Let us now consider the action of automorphisms on the height zero characters of the block
B
. We claim that every height zero character
\psi\in\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(\operatorname{N}_{G}(\mathbf{L}),B)
extends to its inertia group in
\operatorname{N}_{\tilde{G}\mathcal{B}}(\mathbf{L})
.
We first observe that
\lambda
has an extension
\hat{\lambda}
to
\operatorname{N}_{\tilde{G}\mathcal{B}}(\mathbf{L})
by Lemma
13.1
. It therefore remains to describe extensions of the characters
\psi\in\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(\operatorname{N}_{G}(\mathbf{L}),B)
which cover the character
\mu=\lambda\hat{z}
, with
z\in\operatorname{Z}(\mathbf{L})_{2}^{F}
of order
2
as described in the proof of Lemma
13.3
. First note that by Lemma
13.2
, the character
\hat{z}
extend to a character
\hat{z}^{\prime}
in its inertia group in
\operatorname{N}_{G\mathcal{B}}(\mathbf{L})
. In case (i) note that we have
\operatorname{C}_{\mathbf{G}^{\ast}}(z)=(E_{6}(q)\Phi_{1}).2
.
Therefore, by Lemma
13.2
any such extension
\hat{z}^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Irr}(\operatorname{N}_{G}(\mathbf{L}))
of the character
\hat{z}
to
\operatorname{N}_{G}(\mathbf{L})
is not invariant under diagonal automorphisms. In particular, we have
\operatorname{N}_{\tilde{G}\mathcal{B}}(\mathbf{L})_{\psi}=\operatorname{N}_{G\mathcal{B}}(\mathbf{L})_{\psi}
. Since
\operatorname{N}_{G\mathcal{B}}(\mathbf{L})_{\psi}/\operatorname{N}_{G}(\mathbf{L})
is cyclic, the character
\psi
thus extends to its inertia group,

We now discuss the underlying central characters. Note that in case (ii),
\operatorname{Z}(G)=1
so there is nothing to do. In case (i) the two unipotent characters in
\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(G,b)
(resp. in
\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(\operatorname{N}_{G}(\mathbf{L}),B)
) have
\operatorname{Z}(G)
in their kernel. Moreover, for
1\neq z\in G^{\ast}
with
\mathcal{E}(G,z)\cap\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(G,b)\neq\emptyset
we have
z\in[G^{\ast},G^{\ast}]
by
[
Lüb
]
and thus, the characters in
\mathcal{E}(G,z)
and
\mathcal{E}(L,z)
have
\operatorname{Z}(G)
in their kernel as well.

By the description of automorphisms it is now clear how to construct an
\operatorname{N}_{\tilde{G}\mathcal{B}}(\mathbf{L})_{B}
-equivariant bijection
\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(\operatorname{N}_{G}(\mathbf{L}),B)\to\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(G,b)
. The block
b
is therefore AM-good by Lemma
9.6
.
∎

14.
Proof of the main theorem

14.1.
Navarro–Späth’s Theorem

Recall that a group
X
is said to be involved in a finite group
Y
, if there exist
H\lhd K\leq Y
such that
X\cong K/H
.

We recall
[
NS14
, Theorem 7.1]
. In the statement of the theorem,
\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(Y\mid P)
denotes the set of height zero characters in a block of
Y
with defect group
P
.

Theorem 14.1
.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be a collection of finite non-abelian simple groups of order divisible
by
\ell
that satisfy the inductive AM-condition for the prime
\ell
. Assume that
N
is a finite group such that all the non-abelian simple groups involved in
N

of order divisible by
\ell
are in
\mathcal{S}
. Suppose that
N\lhd H
for some finite group
H
, and let
P

be any
\ell
-subgroup of
N
. Then there exists an
\operatorname{N}_{H}(P)
-equivariant bijection

\Pi:\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(N\mid P)\to\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(\operatorname{N}_{N}(P)\mid P)

with

(H_{\tau},N,\tau)\geq_{b}(\operatorname{N}_{H}(P)_{\Pi(\tau)},\operatorname{N}_{N}(P),\Pi(\tau))

for every
\tau\in\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(N\mid P)
.

Definition 14.2
.

Let
S
be a finite non-abelian simple group. We say that
S
is a minimal counterexample if
S
is not AM-good (with respect to the prime
\ell
) but all finite simple groups
S^{\prime}
with
|S^{\prime}|<|S|
are AM-good (with respect to the prime
\ell
).

We use the following consequence of Proposition
14.1
. This will become important in the proof of Theorem
14.6
.

Corollary 14.3
.

Let
b
be a block of the universal covering group
G
of a finite simple group
S
of Lie type and assume that all finite simple groups
S^{\prime}
with
|S^{\prime}|<|S|
are AM-good. Then the following are equivalent:

(i)

The block
b
is AM-good relative to the normalizer of its defect group.

(ii)

The block
b
is AM-good.

Proof.

Clearly (i) implies (ii). So we need to show that if
b
is AM-good, then it is also AM-good relative to the normalizer of its defect group
P
. By the assumption of (ii), there exists a strong iAM-bijection
\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(G,b)\to\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(M,B)
for some suitable
\mathrm{Aut}(G)_{b}
-stable subgroup
M
with
\operatorname{N}_{G}(P)\leq M
. Now by assumption all simple non-abelian groups involved in
M
are AM-good. Therefore, by Theorem
14.1
there exist an iAM-bijection
\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(M,B)\to\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(\operatorname{N}_{G}(P),B_{P})
. In particular, the composition
\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(G,b)\to\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(\operatorname{N}_{G}(P),B_{P})
is again an iAM-bijection, see
[
NS14
, Lemma 3.8(a)]
. By
[
Ruh22
, Remark 3.15]
, the block
b
is therefore AM-good relative to the normalizer of its defect group.
∎

14.2.
Proof of the inductive conditions

Proposition 14.4
.

Let
b
be a strictly quasi-isolated
2
-block of an exceptional group of Lie type
G
as in Section
2
which is not associated to a semisimple element with centralizer
A_{2}(\pm q^{3}).3
or
{}^{3}D_{4}(\pm q)
in
E_{6}(\pm q)
. Assume that
\mathrm{Out}(G)_{b}
is cyclic and assume that
S:=G/\mathrm{Z}(G)
is a minimal counterexample. Then the block
b
is AM-good.

Proof.

We use the criterion of Lemma
9.6
. By Proposition
8.3
there exists a block
c
of
G^{\circ}(s)
and an

\operatorname{N}_{\tilde{G}\mathcal{B}}(G^{\circ}(s),c)
-equivariant bijection
\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(G,b)\to\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(G^{\circ}(s),c)
, which preserves central characters.

Let
(D,b_{D})
be the
b
-Brauer pair constructed in Lemma
6.1
resp. Lemma
7.2
such that
\operatorname{N}_{G}(D,b_{D})=\operatorname{N}_{G(s)}(D)
. We obtain that
C_{D}=b_{D}
.

By assumption,
S
is a minimal counterexample. Hence, by Theorem
14.1
there exists an
\mathrm{Aut}(G^{\circ}(s))_{c,P}
-equivariant bijection
\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(G^{\circ}(s),c)\to\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(\operatorname{N}_{G^{\circ}(s)}(D),C_{P})
, which preserves central character. In addition, Theorem
14.1
also yields an
\mathrm{Aut}(G^{\circ}(s))_{c,D}
-equivariant bijection
\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(\operatorname{N}_{G^{\circ}(s)}(D),C_{D})\to\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(\operatorname{N}_{G^{\circ}(s)}(D),C_{P})
preserving central characters. Combining these bijections yields an
\mathrm{Aut}(G^{\circ}(s))_{c,D}
-equivariant bijection
\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(G^{\circ}(s),c)\to\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(\operatorname{N}_{G^{\circ}(s)}(D),C_{D})
, which preserves central character.

Now induction of characters yields a natural bijection
\operatorname{Ind}_{\operatorname{N}_{G}(D,b_{D})}^{\operatorname{N}_{G}(D)}:\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(\operatorname{N}_{G}(D,b_{D}),b_{D})\to\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(\operatorname{N}_{G}(D),B_{D})
. We conclude that the composition of these bijections yields an
\operatorname{N}_{\tilde{G}\mathcal{B}}(D,B_{D})
-equivariant bijection
\Psi:\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(G,b)\to\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(\operatorname{N}_{G}(D),B_{D})
, which preserves central characters. Let
\chi\in\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(G,b)
and
\chi^{\prime}=\Psi(\chi)
. If
G
is not of type
E_{7}(q)
, then
\chi
and
\chi^{\prime}
satisfy the conditions of Lemma
9.6
by Lemma
2.5
.
On the other hand, if
G
is of type
E_{7}(q)
then the condition of Lemma
9.6
are satisfied by Lemma
8.7
. Hence, by Lemma
9.6
the block
b
is AM-good.
∎

Lemma 14.5
.

Let
b
be one of the blocks of
G=E_{6}(\pm q)
associated to a semisimple element with centralizer
{}^{3}D_{4}(q)
. Then
b
is AM-good relative to the normalizer of its defect group.

Proof.

By the main result of
[
BDR17
]
there exists a Morita equivalence between
\mathcal{O}G(s)e_{s}^{G^{\circ}(s)}
and
\mathcal{O}Ge_{s}^{G}
which is obtained by lifting the Deligne–Lusztig induction functor
R_{G^{\circ}(s)}^{G}
. In particular, there exists a maximal
b
-Brauer pair
(P,b_{P})
with
P\leq G(s)
. By Lemma
2.4
and Lemma
2.5
, the blocks of
\mathcal{O}G(s)e_{s}^{G^{\circ}(s)}
are Morita equivalent by restriction to the unique block
c
of
\mathcal{O}G^{\circ}(s)e_{s}^{G^{\circ}(s)}
. We deduce that Deligne–Lusztig induction followed by truncation with the idempotent
b
induces a Morita equivalence between
\mathcal{O}G^{\circ}(s)c
and
\mathcal{O}Gb
. This induces a character bijection
bR_{G^{\circ}(s)}^{G}:\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(G^{\circ}(s),c)\to\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(G,b)
which is
\operatorname{N}_{\tilde{G}\mathcal{B}}(\mathbf{G}^{\circ}(s),b)
-equivariant. Hence, as in the proof of
[
Ruh21
, Theorem 10.6]
, there exists a maximal
c
-Brauer pair
(P,c_{P})
together with an
\operatorname{N}_{\tilde{G}\mathcal{B}}(\mathbf{G}^{\circ}(s),P,C_{P})
-equivariant bijection

R_{\operatorname{N}_{G^{\circ}(s)}(P)}^{\operatorname{N}_{G}(P)}:\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(\operatorname{N}_{G^{\circ}(s)}(P),C_{P})\to\operatorname{Irr}_{0}(\operatorname{N}_{G}(P),B_{P}).

We are thus exactly in the situation of Lemma
14.4
and the proof of said lemma shows the statement.
∎

The blocks in Lemma
14.5
could have been treated alternatively with the same methods as the ones developed in Section
9.3
.

We are now ready to prove our main theorem.

Theorem 14.6
.

The Alperin–McKay conjecture holds for the prime
2
.

Proof.

According to Späth’s reduction theorem
[
Spä13
, Theorem C]
, it suffices to show that all finite simple non-abelian groups
S
are AM-good for the prime
2
.

We assume that
S
is a minimal counterexample. In particular, by
[
BR21
, Section 7,Section 8]
we can assume that its universal covering group
G
is a group of Lie type defined over a field of odd characteristic with exceptional root system. Assume first that the block
b
of
G
is not quasi-isolated and let
\mathbf{M}^{\ast}
be the minimal Levi subgroup of
\mathbf{G}^{\ast}
containing
\operatorname{C}_{\mathbf{G}^{\ast}}(s)
. In particular, by assumption on the minimality of
S
, the
2
-blocks of the simple components of
[\mathbf{M},\mathbf{M}]^{F}
are AM-good relative to the normalizer of their defect group, see Corollary
14.3
. By the main theorem of
[
Ruh22
]
, the block
b
is therefore AM-good relative to the normalizer of its defect group. We may therefore assume that
b
is a quasi-isolated block. Suppose first that
b
is a unipotent block. If
b
is the principal block, then the result follows from
[
BR21
, Theorem 8.3]
and if
b
is not the principal block, the result follows from Proposition
13.4
. Therefore, we can assume that
b
is not unipotent. If
\mathrm{Out}(G)_{b}
is cyclic, the result follows from Proposition
14.4
, while if
\mathrm{Out}(G)_{b}
is non-cyclic, the result follows from Corollary
14.3
and Proposition
11.3
.
∎
